остальное здесь проверяется открытием файла; проверить это — нечем, потому что
самого файла ещё нет. Так и написано, вместо того чтобы выдать обещание за
свойство формата.

**Зачем оно тогда сейчас.** Архив Beresta (человекочитаемая раскладка папок,
копия базы и манифест) задуман как бэкап и экспорт одновременно, и поверх него
начнут писать скрипты в первый же месяц. Молчаливая смена разметки — не
гипотеза, а готовый прецедент из мира Calibre: «you seem to have changed the
HTML markup of the annotations saved in the calibre database… I run a script to
extract my Annotations» (форум MobileRead, пост 3971136, 20200401). Объявить
версию задним числом нечем: у архивов, уже разошедшихся по чужим дискам, не
будет признака, по которому их различить. Поэтому обещание версии входит в 1.0
раньше самого архива.

**Манифест** — файл `manifest.json` в корне архива:

```json
{
  "format": "beresta-archive",
  "formatVersion": 1,
  "schemaVersion": "v33_book_file_identity",
  "schemaMigrations": ["v1_books", "v2_files_tags_collections", "…"],
  "application": "Beresta",
  "applicationVersion": "1.0",
  "generatedAt": "2026-08-11T12:00:00Z"
}
```

Текущая версия формата выгрузки — `formatVersion` = 1.

**Что обещано.**

- `format` и `formatVersion` — заголовок манифеста, и он обещан неизменным на
  ВСЕ будущие версии. Без этого читающая сторона не смогла бы даже узнать,
  чего именно она не понимает.
- `formatVersion` — целое, растёт ТОЛЬКО когда читатель предыдущей версии не
  может прочитать архив правильно. Добавление файлов и полей, не ломающее
  прежнего читателя, версию не двигает.
- `schemaVersion` и `schemaMigrations` — это то, что лежит в `grdb_migrations`
  базы, приложенной к архиву, а не то, что умеет собравшая архив сборка.
  **Единственное утверждение этого раздела, проверяемое открытием базы:**
  откройте копию базы из архива и сверьте
  `SELECT identifier FROM grdb_migrations ORDER BY rowid` со списком в
  манифесте — они обязаны совпасть строка в строку.
- `generatedAt` — та же строка ISO 8601, что и во всех колонках-датах базы.

**Чего НЕ обещано.** Раскладки самого архива: имён папок, имён файлов книг,
формата выгрузки аннотаций рядом с базой. Всё это описывается той версией
документа, в которой архив появится; сегодня описывать нечего, и придумывать
раскладку заранее — значит выдать за контракт то, что ещё ни разу не собрано.
Читающая сторона, встретившая `formatVersion` больше знакомого ей, обязана
остановиться и сказать об этом, а не разбирать архив по догадке.

---

## English

### Purpose

The Beresta database is the source of truth for the library, book files,
annotations, reading positions, and reading history. The application (macOS
first, other platforms later) is just one possible reader of this database. The
file is plain SQLite; any program that speaks SQL can open and read it without a
single line of Beresta code.

**File location:** since 20260902 —
`~/Library/Group Containers/group.com.secondbrain.beresta/Beresta/beresta.sqlite`.
The app group container, not the app's own: the widget extension reads the same
database, and the sandbox lets a second process into the shared container only.
Before 20260902 the file lived in `Application Support/Beresta` inside the app
container; it moves out of there once, on the first launch of the new build.
The database runs in WAL mode: `beresta.sqlite-wal` and
`beresta.sqlite-shm` sit next to it; for a consistent snapshot either quit the
app or run `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)` before copying the file.

**What else sits next to it: pre-migration snapshots `beresta-до-vNN.sqlite`.**
Over time the same directory grows files named like `beresta-до-v34.sqlite`
("beresta-before-v34"). This is neither a second library nor an application
backup: it is a snapshot of the database taken right before migration `vNN+1`
touched the schema for the first time. Three rules, all of them relevant to
third-party tools:

1. **The snapshot matches its name.** `beresta-до-v34.sqlite` is the database as
   of the moment when `v34` was the last applied migration — verifiable by the
   same `grdb_migrations` query. The file is plain SQLite and reads like any
   other, but it is no longer in WAL mode: it is produced by `VACUUM INTO`, so
   no `-wal` or `-shm` companions appear next to it.
2. **The application never overwrites or deletes them.** A snapshot for a given
   version either does not exist yet, or exists and is untouchable from then on.
   There is no rotation at all. Snapshots are deleted by a human who decides
   they are no longer needed — by nobody else, and third-party tools should
   follow the same rule.
3. **Do not write to them.** A snapshot is evidence: it is how one returns to
   the pre-migration state. Editing it makes it useless exactly when it is
   needed.

You may also come across `beresta-до-vNN.sqlite.частичная` ("partial") — a copy
interrupted mid-write (out of disk space, power loss). It is not a snapshot: the
application writes the copy under that name and renames it to the real one in a
single move, removing any leftover on the next launch. Do not read it.

**What else sits next to it: the `доводка-привязок/` directory**
("anchor-refresh"). It holds files named like `20260816.jsonl` — the log of what
the anchor refresher rewrote that day. Refreshing is a repair of a derived
value: the application spreads a Calibre offset across the text chunks of an
element, checking against the book file itself, and rewrites
`annotation.position` and `annotation.sortIndex`. No human edit is involved, so
these writes never reach `changeLog`, and the log is the only place where the
PREVIOUS anchor string survives.

Each line is one JSON object: `annotationId`, `previousPosition`, `position`,
`previousSortIndex`, `sortIndex`, `ruleVersion` (the version of the placement
rule that produced the new string), `fileSHA256`, `movedToOtherSection`, `at`.
There are exactly as many lines as anchors rewritten; anchors the refresher left
alone produce no line.

The rules match those of the snapshots: files are only appended to, the
application never truncates or deletes them, and you should not write to them.
They are not part of the data — the database remains the single source of truth,
and the log answers one question: "what was this anchor before it was touched,
and which rule produced the current one".

**Documentation license:** this document, and the schema format in general,
is CC0 1.0 Universal (`docs/schema/LICENSE`) — unlike the application code,
which is proprietary. The ecosystem is free to read, write, and reimplement
this format in other programs, with no permission and no attribution
required.

**Compatibility rule.** The format is frozen at version 1.0: columns of
existing tables are never renamed or dropped — only added by a new migration
(with a default value, so existing rows stay valid). Code written against an
older version keeps reading a newer database correctly: it simply won't notice
the new columns. The full account — what is promised, what we reserve, and what
to do with the unfamiliar — is in the Compatibility section below.

**How to read the schema version.** The version is the SET of applied
migrations, and it lives in the `grdb_migrations` table (a single `identifier`
column, one row per migration). Third-party code may and should read it —
otherwise the "pin the exact schema version" requirement in the status note
above would be impossible to satisfy:

```sql
SELECT identifier FROM grdb_migrations ORDER BY rowid;
```

The last row is the current version. **The schema version is not the format
version**, and confusing the two is expensive: the schema grows with every piece
of work (v33, v34, v35 — a counter of migrations), while the format version
changes rarely and answers a different question — "which promise about the shape
of the data does this file keep". A migration name cannot decide whether a file
is readable: behind an unfamiliar one there may be an added column (readable) or
a changed shape (not). That decision has its own number, and it lives in the
file header — see Compatibility below.

About the migration list itself, exactly one thing is guaranteed:
migration names and their order are part of the format and are never renamed
after the fact. Everything else about this table (its own structure, any
housekeeping columns it may grow) is a library detail and must not be relied
upon. Third-party code must never WRITE to it under any circumstances: a row
here means "this migration has already run", and a hand-written row will
silently cancel a real migration.

How many migrations there are right now is one query against `grdb_migrations`
(see above); the number is deliberately not repeated here — the list below is
checked by a test against the live database, while a number written next to it
would quietly go stale.

The schema consists of the following migrations, in the order they are applied:
`v1_books`, `v2_files_tags_collections`, `v3_annotations`, `v4_changelog`,
`v5_search`, `v6_sync_identity`, `v7_change_origin`,
`v8_deterministic_import_identifiers`, `v9_annotation_color`, `v10_settings`,
`v11_collection_order`, `v12_annotation_source_key`, `v13_annotation_cfi`,
`v14_annotation_anchor_refresh`, `v15_reading_history`,
`v16_reading_position_file`, `v17_sort_title_key`,
`v18_annotation_outlives_book`, `v19_value_dictionaries`,
`v20_annotation_color_pairing`, `v21_source_identity`, `v22_edition_merge`,
`v23_author_merge`, `v24_reading_states`, `v25_reader_decision`,
`v26_reading_fields`, `v27_reading_intent`, `v28_annotation_fields`,
`v29_annotation_search`, `v30_golden_shelf`, `v31_bookmark`,
`v32_format_debts`, `v33_book_file_identity`, `v34_format_version`,
`v35_stored_quote_rule`, `v36_article_kind`, `v37_article_shelf`,
`v38_manual_fields`, `v39_reading_journal`, `v40_manual_tags`,
`v41_inbox_status`, `v42_book_relations`, `v43_feeds`, `v44_supplement_kind`,
`v45_collection_tree`, `v46_annotation_review`,
`v47_session_file_and_collection_kind_name`.
Numbering may contain
gaps: a migration name is an identifier, not a counter, and a number claimed
by parallel work is never handed out twice.

Besides the tables described below, the file holds housekeeping tables that
this document does not describe and whose contents are of no use to
third-party code: `sqlite_sequence` (`AUTOINCREMENT` counters, created by
SQLite itself) and the eight FTS5 shadow tables — `bookSearch_data`,
`bookSearch_idx`, `bookSearch_docsize`, `bookSearch_config` and the same four
for the second index, `annotationSearch_*`.

### Compatibility: what version 1.0 promises

The data format was frozen at version **1.0** by the owner's decision on
20260812. Until that day this document was a working note and could change
freely; from that day it is a commitment, and changing the shape of the data
stops being an edit — it becomes compatibility.

**The rule of this section: not a single claim that cannot be checked by
opening the database.** Where there is nothing to back a guarantee with, it says
"not promised" rather than something vague.

**How the promises are checked.** The repository carries a snapshot of the
frozen shape — `docs/schema/frozen-1.0.txt`: one line per claim (table, column
with its ordinal, type, nullability, default, key, index, trigger, foreign key).
The `FormatFreezeTests` suite unfolds the live schema into the same lines and
compares both ways: a line gone from the live schema is a broken freeze, an
extra live line is an addition the snapshot does not know about yet. Both are
red, and the failure message says which. The snapshot is taken from the same
database this document describes, so third-party developers are welcome to check
their own reading against it too.

#### How to read the format version of a file on disk

From two numbers in the SQLite file header. Both live in the first hundred
bytes, both were introduced by the engine for exactly this question, and both
are read without touching a single table:

```sql
PRAGMA application_id;   -- 1112691540 → this is a Beresta file
PRAGMA user_version;     -- 1012       → format version 1.12
```

- `application_id` = 1112691540 — ASCII "BRST", the "this is Beresta" mark. Any
  other non-zero value means a file belonging to another program, and matching
  table names in it mean nothing.
- `user_version` = 1012 — the format version as an integer, by the rule
  `major × 1000 + minor`: 1.0 → 1000, 1.1 → 1001, 2.0 → 2000. The minor part
  grew seven times: `v36_article_kind` gave books a record kind and a source
  address, and the format dictionary gained `html`; `v37_article_shelf` added
  `articles` to the collection-kind dictionary; `v38_manual_fields` gave books
  the list of fields the human edited by hand; `v39_reading_journal` added the
  book's size and the journal of reading stretches; `v40_manual_tags` added the
  mark that a hand attached the tag; `v41_inbox_status` added `inbox` to the
  reading-status dictionary; `v42_book_relations` added record links (`bookRelation`) — Zotero's Related;
  `v43_feeds` added RSS subscriptions (`feed`) — the article feed refills
  itself; `v44_supplement_kind` added the `supplement` kind — an
  attachment of another record, not a top-level citizen;
  `v45_collection_tree` added the collection tree
  (`collection.parentUUID`); `v46_annotation_review` added the excerpt
  review state: the next due date, FSRS-6 stability and difficulty, the
  answer count and the moment of the last answer (format 1.11);
  `v47_session_file_and_collection_kind_name` added the file of a reading
  stretch (`readingSession.fileSHA256`) and relaxed collection name
  uniqueness to the (kind, name) pair (format 1.12). A reader that only
  knows 1.0 still reads such a file correctly.

**Why not the migration list.** It answers a different question — "how much work
has been applied to this file" — and cannot decide the important one: whether
the file is readable. Behind an unfamiliar migration name there may be an added
column or a changed shape. Besides, `grdb_migrations` is a table of a
third-party library (GRDB): its name and structure are not ours to promise for
years ahead. We promise only what we own — the header of our own file.

**What zero means.** An `application_id` of zero means "no mark". That is how a
Beresta file created before 1.0 and never opened by a 1.0 build looks, and any
SQLite database whose author never filled the header fields. **The header cannot
tell them apart** — from there on it is the presence of the `book` and
`annotation` tables that decides. Zero here is not "version 0".

**Who writes the number.** The `v34_format_version`, `v36_article_kind`,
`v37_article_shelf`, `v38_manual_fields`, `v39_reading_journal`,
`v40_manual_tags`, and `v41_inbox_status` migrations, as literals. That
is why the mark means the shape of the data rather than the build that happened
to open the file: the format version is raised by a new migration — exactly the
way a closed value dictionary is extended by a new migration.

#### What will not be broken within 1.x

1. **Tables are never dropped or renamed.** All of those described under
   "Tables" below (21 of them) stay, under the same names.
2. **Columns are never dropped, renamed, or reordered.** The order is part of
   the promise, not typography: `SELECT *` returns values by column position,
   and a shift by one turns a file digest into a reading position. A new column
   goes to the end of the table; the ordinals of existing ones do not move.
   Every ordinal is recorded in the snapshot
   (`table book column 12 = readStatus …`).
3. **The type, nullability, and default of an existing column do not change.**
   In particular, a column that accepts `NULL` will not become `NOT NULL`: that
   would reject writes which were legal in 1.0.
4. **Constraints are never tightened.** Primary keys and unique constraints are
   not extended to other columns; a column that takes a free value today will
   not grow a closed dictionary. Extending a dictionary is allowed (see below);
   narrowing one is not.
5. **The meaning of a value is never redefined.** A value declared today will
   mean the same thing tomorrow: `read` stays "finished", `highlight` stays a
   highlight, `origin = 'import'` stays an importer's edit. A new meaning gets a
   new name instead of taking an old one.
6. **Identifier formulas do not change.** The deterministic `uuid`s of a book,
   an annotation, the reference tables and a book file row, the ordering key,
   the title sort key, the length-prefixed join, the clock stamp — all are
   computed by the same formulas, and the published worked examples stay valid.
   This matters more than the rest: a third-party program that computes a row's
   address from this document must arrive at the same address we do — otherwise
   the first sync gets two facts instead of one.
7. **Dates stay ISO 8601 in UTC** — one set of rules for every date column.
8. **Soft deletion stays soft.** A row with a non-empty `deletedAt` does not
   physically disappear, and that will not come to mean something else.
9. **Derived columns stay derived** (`searchText`, `colorHex`, sort keys): they
   can be recomputed from the same inputs by the same formula, and the
   full-text indexes can be rebuilt from scratch.

#### What we reserve

Everything listed here is an addition; it raises the minor part of the format
version (1.0 → 1.1) and leaves the major part alone. A reader written for 1.0
keeps reading such a file correctly.

1. **Adding new tables.**
2. **Adding columns** — at the end of a table, and only ones that keep an
   existing row valid: either nullable or with a default.
3. **Extending closed value dictionaries** by a new migration that rewrites a
   pair of triggers. Third-party readers must be ready for this — the rule for
   an unfamiliar value below is written for exactly this case.
4. **Adding indexes and triggers** that maintain derived values. An index is
   speed, not shape, but the snapshot records it too: a unique index appearing
   quietly would be a tightening, and tightening is forbidden by point 4 above.
5. **Adding new setting keys** (`setting.key`) and new kinds of entities that
   arrive as values rather than columns.
6. **Rebuilding a table as a migration technique** — provided the snapshot
   matches line for line afterwards. The technique is legitimate (v6, v16 and
   v18 used it), but it carries the table's triggers away with the old table,
   which is precisely why the snapshot lists them.

#### What we do not promise

1. **The layout of the export archive** — the names of folders and files inside
   it. Only the manifest header is promised; see "Export format version" below.
2. **The structure of the `grdb_migrations` table.** Migration names and their
   order are part of the format; the table itself belongs to GRDB. Reading it is
   fine, relying on its structure is not, and **writing to it is never
   acceptable**.
3. **The FTS5 shadow tables** (`bookSearch_*`, `annotationSearch_*`) — SQLite
   internals. Their contents change with any rewrite of the index.
4. **The values of `id` and `rowid`.** They are local: two copies of the same
   library carry different numbers. The portable address of a row is its
   `uuid`, and every entity that travels to another device has one.
5. **That a third-party WRITE will reach synchronization.** Anyone may read this
   format with anything. Writing is allowed too, but edits that never made it
   into `changeLog` by the documented rules do not exist as far as sync is
   concerned: it replays the journal, it does not diff tables. That is a
   limitation of the mechanism, not our reluctance.
6. **That derived columns will be recomputed on a third-party writer's
   behalf.** Whoever writes `book.title` outside our code must update
   `searchText` and the sort keys themselves, or search will fall behind the
   data. The discrepancy can be detected with a query; it cannot be repaired
   automatically.
7. **Anything about the `-wal` and `-shm` files.** They are connection state,
   not data; for a consistent snapshot, close the database or run
   `PRAGMA wal_checkpoint(TRUNCATE)`.

#### What to do when you meet something unfamiliar

The rule in one line: **the unfamiliar is neither deleted nor overwritten.**

- **An unfamiliar value in a dictionary column** (say an `annotation.type` your
  enumeration does not have). Do not substitute something "similar" and do not
  overwrite it with a default: the value was written by another version of
  Beresta or another program, and substituting it silently corrupts someone
  else's data. There are two sensible options: show it as it is, or skip the row
  and say so out loud.
- **An unfamiliar column.** Ignore it when reading and **preserve it when
  writing.** The danger is not `SELECT`, it is `INSERT OR REPLACE` of a whole
  row and `UPDATE` with every column of your own model: the row gets rewritten
  without whatever your code does not know about. Write by naming columns
  explicitly.
- **An unfamiliar table.** Ignore it; do not drop it and do not clean it up.
- **A `user_version` whose major part is above the one you know** (2000 while
  you know 1.x). Stop and say so rather than guessing your way through: the
  major part is raised precisely when an older reader would read the file
  wrongly. A higher minor part (1001 while you know 1.0) is readable: those are
  additions you will simply not notice.
- **An `application_id` other than 1112691540.** Zero means "no mark", see
  above. Any other value means another program's file; matching table names may
  be a coincidence.

Our own code behaves the same way and fails loudly: an unfamiliar value in a
dictionary column is a decoding error, not a quiet substitution. The database
refuses foreign values from its own side, by trigger
(`collection_kind_dictionary_on_insert` and a matching pair for every
dictionary), so this document's promise is checked by attempting a write rather
than by trust.

#### What is frozen with reservations

The pre-freeze acceptance
(`docs/plans/notes/20260812-готовность-к-заморозке.md`, verdict "ready with
reservations") named what enters 1.0 **without having been exercised by any
mechanism**. These are not defects: it is the price of a deliberate order —
shape first, screens later — because a column, unlike a screen, cannot be added
after the fact in a way that gives old records meaning. But a third-party
developer is entitled to know where the shape has been proven by use and where
only by tests.

1. ~~**A dictionary with a single value.**~~ **Closed on 2026-08-27.** The
   promise that "a second kind of shelf will arrive as a value rather than as a
   data migration" has been kept: `v37_article_shelf` added `articles` as a
   single value, with no data migration at all. The dictionary can now be
   checked: probes of the two kinds differ.
2. **Two mechanisms for an annotation's topic live side by side, and both are
   empty.** `annotation.topic` is a free string, "what this excerpt is about";
   `annotationTag` links an excerpt to a label from the `tag` reference table.
   Both are frozen, and **which of the two is the primary one was not decided on
   the day of the freeze**: the format's owner has zero topics and zero labels
   across 287 excerpts, so there is nothing to decide it on yet. It is said
   plainly because silence would cost more than the uncertainty: until the
   decision is made, a third-party reader must read both rather than choose on
   our behalf. Whichever wins will leave the other a dead column forever —
   dropping it after 1.0 is not allowed.
3. **Nine tables out of twenty-one are empty** in the format owner's library on
   freeze day: `bookmark`, `readerDecision`, `annotationLayer`, `annotationTag`,
   `changeLog`, `changeLogClock`, `device`, `readingPosition`, `setting`. Some
   fill up on first use; `changeLog` waits for synchronization. An empty table
   is checked by probes, not by live data.
4. **Forty-five of the forty-six columns added by the last plan are read by no
   screen.** The only one with a consumer is `annotation.chapterPath`. The shape
   of these columns is proven by tests and not by use.
5. **The export format version is declared with zero archives in existence.**
   Nothing writes the export file yet; the version is promised, the layout is
   not. See "Export format version".
6. **The ink format is described with zero ink strokes.** `ink` was a value of
   the `annotation.type` dictionary long before it was described — that is, the
   format promised what it did not describe. Describing it turned out cheaper
   than removing the value; but if drawing ever demands a different structure, a
   second kind of position will have to be declared alongside the first.

Separately, on **bookmarks and annotations**, the two kinds of place in a book:
an annotation has 29 columns against a bookmark's 10, and six of them are a
snapshot of the book, which is what lets an annotation outlive the book's
deletion. A bookmark, by contrast, dies with its book by cascade: a place there
is no returning to addresses nothing. Merging them would hand a bookmark six
columns that harm it; hence two kinds of place, by decision rather than by
oversight.

### Tables

Tables below are listed in the order their migration created them. Column
types are given exactly as `sqlite3 <file> ".schema"` reports them (SQLite
does not distinguish `INTEGER`/`BIGINT`; every integer column is `INTEGER`).

#### `book`

One row per library book. The central entity: files, annotations, and
reading positions all reference it by `id`.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier; foreign keys reference it. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the book (independent of the local database), used by sync and export. For a book imported from Calibre, deterministic (not random) on first insert — see "`book.uuid` and `annotation.uuid` for imported records". |
| `title` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Book title as-is. |
| `sortTitle` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The sort **key** for the title, kept separate from `title` because display and sort order need different strings: case and «ё» folded, leading quotes dropped, numbers zero-padded. Plain byte comparison of this column yields the correct order without knowing any Beresta rules — see "`book.sortTitle`". Recomputed by migration v17. |
| `subtitle` | TEXT | NULL | Subtitle. |
| `seriesName` | TEXT | NULL | Series name, if the book belongs to a series. |
| `seriesIndex` | DOUBLE | NULL | Book number within the series (fractional, to allow books like "3.5"). |
| `publisher` | TEXT | NULL | Publisher. |
| `publishedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Publication date, ISO 8601 (see "Value formats"). |
| `language` | TEXT | NULL | Book language code (e.g. `ru`, `en`). |
| `bookDescription` | TEXT | NULL | Book blurb/description. Named `bookDescription`, not `description`, to avoid clashing with `Codable`/GRDB machinery. |
| `rating` | INTEGER | NULL | User rating. |
| `readStatus` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'unread'` | Reading status, see "Value formats". **Derived value**: computed from the reading history (`reading`); it has no write path of its own — see "`book.readStatus`". |
| `finishedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Date the book was finished, ISO 8601. **Derived value**: the latest known finish date among the book's readings, see "`book.readStatus`". |
| `addedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the book was added to the library, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the row was last modified, ISO 8601. |
| `coverSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | Hash of the cover file in the blob store (outside the database, outside subproject 1a). |
| `sourceLibraryUUID` | TEXT | NULL | UUID of the source library (Calibre), if the book was imported. |
| `sourceBookUUID` | TEXT | NULL | UUID of the book in the source library. |
| `sourceModifiedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Modification date of the record in the source, ISO 8601 — needed to resolve conflicts on re-import. |
| `searchText` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Normalized text for full-text search (title + authors + series). See "Relationship with full-text search". Added by migration v5. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL if not deleted. Column added by migration v6, wired up in task 18: `BookRepository.deleteBook(uuid:)` sets it, and every read of books (list, count, search, books of a collection) filters marked rows out. See "What soft deletion means". |
| `mergedIntoUUID` | TEXT | NULL | Merge tombstone: the `book.uuid` of the book this one was merged into, or NULL if it was never merged. A non-empty value means "this record is not in the library; all of its content now lives there". Added by migration v22. Always set together with `deletedAt`, in a single statement. See "The merge tombstone". |
| `provenance` | TEXT | NULL | Provenance of the COPY, free text: "signed by the author", "a gift for my thesis defence", "found at my father's". NULL means "not stated"; never written as an empty string. Added by migration v30. It belongs to the book, not to the shelf link: an autograph holds regardless of which shelf the book sits on, or whether it sits on one at all — see "The golden shelf: reason, provenance, and the seat limit". |
| `kind` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'book'` | What this record is: `book` for an edition, `article` for an article made from a web page snapshot, `supplement` for an attachment of another record (added by migration v44, format 1.9: a full record linked to its parent via `bookRelation`, excluded from the All-books and Articles sections). A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". Added by migration v36. The default is not a formality: before v36 no article had ever been created, because there was nothing to create one with. Why a kind rather than a second table — see "An article as a library record". |
| `sourceURL` | TEXT | NULL | The address of the article's original. NULL means there is no address: a book imported from Calibre has none and cannot have one. Never written as an empty string. Added by migration v36. It lives in a column, not only inside the file (`dc:source` of the assembled EPUB): a field inside an archive cannot be searched, shown in a list, or opened without unpacking the whole file first. |
| `manualFields` | TEXT | NULL | The fields the human edited BY HAND: names of this table's columns, comma-separated, in byte order, without spaces — `publishedAt,title`. An import never touches such fields: before v38 a repeated Calibre import overwrote them with the source's values, and the human's edit would have vanished silently. NULL and the empty string mean the same — "edited nothing". The mark is removed by the same human ("restore from the source"). Added by migration v38. Besides column names the vocabulary knows one special name — `authors` (since 20260829): it shields the book's author LIST as a whole (the `bookAuthor` links) — seeing it, an import leaves the list alone. The shape did not change: an older reader does not know this name and simply does not shield. |
| `charCount` | INTEGER | NULL | The book's size in characters. NULL means it has not been counted yet. **Zero means counted and none found:** the book has no file (7 of the owner's), the file is unreadable, or it is a scan with no text layer (17 of the owner's 391 PDFs). Counted without the engine: an EPUB is an archive, an FB2 is XML, a PDF is walked page by page through PDFKit (measured 2026-08-28: EPUB and FB2 — 92 seconds for 1552 files, 59 ms per file; PDF — 196 seconds for 391 files, 502 ms per file). When a PDF sits next to an EPUB, the number comes from the EPUB: it is both more accurate (a PDF count includes every page's running heads) and eight times cheaper. Added by migration v39. |
| `wordCount` | INTEGER | NULL | The same size in words. BOTH numbers are stored: words are what a person measures a book by, characters are what reading time is computed from in Russian (Russian words are longer, and a multiplier taken from English sources would lie). Added by migration v39. |

Index: `book_on_source` — a unique index on (`sourceLibraryUUID`,
`sourceBookUUID`), restricted to rows with a non-null `sourceBookUUID`
(partial index, condition `sourceBookUUID IS NOT NULL`) — this prevents
importing the same source book twice while still allowing books without a
source (`sourceBookUUID IS NULL`).

The index condition says "not null" while the promise is "non-empty"; the two
coincide because the empty string is forbidden separately, on write (triggers
`book_source_identity_on_insert`/`_on_update`, migration v21). The source pair
obeys two rules, and the database enforces both itself:

- `sourceBookUUID` is either `NULL` ("this book was not imported") or a
  non-empty string. The empty string is rejected: it would mean "there is a
  source but no address inside it", and two such books would additionally
  collide with each other in the unique index for no reason;
- if `sourceBookUUID` is set, `sourceLibraryUUID` must be set too. Without
  this rule there would be no uniqueness at all: in a unique index SQLite
  treats every `NULL` as DISTINCT, so rows with an unknown library would
  never collide with anything and the same source book could land twice.

The same two rules, enforced by the same kind of triggers, apply to
`annotation` (`sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceAnnotationUUID`) and `reading`
(`sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceBookUUID`) — see `annotation_on_source` and
`reading_on_source`.

No foreign keys: `book` is the root of the reference graph.

#### `author`

One row per author, reused by all their books through `bookAuthor`.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `name` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Author name as-is ("Mikhail Bulgakov"). Uniqueness is the reuse mechanism: inserting the same name again returns the existing row. |
| `sortName` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Name used for sorting ("Bulgakov, Mikhail"). |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the author, used by sync — same role as `book`/`annotation`'s `uuid`. Added by migration v6. `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` cannot give a non-empty table a `NOT NULL` column without one default shared by every row, and a shared default would make every row's uuid identical, so v6 rebuilds the table instead (new table → transfer rows, generating a unique uuid for each → drop the old table → rename the new one). New rows get a deterministic (not random) one as of migration v8 — see "`book.uuid` and `annotation.uuid` for imported records". |
| `mergedIntoUUID` | TEXT | NULL | Merge tombstone: the `author.uuid` of the name spelling this one was merged into, or NULL if it was never merged. Added by migration v23. Always set together with `deletedAt`, in a single statement. The name STAYS on the tombstone — there is nothing to erase it with (`name` is `NOT NULL UNIQUE`), and that is exactly what makes the tombstone the record of the alias. See "The merge tombstone". |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft deletion, ISO 8601. Added by migration v23 — before it there was no way to delete an author at all. Beresta has no author-deletion path of its own: merging name spellings is the only thing that sets this column. |

No foreign keys. There is no `modifiedAt` on an author and none was added: a
reference table keeps no timestamp of its own, and merging does not invent one.

#### `bookAuthor`

Many-to-many link between books and authors, carrying author order.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `bookId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book. |
| `authorId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `author.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The author. |
| `position` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, default `0` | Author's order for the book (0 is the first/primary author). |

Primary key is the pair (`bookId`, `authorId`). Deleting a book or an author
cascades the link row; deleting a book does not touch the author itself (it
may have other books).

#### `bookFile`

A book file — one format of one book. The file's content does not live in
the database; it lives in a content-addressed blob store (outside subproject
1a) — here there is only a reference by hash.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book this file belongs to. |
| `sha256` | TEXT | NOT NULL | SHA-256 of the file content — also its name in the blob store (deduplication: identical content is stored once). |
| `format` | TEXT | NOT NULL | File format: one of `epub`, `pdf`, `fb2`, `mobi`, `azw3`, `cbz`, `djvu`, `txt`, `html`, `unknown`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". |
| `sizeBytes` | INTEGER | NOT NULL | File size in bytes. |
| `originalName` | TEXT | NULL | Original file name (for UI and diagnostics; not a filesystem path). |
| `addedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the file was added, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL if not deleted. Added by migration v6 (a plain `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` — the column allows NULL, so no default value is needed); wired up in task 18: `BookRepository.deleteBook(uuid:)` flags the files of a deleted book, `files(of:)` hides flagged rows, and `addFile` REVIVES a flagged row instead of the silent `INSERT OR IGNORE` that would otherwise hit the uniqueness constraint. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NULL, UNIQUE (index `bookFile_on_uuid`) | Portable address of the row: identical on every device that independently registered the same file of the same book. Derived DETERMINISTICALLY from `book.uuid`, `format`, and `sha256` — the full formula is below. Migration **v33**; the same migration addressed every row that already existed. NULL is legitimate and means exactly what was true of every row before v33: there is nothing to address the row with (see "An empty address" below). |

Uniqueness: (`bookId`, `format`, `sha256`) — adding the same file of the same
format to the same book again does not create a duplicate (the insert is
silently ignored at the repository level, `INSERT OR IGNORE`). Index
`bookFile_on_sha256` allows fast lookup by hash regardless of book. Deleting
a book cascades its `bookFile` rows (the on-disk blob itself is not removed
by this — that is the job of a separate garbage collector outside subproject
1a).

##### `uuid` — the row's address and how to compute it

**What it is derived from.** From the triple "book address + format + content
fingerprint" — that is, from the row's NATURAL KEY expressed in portable
terms: the table's uniqueness is declared on (`bookId`, `format`, `sha256`),
and `bookId` is a local autoincrement, which on another device points at a
different book. Its place in the formula is taken by `book.uuid`, which is
itself deterministic for everything imported (see "`book.uuid` and
`annotation.uuid` for imported records").

```
uuid = DeterministicUUID(
           kind     = "bookFile",
           instance = book.uuid,
           key      = LengthPrefixedJoin([format, sha256]))
```

`DeterministicUUID` and `LengthPrefixedJoin` are the same as for `book.uuid`;
the full formula, with the namespace and byte order, is in "The formula in
full" below. `format` and `sha256` are joined with a length prefix INSIDE
`key` (`4:epub64:e3b0…`) rather than by a separator, so the join stays
injective whatever the parts contain.

**Worked example** (an implementation that produces this value from this input
is compatible with ours):

| `book.uuid` | `format` | `sha256` | `key` | `uuid` |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111` | `epub` | `e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855` | `4:epub64:e3b0c44298fc1c149afbf4c8996fb92427ae41e4649b934ca495991b7852b855` | `8CB17463-2C17-576A-A851-E9D8E1FC0683` |

The example is pinned by `BookFileIdentityTests.publishedWorkedExample`, and
that test checks the value against THIS text as well as against the code — the
two cannot drift apart silently. The value itself was computed by an
independent implementation (`uuid.uuid5` in Python).

**Why not from `sha256` alone.** The same content legitimately hangs off
different books — that is what two not-yet-merged editions look like; in the
format author's own library that is 8 rows out of 1949 (measured 20260812). An
address derived from the fingerprint alone would collapse them into one row.

**Why `sha256` is included even though the format is.** The schema
deliberately allows a book to have two files of the same format (uniqueness
includes the fingerprint): a different conversion, a proofread version.
Without `sha256` the second such row would have no address.

**A re-saved file is a different row, not the same file with a new address.**
The fingerprint is part of the table's unique key, so new content creates a
new row; Beresta does not and will not change `sha256` on an existing row.

**The address is issued once and never recomputed — not even by an edition
merge.** A merge moves the file onto the surviving book (`bookId` changes)
while `uuid` stays as it was. Hence an important consequence for a
third-party reader: **do not verify the address by recomputing it** — for a
moved row it will not match today's book, and that is not corruption.
Recomputing on the move would be worse: a device that has already merged and a
device that has not would call one physical file by two different addresses —
exactly the duplicate the address exists to prevent. Same order as
`annotation.uuid`, which also survives moving between books.

**An empty address.** `NULL` is legitimate and means "nothing to address the
row with" — the state every row was in before v33. The migration leaves `NULL`
wherever the address cannot be derived honestly: the row points at a
non-existent book, the book has an empty `uuid`, or two rows are
indistinguishable by the natural key (a third-party writer need not have the
v2 uniqueness). A migration must OPEN any file; repairing someone else's data
is not its job. A third-party writer may leave `uuid` empty: the column is not
required.

#### `collection`

A user-defined "shelf" — a way to group books manually, unlike tags, which
tend to be topical.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `name` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Collection name. |
| `sortOrder` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, default `0` | Display order of collections relative to each other. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the collection, used by sync. Added by migration v6 by rebuilding the table — same reason as `author.uuid` above. New rows get a deterministic (not random) one as of migration v8 — see "`book.uuid` and `annotation.uuid` for imported records". |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL if not deleted. Added by the same v6 rebuild; the repository does not yet set or filter on it. |
| `defaultSort` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'title'` | Default display order for the collection's books: `manual` or `title` (task 13, see "`bookCollection.sortKey` and `collection.defaultSort`"). `manual` is what turns a collection into a reading list — the flag lives in a setting, not in the structure. Added by migration v11. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Creation date of the collection, ISO 8601. Added by migration v11; rows that predate it get the migration's moment. The `''` default is a technical placeholder left by `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` (the same trap as `bookCollection.sortKey`/`addedAt` below): an empty string is not a valid ISO 8601 date and means "not assigned yet". |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Last-modification date of the collection, ISO 8601. Added by migration v11; rows that predate it get the migration's moment. The `''` default is the same technical placeholder as in `createdAt` above. |
| `kind` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'books'` | What the collection is made of: `books` or `articles` (the value was added by migration v37 — articles have their own seats on the golden shelf). Added by migration v30. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". Why a column with a single value — see "The golden shelf: reason, provenance, and the seat limit". |
| `parentUUID` | TEXT | NULL | Parent collection of the tree — a `collection.uuid`, or NULL for a root. Added by migration v45 (format 1.10). A cycle written by a third-party writer is not rejected by the database (an incoming change must apply); reading untangles it: the cycled node is shown at the root and the trouble is named. |

#### `bookCollection`

Many-to-many link between books and collections, carrying the book's manual
order within the collection (task 13).

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `bookId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book. |
| `collectionId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `collection.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The collection. |
| `sortKey` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Fractional index for the book's order within the collection, see "`bookCollection.sortKey` and `collection.defaultSort`". Added by migration v11; links that predate it are backfilled in `book.sortTitle` order (the display order at the time). The `''` default is a technical placeholder, not a valid key — see "The `sortKey`/`addedAt` placeholder trap". |
| `addedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Date the book was added to the collection, ISO 8601. Added by migration v11; links that predate it get the migration's moment (an honest caveat — not the date the book landed on the shelf in the source, which the source doesn't keep). The `''` default is a technical placeholder, not a valid date — see "The `sortKey`/`addedAt` placeholder trap". |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion tombstone, ISO 8601, or NULL if not deleted. Added by migration v11; the order-reading queries (`BookRepository.books(inCollection:)`, `moveBook`, `collections(of:)`) filter on it. As of task 18 its only writer is `deleteBook` (`removeCollection` still deletes the link physically), while `addCollection`/`setCollections` REVIVE a flagged link with a fresh order key — otherwise adding a book back to a shelf would be silent inaction (finding B13, see "What soft deletion means"). |
| `reason` | TEXT | NULL | Why the book sits on THIS shelf: `reread`, `gratitude`, or `milestone`. NULL means "no reason stated" — a legitimate state of the row, not an unfilled field. Added by migration v30. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database; the dictionary does not fire on NULL — see "What the database itself enforces" and "The golden shelf: reason, provenance, and the seat limit". |
| `reasonNote` | TEXT | NULL | The same reason in the owner's own words ("my teacher's book"). NULL means "no words"; never written as an empty string. Added by migration v30. The "dictionary value + free text" pair is shaped exactly like `readerDecision.reason`/`note`. |

Primary key is the pair (`bookId`, `collectionId`). Deleting a book or a
collection cascades the link row. Index `bookCollection_on_order` on
(`collectionId`, `sortKey`) — fast retrieval of a collection's books in
manual order with a single `ORDER BY`, without sorting in memory.

#### `tag`

A free-form tag, applicable both to books (`bookTag`) and to annotations
(`annotationTag`).

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `name` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Tag name. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the tag, used by sync. Added by migration v6 by rebuilding the table — same reason as `author.uuid` above. New rows get a deterministic (not random) one as of migration v8 — see "`book.uuid` and `annotation.uuid` for imported records". |

#### `feed`

An RSS/Atom subscription (migration **v43**, format 1.8): a source the app
watches on its own, turning new entries into articles. User data — the row
travels through the journal to all devices. The polling schedule is
deliberately absent: "when we checked" and "which entries we saw" are local
to each device and live in its settings.

| Column | Type | Constraints | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier, used by sync. |
| `url` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The feed address as the user provided it. |
| `title` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Subscription name — taken from the feed itself on adding; the user may rewrite it. |
| `addedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the subscription was added, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last row edit, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-delete tombstone; `NULL` — the subscription is alive. Articles it brought survive its removal. |

#### `bookRelation`

A link between records — Zotero's Related (migration **v42**, format 1.7).
Undirected: a pair of books, order means nothing. The storage canon keeps
the end with the SMALLER `uuid` in `bookAId`; the row's journal address is
`LinkRowUUID` over the sorted pair of end `uuid`s. The first self-link table:
both columns reference `book`, so end resolution during sync takes column
names from the dataset declaration, not from the foreign-key dictionary.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Local row number. |
| `bookAId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE, part of UNIQUE | The end with the smaller `uuid`. |
| `bookBId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE, part of UNIQUE | The end with the larger `uuid`. |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the link appeared, in the schema's time format. |

The `bookRelation_on_bookBId` index over `bookBId` serves link lookups
without a table scan (the first column is covered by the pair's UNIQUE).

Edition merges re-hang links onto the survivor; the loser—survivor link
would become a self-link and is dropped, duplicates collapse.

#### `bookTag`

Many-to-many link between books and tags.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `bookId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book. |
| `tagId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `tag.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The tag. |
| `manualAt` | TEXT | NULL | When a human hand attached the tag, in the schema's time format. NULL means the link came from a source (the Calibre import). Added by migration **v40**. |

Primary key is the pair (`bookId`, `tagId`).

**About `manualAt` and importing.** The Calibre import sends a book's whole
tag set on every run and removes anything the set does not contain. A link
with a non-empty `manualAt` is the exception: the import never removes it.
Without that rule a tag attached by hand in Beresta would live until the next
import and then vanish silently — exactly how books used to fall off the
Golden shelf before 2026-08-28.

**What the rule cannot do, said out loud.** It does not protect a tag REMOVED
by hand: once the link row is gone there is nowhere for the mark to live, and
the source will bring the tag back on its next run. Only a separate table of
removals could close that, with its own identity and its own cascades; as of
2026-08-28 the schema has no such table.

**The set is edited by difference, not replaced.** An earlier revision of this
section claimed the opposite — that the repository replaces the set wholesale
and that no API-level single-tag add or remove exists. That stopped being true
before anyone noticed: `setTags` computes a difference and leaves untouched
links alone, and `addTag`/`removeTag` operate on a single link. Corrected
2026-08-28.

#### `annotation`

An annotation: a highlight, a note, an underline. This table is the source
of truth for annotation content; nothing is written back into the book file
(the book file stays byte-for-byte unchanged).

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the annotation. For an annotation imported from Calibre, deterministic (neither the raw source identifier nor random) — see "`book.uuid` and `annotation.uuid` for imported records". It is NOT the key re-import idempotency rests on: that is the `sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceAnnotationUUID` pair below. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE SET NULL | The book this annotation belongs to. `NULL` means the book was deleted and the annotation outlived it; what the book was is remembered by the snapshot in the last four columns. Migration v18 dropped the NOT NULL constraint and replaced the cascade with nulling — see "An annotation outlives its book". |
| `fileSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | Hash of the specific book file the position is anchored to (useful when a book has several formats with different pagination/structure). |
| `type` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Annotation type: `highlight`, `underline`, `note`, `text`, `area`, `ink`. The last three are for PDF, arriving in subproject 2. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". |
| `text` | TEXT | NULL | The highlighted text. |
| `comment` | TEXT | NULL | User's comment on the annotation. |
| `color` | TEXT | NULL | Highlight color NAME (`"yellow"`), not a value — see "`annotation.color` and `annotation.colorHex`". |
| `pageLabel` | TEXT | NULL | Displayed page label (not an index — what's actually printed in the book, e.g. "xiv" or "241"). **A caveat about databases Beresta wrote before v32:** until `chapterPath` existed, the Calibre import put the top-level table-of-contents HEADING here, because the schema had no place for the path. Migration v32 does not repair that (a migration never rewrites data, its own past data included); the first import after it writes the path into `chapterPath` and leaves this column empty, because a Calibre EPUB annotation carries no printed page number at all. In an existing database there is nothing to tell one from the other — a known limitation, not a guarantee. |
| `sortIndex` | TEXT | NOT NULL | "Book order" sort string, see "Value formats". |
| `position` | TEXT | NOT NULL | JSON representation of the annotation's position, see "Value formats". |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Creation date, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last modification date, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL if not deleted. See "What soft deletion means". |
| `colorHex` | TEXT | NULL | Highlight color VALUE (`"#ffd54f"`), always consistent with `color` — see "`annotation.color` and `annotation.colorHex`". Column added by migration v9, and it sits LAST rather than next to `color`: `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` appends the column at the end, and that is exactly how the table looks in a live database. Third-party code that reads rows positionally (`SELECT *`) must account for this order; naming columns explicitly is safer. |
| `sourceLibraryUUID` | TEXT | NULL | The source library of the annotation (`library_id.uuid` of a particular Calibre library) — the first half of the key by which re-import recognizes an already stored row. NULL for an annotation created by Beresta itself. Column added by migration v12 and, like `colorHex`, appended AT THE END of the table. |
| `sourceAnnotationUUID` | TEXT | NULL | The annotation's identifier IN THE SOURCE (Calibre's raw `annot_data.uuid`/`annot_id`, 22 base64 characters) — the second half of the source key. NULL for an annotation created by Beresta itself. Added by migration v12. |
| `anchorFileSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | Hash of the book file the anchor in `position` has already been checked AGAINST (migration v14). Equal to `fileSHA256` — the anchor has been refined against that file; NULL or a different value — refinement has not happened yet, or the book file has been replaced since. It is not a reference to content but a memory of work done: see "`annotation.anchorFileSHA256` and anchor refinement". Appended AT THE END of the table, like `colorHex` and the source-key pair. |
| `readingId` | INTEGER | NULL, FK → `reading.id` ON DELETE SET NULL | The reading during which the highlight was made. NULL means "outside any reading": the highlight predates the book's history and there is nothing to attribute it to. Added by migration v15, appended AT THE END of the table. |
| `bookUUID` | TEXT | NULL | The book's portable ADDRESS (`book.uuid`) as of the annotation's last write. It is the only way an annotation addresses its book beyond this device: `bookId` is a local autoincrement. An orphan is reunited with a returning book through it. It permits NULL for the reason given in "An annotation outlives its book"; every Beresta write path fills it in. Added by migration v18, which rebuilds the table; appended AT THE END. |
| `bookTitle` | TEXT | NULL | The book's title as of the annotation's last write — a CAPTION, not a mirror of the book: while the book is alive, display its live fields. Added by migration v18, appended AT THE END. |
| `bookAuthors` | TEXT | NULL | The book's authors as one string joined with ", " in `bookAuthor.position` order. NULL means the book had no authors (an empty string would claim "the author is known and it is empty"). Added by migration v18, appended AT THE END. |
| `bookCoverSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | The book's cover as of the annotation's last write. Listed as a garbage-collection root — see "Blob store garbage-collection roots". Added by migration v18, appended AT THE END. |
| `intent` | TEXT | NULL | What the excerpt is FOR: `implement` (act on it) or `remember` (keep the thought). A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. NULL means "not assigned", and that is a legitimate state. Neither a color nor a type — see "Color, topic, intent, and type: four different things". Migration **v28**. |
| `processed` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'raw'` | Processing state: `raw`, `sorted`, `used`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. Stored, not derived from the presence of a comment — see below. Migration **v28**. |
| `topic` | TEXT | NULL | The excerpt's topic in the reader's own words. Free text, not a heading picked from a list. NULL means the topic was not named; it is never written as an empty string (the same rule as `readerDecision.note`). Migration **v28**. |
| `layerId` | INTEGER | NULL, FK → `annotationLayer.id` ON DELETE SET NULL | The layer the mark sits on; NULL is the main layer. Nulling rather than a cascade: deleting a layer returns its marks to the main layer, it does not erase them. Migration **v28**. |
| `searchText` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Normalized text for full-text search: `text`, `comment` and `topic` joined into one string. A derived column — computed by a formula, like `colorHex`; stored for the sake of the `annotationSearch` index. See "Relationship with full-text search". Appended AT THE END of the table. Migration **v29**. |
| `chapterPath` | TEXT | NULL | Path through the table of contents: headings from the top level down, a JSON array of strings — see "Value formats". NULL means the path is unknown; it is never written as an empty array. Appended AT THE END of the table. Migration **v32**. |
| `reviewDueAt` | TEXT | NULL | When to show the excerpt again, ISO 8601 UTC. NULL means it is not scheduled, and that is the state of most excerpts: an entry joins the queue by the reader's decision or by the `remember` intent. Appended AT THE END of the table. Migration **v46**. |
| `reviewStability` | REAL | NULL | FSRS-6 stability: the number of days after which the chance of recall falls to 0.9. Computed by `SpacedRepetition`; the database only stores it. Migration **v46**. |
| `reviewDifficulty` | REAL | NULL | FSRS-6 difficulty, 1…10. Migration **v46**. |
| `reviewCount` | INTEGER | NULL | How many times the reader answered on this excerpt. NULL means never; there is deliberately no `0` default, otherwise "never shown" would be indistinguishable from "removed from the schedule". Migration **v46**. |
| `reviewLastAt` | TEXT | NULL | When the reader last answered, ISO 8601 UTC. Elapsed time is measured from this, not from the scheduled date: people answer when they come. Migration **v46**. |

The four columns of migration v28 were appended with `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN`
and therefore sit AT THE END, in the order listed; the table was not rebuilt
(v18 was the last to do that), so its four indexes, both foreign keys, and the
v19/v21 triggers are the same ones. The order is observable
(`PRAGMA table_info('annotation')`) and pinned by a test — including a check
against THIS VERY TABLE: since 20260811 seam 2 (`PreFreezeSeamBTests`) reads
this document and requires the column order in it to match the live database,
in both language halves. The open-format promise is verifiable exactly as far
as the order in the document matches the order in the file. `searchText` of
migration v29 was appended the same way — after the v28 fields — and
`chapterPath` of migration v32 after it, which is the table's last column
today.

Index `annotation_on_book_sort` on (`bookId`, `sortIndex`) — fast retrieval
of a book's annotations in reading order with a single `ORDER BY`.

Index `annotation_on_processed` on (`processed`, `createdAt`) (added by
migration v28) — the sorting queue: "raw first, oldest first". The second
column spares the read a sort (the same trick as `readerDecision_on_book`).

Physically deleting a book **does not take its annotations with it**: since
migration v18 the foreign key is `ON DELETE SET NULL`, so annotations stay in
the database with an empty `bookId` and the book's snapshot on their own row.
Before v18 this was a cascade, and deleting a book erased every note about it.
Details and consequences are in "An annotation outlives its book".

The partial unique index `annotation_on_source` on (`sourceLibraryUUID`,
`sourceAnnotationUUID`) with the condition `sourceAnnotationUUID IS NOT NULL`
(added by migration v12) works exactly like `book_on_source` does for books: one
and the same source annotation cannot land in the database twice, while
annotations without a source (created by Beresta itself) are not in the index at
all and therefore never collide with each other. The condition is mandatory: in
SQLite every NULL in a unique index counts as distinct from every other NULL, so
without the condition the index would neither forbid the duplicate nor stop
demanding uniqueness from empty pairs for no reason.

Index `annotation_on_reading` on (`readingId`) (added by migration v15) —
retrieving the highlights of a single pass: without it "show me what I marked on
the first read" would cost a full table scan.

The partial index `annotation_on_orphan` on (`bookUUID`) with the condition
`bookId IS NULL` (added by migration v18) covers orphans and nothing else. It
is partial rather than full because both consumers of a `bookUUID` lookup from
the annotation side (the orphan list, and reunification when a book comes back)
ask exactly `WHERE bookId IS NULL AND bookUUID = ?`: a live annotation already
has its book and has no reason to look it up by address. The same trick as
`book_on_source`, `annotation_on_source`, and `reading_on_source`.

**Color, topic, intent, and type: four different things.** They are easy to
conflate and expensive to conflate, because they answer different questions:

- `type` — the mark's FORM: highlight, underline, note;
- `color`/`colorHex` — its APPEARANCE: what the mark is drawn with. Color names
  themselves are not a column but `setting` rows: the palette is a preference,
  not part of the record;
- `topic` — what the excerpt is ABOUT;
- `intent` — its ROUTE: act on it or keep the thought.

The temptation to fold the route into the color ("yellow means a task") was
measured and rejected: the owner uses five colors — 217 blue, 41 green, 25
yellow, 2 red, 2 purple — and deriving intent from color would have declared one
single route for 217 excerpts at once. The rule for third-party code: a color
carries no meaning beyond the one a human put there by eye, and it must not be
read as an intent.

**The processing state is STORED, not derived from the presence of a comment.**
The second temptation — "there's a thought written down, so it has been
processed" — was checked against the same measurement: 23 of the owner's 287
excerpts carry a non-empty comment, and the very first of them is the single
word "Сделать" ("To do") — an INTENT, not a processing state. Comment and
processing answer different questions, and deriving one from the other would
have declared twenty-three excerpts processed when nobody ever said so.
Migration v28 therefore backfills nothing: every existing row gets `raw` from
the column default, and that is the truth rather than a placeholder — the
owner's 287 excerpts are exactly raw material.

That default is non-empty, unlike its three neighbours, and that too is
content: "not processed" is an ANSWER, while "not assigned", "topic not named",
and "main layer" are the absence of one. Hence the difference in obligation:
`processed` is `NOT NULL`, the other three permit NULL and have no default at
all.

**The topic is free text, not a heading from a list.** A mandatory heading at
save time was rejected as maximal friction: excerpts are made on the run, and a
field that demands a decision at that moment stops being filled in within half a
year. The topic has no backfill and nowhere to get one: the owner's annotations
carry zero tags (`annotationTag` is empty), so there is nothing to derive a
topic from.

**A mark's layer is a reference, not a visibility flag.** Layer visibility
("clean view" versus "with traces") is a `setting` row with `scope = 'book'`,
not a column here: it is one particular screen's answer, and a column would
override the choice made on another screen. See `annotationLayer`.

#### `annotationTag`

Many-to-many link between annotations and tags.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `annotationId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `annotation.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The annotation. |
| `tagId` | INTEGER | part of PK, NOT NULL, FK → `tag.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The tag. |

#### `readingPosition`

The current reading position of a book on a specific device. Kept separate
from `changeLog` because it is written far more often than everything else
(on every scroll/page turn) — folding writes like these into the general
change log would bloat it for no benefit.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Identifier. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book. |
| `deviceId` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Identifier of the device the position was recorded on. |
| `fileSHA256` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Digest of the book file the position belongs to. An empty string means the file is unknown (rows that existed before migration v16). Empty string rather than NULL on purpose: in SQLite every NULL in a unique index counts as distinct, so uniqueness over such a column would not work at all — the same trick as `setting.deviceId`. Added by migration v16, which **rebuilds the table** — which is why the column sits here, fourth, rather than at the end: a rebuild puts a column where it was declared, next to the two other members of the unique key. |
| `position` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Position as a **plain string**, not JSON: a CFI for EPUB, a page number for PDF. Unlike `annotation.position`, no multi-selector format is needed here — a reading position doesn't require a fallback anchor. |
| `progressFraction` | DOUBLE | NOT NULL, default `0` | Fraction read, a number from 0 to 1 — lets a progress bar render without parsing `position`. |
| `updatedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last update date, ISO 8601. |

Uniqueness: (`bookId`, `deviceId`, `fileSHA256`) — exactly one current position
per book FILE per device; writing again updates it.

**Why the key is per file, not per book.** Before migration v16 uniqueness was
(`bookId`, `deviceId`). 398 of the owner's 1555 books have more than one file,
and a place in one edition is not the same place in another: opening the second
file overwrote the position of the first, and coming back led nowhere.
Uniqueness was declared as a TABLE CONSTRAINT (`UNIQUE(...)` inside `CREATE
TABLE`), which SQLite cannot drop by any means other than rebuilding the table
— so v16 creates a new table, copies the rows over and renames it.

**Known limitation: conflicts resolved by wall-clock time.** Across devices
there is nothing to compare positions by except `updatedAt`, while the correct
answer comes from a hybrid logical clock — as in `changeLog.hlc`. That clock is
absent here, and this is recorded as a limitation rather than fixed: there is no
sync transport yet, so there is nothing to fix. Once there is one,
`readingPosition` must get its own clock stamp.

**Reading positions are written outside the change log.** The dataset registry
declares the table `localOnly` (see “What travels to other devices”), and the
journal guard cannot police it at all. The reason is the same one that keeps it
out of `changeLog`: writes are far too frequent.

**`fileSHA256` is listed as a garbage-collection root** (`BlobStore.GCRoots`):
while a file has a position, sweeping its bytes would take away the reader's
way back to where they stopped. Unlike `annotation.anchorFileSHA256` this pin is
NOT temporary — a position row lives as long as the book does — so deleting a
book file must delete the positions of that file too.

#### `changeLog`

Change log for future cross-device synchronization (populated in subproject
3). The schema is laid down ahead of time so no data migration is needed
after the first large library import.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Local row identifier of the log entry (local only, not used in sync). |
| `dataset` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Name of the dataset the change belongs to (e.g. `"book"`). |
| `rowUUID` | TEXT | NOT NULL | UUID of the changed row within that dataset (not `id` — `id` is local to a given database, while `uuid` survives synchronization between devices). |
| `column` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Name of the changed field. A reserved SQL word — manual queries must quote it: `"column"`. |
| `value` | TEXT | NULL | The field's new value as a string. `NULL` means the field was set to `NULL`, not that the value is unknown. |
| `hlc` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Hybrid logical clock stamp at the moment of the change, see "Value formats". |
| `syncedSeq` | INTEGER | NULL | Sequence number assigned by the sync server once successfully sent. `NULL` means the change hasn't been sent yet. |
| `origin` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'user'`, CHECK (value shape) | Who made the edit, see "`changeLog.origin`" under "Value formats". Added by migration v7. |

**The log is not a send queue — it is an index of per-field timestamps, and
it must not be pruned.** The "last write wins" merge rule needs the `hlc`
stamp of each field's CURRENT value, and the tables themselves do not store
it — this log does. Hence, for every "row + field" pair the winning entry
(the one with the greatest `hlc`) must stay in the log forever; only entries
superseded by a newer entry of the same pair may be removed. `syncedSeq`
marks delivery, not a licence to delete. A third-party tool that decides to
"clean up the grown log" wholesale will silently break merging: the next
incoming change will have nothing to compare against and an older value will
overwrite a newer one.

Index `changeLog_on_pending` on (`syncedSeq`, `hlc`) — fast retrieval of
unsent changes in chronological order (`WHERE syncedSeq IS NULL ORDER BY
hlc`). `changeLog` deliberately has no foreign keys to `book`/`annotation`/…:
`rowUUID` can outlive its source row (recording the fact of a deletion is
itself a log entry), and a single foreign key cannot span a log table that
covers several datasets with different-typed primary keys.

Index `changeLog_on_row_column` on (`dataset`, `rowUUID`, `column`, `hlc`) —
added by migration v7 for the conflict-resolution rule: find the current
winner for a "row + field" pair (the row with the highest `hlc` among rows
sharing the same `dataset`/`rowUUID`/`column`). `changeLog_on_pending` does
not serve this query — it only answers "what hasn't been sent yet".

**Precondition the conflict-resolution rule rests on.** The winner for a
"row + field" pair is the LOG ENTRY with the highest `hlc`, not necessarily
the source of the row's current value in its main table. The two coincide
only as long as EVERY edit goes through the journal. Today — even as this
section is being written — none does: it is populated in subproject 3 (see
the table description above). Once journaling gets wired in piece by piece,
any write path that bypasses the journal will leave the conflict resolver
with no record of a user's edit where one really happened — and it will
silently decide that row is fair game to overwrite.

#### `changeLogClock`

Hybrid logical clock state per device — the last stamp issued. Not listed
among the tables in the subproject plan, but present in the actual database:
without it, the clock would lose its monotonicity guarantee across every app
restart (each restart the stamp would simply start over from the current
physical time, which is not a problem by itself, but gives no protection
against the system clock being turned back between runs).

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `deviceId` | TEXT | PK, NOT NULL | Device identifier. |
| `lastHLC` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last stamp issued to this device (same format as `changeLog.hlc`). |

One row per device: `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT(deviceId) DO UPDATE` maintains
exactly one current stamp. The row is updated in the same transaction as the
corresponding `changeLog` insert — preserving the atomicity of "read the last
stamp → compute the next one → save it as the last one → write the log
entry".

#### `bookSearch` (virtual, FTS5)

The full-text search index over books. See "Relationship with full-text
search" for details.

| Column | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `searchText` | no declared type | A copy of `book.searchText`, indexed by FTS5. The only content column of the virtual table; the virtual table's `rowid` equals `book.id`. FTS5 columns have no type at all: `PRAGMA table_info('bookSearch')` returns an empty type and `.schema` shows `fts5(searchText, …)` without one — values are stored and indexed as text. |

Alongside the virtual table SQLite creates four shadow tables of its own —
`bookSearch_data`, `bookSearch_idx`, `bookSearch_docsize`,
`bookSearch_config`. They are FTS5 internals: never read or write them
directly, and skip them when enumerating the database's tables.

#### `setting`

Unified settings storage. Added by migration v10 (task 12 of plan
`20260806-1b-format-fixes`) instead of the four different places
independently proposed by four project documents: module toggles, reading
settings, theme choice, and book settings. A book setting, a module toggle,
and a theme choice differ from each other ONLY in scope (`scope`/`scopeId`)
and in whether the setting syncs (`deviceId`) — one row of this table covers
all four cases.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Deterministic (not random) row identifier — see "`setting.scope`, `setting.scopeId`, and `setting.deviceId`" below. |
| `scope` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'app'` | Scope: `app` — an application setting (module toggle, theme choice, reading setting), `book` — a specific book's setting. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". |
| `scopeId` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Address within `scope`: empty string for `app`, `book.uuid` for `book`. |
| `key` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Setting name (e.g. `"reader.font"`). |
| `value` | TEXT | NULL | Setting value. `NULL` means the setting was cleared (does not resurrect its old value on the next read). |
| `deviceId` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | `''` — a shared setting (visible to every device). A non-empty value — a setting of ONE device (visible only to it). `NULL` is not allowed here — see the next section. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the row was last modified, ISO 8601. |

Uniqueness: (`scope`, `scopeId`, `key`, `deviceId`) — exactly one row per
setting address (device included). One column, one value: the set of
settings is NOT packed into a single JSON object — merge resolution works
per field of a row, not per whole object, and two devices' edits to
different keys of the same object would lose one of the two.

#### `device`

Registry of known devices for future synchronization (subproject 3). Added
by migration v10 together with `setting`. Its own row is created by
`DeviceIdentity.current(in:)` the first time the storage is opened; rows for
other devices will appear only together with synchronization.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `deviceId` | TEXT | PK, NOT NULL | Device identifier — same one used in `setting.deviceId`/`changeLog.hlc`/`readingPosition.deviceId`. |
| `name` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Display name of the device. |
| `platform` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Platform (e.g. `"macOS"`). |
| `lastSeenAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the device was last active, ISO 8601. |

**The identifier is random, not system-derived.** `deviceId` is an ordinary
`UUID` minted the first time this storage is opened — not the machine serial
number and not `IOPlatformUUID`. The reason is not convenience: the value
travels into `readingPosition.deviceId`, into the tail of every
`changeLog.hlc` stamp and, starting with subproject 3, into the sync message
— that is, into an open format read by third-party programs, and into someone
else's storage. A system identifier in that role would be a durable
cross-context identifier of the user; a random one is exactly as useful
(telling devices apart) and says nothing about the hardware. A consequence
third-party code should know about: a copied storage carries the device
identity with it — two copies of one file consider themselves the same device
until synchronization tells them apart.

**Today "our own row" means "the only row".** The table carries no explicit
"this device is me" marker: as long as only Beresta itself populates the
registry, it holds exactly one row. As soon as synchronization starts adding
rows for other devices, "the only one" will stop meaning "ours" — which is
why `DeviceIdentity.current(in:)` does not pick the first row it finds when
there are two or more, but refuses to work
(`RegistryError.ambiguousLocalDevice`). Subproject 3 will have to bring an
explicit marker of the local row with it; this is a known and deliberately
noisy limitation, not an oversight.

#### `readingSession`

A stretch of reading: from which place to which, and from which time to which.
Added by migration v39.

| Column | Type | Constraints | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK AUTOINCREMENT | The local row number. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | The portable address of the stretch — what synchronisation addresses it by. |
