| `bookId` | INTEGER | NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE SET NULL | The book. NULL means the book was deleted and the stretch remained: the person removed someone else's file from the library, not their own reading (the same technique as the annotation's, v18). |
| `bookUUID` | TEXT | NULL | The portable address of the book. The only thing by which the stretch addresses the book beyond this device: `bookId` is a local autoincrement. |
| `deviceId` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | Where the reading happened. **Not a housekeeping mark:** "where did I read" is a meaningful question, and a merged journal of two devices can no longer answer it. |
| `startedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The start of the stretch, ISO 8601. |
| `endedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The end of the stretch, ISO 8601. |
| `fromProgress` | REAL | NOT NULL, default 0 | The fraction the stretch started at. |
| `toProgress` | REAL | NOT NULL, default 0 | The fraction it ended at. May be LESS than the start: a person may page backwards, and re-reading is reading too. Treating "read" as the difference, discarding the negative, is the business of whoever counts, not of the format. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The moment of the row's last edit, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | The soft-deletion tombstone. NULL means the stretch is alive. |
| `fileSHA256` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `''` | The file the stretch ran over (migration v47, format 1.12). An empty string means the file is unknown: that is how pre-1.12 stretches read. **Part of the stretch's identity, not a detail:** EPUB and PDF of one book have their own scales, and without the file a stretch "from 0.60 to 0.02" is indistinguishable from reading backwards. Speed is derived from it too: share × characters for files that have characters, share per minute for PDF. |

Indexes: `readingSession_on_book` on (`bookId`) — one book's history;
`readingSession_on_started` on (`startedAt`) — "how much did I read that day",
the most frequent question asked of this table.

**A stretch, not an event.** A movement event arrives once every one and a half
seconds — the position writer has its own smoothing — and writing each of them
would produce a journal with 2400 rows per hour of reading and no meaning at all.
A row appears when the person stops reading.

**Fractions, not pages.** An EPUB has no pages: they depend on the type size and
the window width and change for the same book from evening to evening. A fraction
is comparable across devices and with itself yesterday.

#### `reading`

One reading of a book: a separate pass with its own dates and its own number.
Added by migration v15.

**Why a separate entity.** Before it, a book had a single reading-date field
(`book.finishedAt`). Read it again and the date was overwritten — the first
reading disappeared. Highlights, meanwhile, sat in one undifferentiated pile:
what was marked on the first pass looked exactly like what was marked on the
second. With a history, a book gains a sequence of passes, and every highlight
gains the pass it belongs to (`annotation.readingId`). Mature products made the
same call: `ReadThrough` in Bookwyrm (its own dates, its own `stopped_date`),
`user_book_reads` in Hardcover, `repeat`/`REPEATING` in AniList.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier; `annotation.readingId` points at it. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier of the reading, used by sync. For a reading assembled from a Calibre flag it is deterministic, see below. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book that was read. |
| `ordinal` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, default `1` | Sequence number of the pass within the book, starting at 1. The schema does NOT require (`bookId`, `ordinal`) to be unique — see indexes below. |
| `status` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'reading'` | State of the pass: `reading` (open), `paused` (set aside), `finished` (read to the end), `abandoned` (given up). A closed dictionary, enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". The `paused` value was introduced by migration v24. |
| `startedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Start date of the pass, ISO 8601. Optional: a reading recorded after the fact usually has none. |
| `finishedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Date the pass stopped running, ISO 8601: for `finished` — when it was completed, for `abandoned` — when it was dropped, for `paused` — when it was set aside, for `reading` — always NULL. Also optional, see "Why dates are optional". |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the RECORD appeared, ISO 8601 — not when the reading happened. A reading can be backdated to any date, so the two moments differ. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Date the record was last changed, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL if the reading is alive. Unlike `book`/`bookFile`/`collection`, soft deletion here is functional: history reads filter on `deletedAt IS NULL`. |
| `sourceLibraryUUID` | TEXT | NULL | Source library — the first half of the key by which a re-import recognizes ITS OWN reading. NULL for a reading created inside Beresta. |
| `sourceBookUUID` | TEXT | NULL | The book in the source — the second half of the source key. |
| `medium` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'unknown'` | Medium of the pass: `paper`, `file`, `audio`, `unknown`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. Added by migration **v26**; on the default, see below. |
| `startedAtPrecision` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'day'` | How precisely the start date is known: `year`, `month`, `day`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. Migration **v26**. |
| `finishedAtPrecision` | TEXT | NOT NULL, default `'day'` | The same for the finish date. Migration **v26**. |
| `rating` | INTEGER | NULL | Rating of THIS pass, NULL if not rated. Not to be confused with `book.rating` — see below. Migration **v26**. |
| `verdict` | TEXT | NULL | The reader's verdict on the pass in their own words, NULL if there are none. It is never written as an empty string (the same rule as `readerDecision.note`). Migration **v26**. |
| `fileSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | Hash of the file the pass was read FROM; NULL if unknown or if it was not a file. A reference by content rather than by `bookFile.id` — see below. A garbage-collection root. Migration **v26**. |
| `purpose` | TEXT | NULL | Why THIS pass was read: `newKnowledge`, `deeperDive`, `otherView`, `recommended`, `trending`, `motivation`, `fun`, `familiarAuthor`, `sleep`, `spiritual`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. NULL means "not stated", and that is a legitimate state. Migration **v27**. |
| `depth` | TEXT | NULL | How deeply THIS pass was read: `skimmed`, `scanned`, `read`, `studied`. A closed dictionary, enforced by the database. NULL means "not stated". Migration **v27**. |

The six columns of migration v26 and the two of migration v27 were appended
with `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` and therefore sit AT THE END, in the order listed
— the six first, then the two, i.e. in migration order; the table was not
rebuilt, so its indexes, foreign key and v21 triggers are the same ones. The
order is observable (`PRAGMA table_info('reading')`) and pinned by a test — and,
as with the annotation, it is checked against THIS VERY TABLE in both language
halves (seam 2, `PreFreezeSeamBTests`, 20260811).

Indexes: `reading_on_book` on (`bookId`, `ordinal`) — retrieving a book's
history with a single `ORDER BY`; `reading_on_source` — a **partial unique**
index on (`sourceLibraryUUID`, `sourceBookUUID`) restricted to
`sourceBookUUID IS NOT NULL` (the same technique as `book_on_source` and
`annotation_on_source`): one source book gets at most one imported reading.

**A paused pass and an abandoned one differ by intent, and by nothing else.**
A paused pass (`paused`) is one you mean to come back to: returning RESUMES it
rather than starting a second one — the state becomes `reading` again and
`finishedAt` is cleared. An abandoned pass (`abandoned`) is not resumed: after
it you start a NEW pass with the next number, and the abandoned one stays in
the history as it was. Hence the difference in the book's status too: a paused
book is `paused`, an abandoned one is `stopped` (see "`book.readStatus`").
Neither state existed before migration v24: setting a book aside could only be
expressed as an abandoned pass, so "set aside" and "gave up" were one and the
same thing in the schema.

It follows that a paused pass **is closed by exactly what would have closed an
open one**: both "finished" and "gave up" move THAT pass rather than adding a
second one next to it. Otherwise a paused pass would be left hanging, and it
outweighs a finished one (rule 2 of the formula) — so a book you had finished
would still read as "paused". A "read" flag arriving from the import source
does the same.

For a third-party writer one rule follows: **only one paused pass per book
makes sense.** The schema does not enforce it — a unique index would abort
applying a change arriving from a second device (the same reason the pass
number is not unique) — but Beresta's reader, on finding several paused
passes, resumes the last one in display order.

**Why the pass number is NOT unique.** Declaring `UNIQUE(bookId, ordinal)` is
tempting, and Bookwyrm does exactly that (`unique_together (("order",
"book_list"))`) — what it costs is dissected in
`docs/specs/20260806-reading-lists-design.md`, §4: moving an item requires
temporarily lifting a row out of the uniqueness constraint and renumbering its
neighbours. Here the same trouble would arrive from the sync side: two devices
that independently start a re-read will compute THE SAME next number, and a
unique index would abort applying the incoming change — wrecking the merge
instead of protecting the data. The number is display order, not a key; order
is read as `ORDER BY ordinal, uuid`, where the second key resolves ties
deterministically (the same contract as `bookCollection.sortKey`).

**Why dates are optional and the state is not.** 216 of the owner's 320 read
books have no reading date at all. If "finished" were expressed by a non-empty
finish date, those 216 readings could not be recorded: they happened, but when
exactly they ended nobody knows. So the fact lives in `status`, and the date is
a separate, optional value.

**The source key on a reading.** Calibre has no reading record — only a flag on
the book (the custom "read" and "date read" columns). A reading created from
that flag must be recognizable by a re-import, otherwise the very first run
after the migration would create a second reading next to each migrated one.
Hence a key based on the source book and a deterministic `uuid`
(`DeterministicUUID` with record kind `calibre-reading`). The kind differs from
the book's (`calibre`) for a concrete reason: the key is the source book's
`uuid`, so with a shared kind the formula would give the reading EXACTLY THE
SAME identifier as the book itself.

**What the import may do.** The import may remove exactly one reading of a book
— the one carrying the source key, i.e. the one it created itself. Clearing the
flag in Calibre soft-deletes it (the book returns to "unread"), but history the
source never created is left alone. A "read" flag first closes an already OPEN
pass (finishing a book and ticking the box is one fact, not two) and only
creates its own reading if no pass is open.

**Who owns pre-existing highlights.** Migration v15 assigns every highlight of a
book to that book's first (and, at migration time, only) reading — there is no
ambiguity by construction: no other pass exists in the history, and the created
reading is not a new pass but a retrospective record of the very one during
which the highlights were made. This is the ONLY backdated attribution. From
then on a highlight gets its reading at creation time (the book's open pass, if
there is one) and is never reassigned; a highlight of a book without history
keeps `readingId IS NULL`, and that is a legitimate state, not a data error.

**Who opens the pass a highlight lands in.** The rule above answers which pass a
highlight belongs to, but not where that pass comes from. The answer: **the
reader opens it, at the moment of the highlight, and only for a book with no
pass started yet.** Case by case:

1. **The book has no live pass** (i.e. it is `unread` — rule 5 of the status
   formula). The human selects text with a gesture, the reader opens a pass, and
   the highlight lands in it. A highlight made by a gesture in an open book is
   proof that the human is reading it right now; only the reader is entitled to
   make that claim, which is why the data layer does not — its own rule is the
   opposite one, and correct: a highlight arriving from import says nothing
   about whether the book is being read.
2. **The book has an open pass** — the reader does not start a second one; the
   highlight joins the existing pass. The same holds for a pass the human opened
   by hand in the book card: the reader never duplicates it.
3. **The book is marked read, paused or abandoned** — the reader does NOT open a
   pass and does NOT change the status: otherwise it would overrule the human,
   who said themselves that they finished or dropped the book. The price is
   stated out loud: a highlight made in such a book keeps `readingId IS NULL`.
   It cannot be attached to a closed pass — that would assert the mark was made
   during that reading, when in fact it was made after the reading ended.
4. **Several passes** — there is nothing to choose: the highlight lands in the
   OPEN one, and a book has at most one open pass. A highlight is never attached
   to a closed pass.
5. **A highlight exists, then a new pass begins** — the highlight does NOT move.
   The entity was introduced for exactly this distinction ("what I marked then
   versus what I mark now"); moving it would destroy that very meaning.

**Why no migration repairs highlights without a reading.** Rows that landed with
an empty link before this rule took effect stay empty, and there is no new
migration for them. The reasoning is the one behind defaulting a pass's medium to
`unknown` rather than `file`: a guess pinned onto live rows is not data.
Attaching such a highlight to a closed pass would lie about when it was made;
giving it a fresh pass would invent a reading the human never declared, and
change the book's status behind their back. An empty link is the only thing here
that is true. The v15 backfill was entitled to act otherwise precisely because
its highlights predated the history, and these do not.

**The default medium is UNKNOWN, not "file".** The tempting default is `file` —
the reader does open files, after all — but it was measured and rejected.
Calibre has no notion of a medium at all, and migration v15 created the owner's
320 readings precisely from Calibre flags: calling them "read on screen" would
put a guess on the whole history at once. Deriving the medium from the presence
of a file does not work either: on a copy of the live database (measured
20260811) ALL 320 finished books have files, so by that criterion there are zero
paper readings — while the owner does own paper books. The criterion
distinguishes nothing. The rule for a third-party reader follows: `unknown`
means "nobody said", not "read on a device", and must not be treated as a file.

**Date precision is stored NEXT TO the date because it cannot be derived from
it.** Sources that hand out a partial date pad it out to a full one: in Calibre
"the year 2011" becomes `2011-01-01T00:00:00`, and no amount of looking at the
date will separate that from a book genuinely finished on 1 January at
midnight. Until precision is stored, reading code has to guess — and "read in
2011" and "read on 1 January 2011" are different claims; showing them
identically lies in one of the two cases. Each of the two dates carries its own
precision: a pass can start on a known day and end "sometime that year".

Three caveats, without which the promise would exceed what the file delivers:

1. **Precision is a claim, not a derivation.** The database does not reconcile
   it with the date: a row with `finishedAt = '2026-03-14T19:20:00Z'` and
   `finishedAtPrecision = 'year'` is legitimate, and it means "only the year
   2026 is known, the rest is padding". A reader should display exactly as much
   as the precision claims, not as much as the date spells out.
2. **The precision of a date that is absent means nothing.** The column is
   mandatory, so a reading without a date still holds something in it (`day` by
   default). The owner has 216 such readings out of 320.
3. **The one place where precision IS derived from a date is the one-off
   backfill in migration v26.** It marks as yearly those and only those dates
   whose STORED string has the shape "1 January, midnight"
   (`substr(finishedAt, 6, 14) = '01-01T00:00:00'`) — the comparison runs on
   the string rather than on a parsed date, because parsing would require a
   time zone that a historical stamp does not carry: in another zone the same
   instant falls on 31 December. The backfill never assigns month precision to
   anyone — no source writes "the first of the month" in place of an unknown
   day, and assigning `month` on a hunch would claim a precision nobody stated.
   On the owner's live database the backfill is a no-op: of 104 reading dates,
   not one falls on 1 January at midnight.

**A rating on the pass is not the same as a rating on the book.**
`book.rating` came from Calibre and rates the BOOK; `reading.rating` rates THIS
pass. The two live side by side and mean different things: a "measure of
growth" is the difference between ratings across passes, and on the book there
is nothing to express it with — the second pass would overwrite the first. The
file does not fix a scale: how many steps it has is up to whoever writes;
Beresta writes the same scale as `book.rating`. The verdict (`verdict`) is the
same thing in the reader's own words, and emptiness is legitimate for both: the
right not to report is part of the design, not an unfinished form.

**`fileSHA256` is a reference by content, not to a `bookFile` row.** The
obvious way to record "which edition was I reading back then" is a
`bookFileId`, but a `bookFile` row has no identifier of its own (see "What
travels to other devices": `bookFile` is marked unaddressable), and a local
autoincrement points at a different file on another device. The same technique
as `annotation.fileSHA256` (v14) and `readingPosition.fileSHA256` (v16). It
follows that the column is a blob-store garbage-collection root (see "Blob
store garbage-collection roots"): while a pass remembers a file, collection
will not take it.

**"Why I am reading" and "how deeply" live on the PASS, not on the book.** Two
columns answer two independent questions that are usually collapsed into a
single status: the purpose (`purpose`) and the depth (`depth`). Their location
is not obvious and was chosen deliberately: on a second pass through the same
book both differ — "on a recommendation, skimmed" the first time and "to dig
deeper, studied" the second. On the book the second would overwrite the first,
and half the value of the reading history would be gone. The argument is settled
by an asymmetry of cost: reducing the value to the book is trivial (take the
last pass), whereas spreading a single book-level value across several passes
has nothing to spread. Third-party code that wants "the purpose of a book"
should take it from the last pass rather than look for a column on `book` —
there is none.

**Emptiness is legitimate for both columns and means "not stated".** The
purpose is asked once, when a book is moved into reading; declining to answer is
a legitimate outcome, not an unfinished form. The columns therefore accept NULL
and **have no default at all** (unlike `medium` and the precisions, whose
defaults are non-empty): a non-empty default would declare, on the reader's
behalf, why and how they read every book the field was never filled in for.
Migration v27 backfills nothing: neither purpose nor depth can be derived from
data — on the owner's live database all 320 readings hold NULL in both columns
after it.

**Depth is the reader's claim, not the fraction read.** The database neither
can nor tries to reconcile the two: nine tenths of the text read does not mean
the book was studied, and thirty pages of an index is not "skimmed". Povarnin's
first commandment ("the manner of reading must match the purpose of reading")
is about intent, while how far you got is answered by `readingPosition`. The
same holds for the purpose: the file does not verify its truthfulness.

**There are four depth steps, not five.** Povarnin ("How to Read Books", 1924,
§7) gives a ladder of five: leaf through, look over, **selectively**, read,
study. Selectivity answers "what did you read", not "how deeply", and on this
ladder it coincides with the `scanned` step; one field does not need a third
name. The ten purpose values are Igor Mann's closed list ("Chitay!") taken
whole.

#### `readerDecision`

The reader's decision journal: what was decided about a book, why, and when.
Added by migration v25.

**One table for four mechanics — a load-bearing decision, not a saving.** What
has to be recorded is: swapping a book out of the Golden Shelf, giving up on a
book at the threshold (the "fifty pages test"), the outcome of a ten-minute
probe, and dropping a book from the library. Four different gestures — and an
identical record shape: which book, what was decided, for what reason, when.
Four tables would repeat that shape four times, and a third-party reader
assembling a book's decision history would have to read four places instead of
one. What tells the mechanics apart is `kind`, and nothing else.

**Why the schema has no consumer for it yet.** The screens that will write
here come later — some of them after version 1.0. The table is created now
because the schema is being frozen: after the freeze a new table can still be
added (that is a pure addition and does not break a 1.0 reader), but it can no
longer become part of the format third-party tools are written against.

| Column | Type | Nullability | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier — what sync addresses the row by. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book the decision is about. |
| `kind` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Kind of decision: `shelfSwap` (gave up its place on the Golden Shelf), `abandon` (gave up on the book), `probe` (outcome of a ten-minute probe), `discard` (dropped from the library). A closed dictionary enforced by the database — see "What the database itself enforces". |
| `reason` | TEXT | NOT NULL | A reason from the list belonging to THAT kind of decision. An empty string means "no reason stated". |
| `note` | TEXT | NULL | In the reader's own words. NULL — no words of their own. |
| `decidedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the decision was MADE, ISO 8601. |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the RECORD ITSELF appeared, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last modification date of the record, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL — the decision stands. Soft deletion here is functional: reads filter on `deletedAt IS NULL`. |

Index: `readerDecision_on_book` on (`bookId`, `decidedAt`) — the journal is
read as "this book's decisions by date", and the second column spares the read
a sort. There is no uniqueness per book, by design: a journal exists precisely
to hold several decisions about one book.

**"No words of their own" has exactly one spelling — `NULL`.** Beresta never
writes an empty string into `note`: a zero-length string next to `NULL` would
give the open format two spellings of the same thing, and a third-party reader
would be telling "there is a thought" from "there is none" by string length. A
reader of someone else's file that does contain `note = ''` should read it as
no words of their own.

**Why `decidedAt` is not `createdAt`.** Decisions get recorded after the fact —
"I gave up on that one last spring" — and that is not a rare case but the
ordinary one, the same as a reading with no date. Were the two columns one, the
decision date would mean the data-entry date, and the very first pass over the
accumulated refusals ("what exactly do I fail to finish, and when") would count
dates that have nothing to do with the decisions. It follows that `decidedAt`
may lie arbitrarily far in the past while `createdAt` may not: the latter is
about the row appearing.

**What the database enforces here and what it does not.** `kind` is enforced —
a closed dictionary implemented with triggers (see "What the database itself
enforces"). `reason` is NOT, and that is a decision: each kind of decision has
its own list of reasons, no dictionary spans all four, and freezing invented
lists today for mechanics that do not exist yet would produce a database that
refuses the application's own writes — the price migration v24 already paid. A
third-party writer may put any string here; the list of reasons is a rule of
the mechanic doing the writing, not a guarantee of the file.

**The book reference is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, as with a reading, not
`SET NULL`, as with a highlight.** A highlight outlives its book because it is
a value in itself (see "An annotation outlives its book"); a decision about a
book means nothing without the book — it is a judgement about it, not about
itself. In practice the cascade never fires today: book deletion is soft and
the row stays — it is about referential integrity going forward.

**When two editions are merged, decisions move to the survivor** — alongside
highlights and reading history (see "The merge tombstone").

#### `annotationLayer`

A layer of marks: a named stack of highlights that can be shown or hidden as a
whole. Added by migration v28.

**What a layer covers and what it does not.** The design promised that one field
would cover three needs. One of the three — "marks from different readings of
the same book" — was already covered by migration v15 via
`annotation.readingId`, and the layer does not duplicate it. Two remain: the
"clean view / with traces" mode (hide your own marks and reread the book without
them) and someone else's layer when files are exchanged (another person's marks
sit next to yours and stay distinguishable).

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier; `annotation.layerId` points at it. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier — what synchronization addresses. |
| `name` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The layer's name as a human sees it. |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Creation date, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last modification date, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL — the layer stands. Soft deletion here is functional: reads filter on `deletedAt IS NULL`. |

**A layer has no book reference at all, and that is a decision.** A layer spans
the library: "excerpts for the article" collects quotes from ten books, and
tying it to one would make it useless. Consequence for edition merges:
`RecordMerge` never touches layers — it walks the tables that declared `bookId`
a local reference, and a layer has no such column.

**Deleting a layer does not take its marks.** The `annotation.layerId` foreign
key is declared `ON DELETE SET NULL` rather than a cascade: a layer is a way of
LOOKING at excerpts, not their owner, and dropping a layer means ceasing to
divide them into stacks, not erasing them. A cascade would mean that discarding
someone else's layer after an exchange takes your own excerpts with it — exactly
the mistake v18 already corrected for books. The book reference (`reading`,
`readerDecision`) cascades for the opposite reason: a record about a book means
nothing without the book, while an excerpt without a layer means exactly what it
meant before layers existed.

The promise is verifiable by opening the file: delete an `annotationLayer` row
and look at `annotation.layerId` — it becomes NULL and the rows stay. Beresta
does the same but by its own path: its deletion is SOFT, the layer row remains
for synchronization's sake, so the foreign key never fires and the code nulls
`layerId` itself.

**There is no uniqueness on the name, by design.** A unique index would break
applying an edit arriving from a second device (the same reason `reading`'s
pass number is not unique); display order is read as `ORDER BY name, uuid`,
where the second key resolves ties deterministically.

**Layer visibility is not stored here.** "Clean view" on this screen and "with
traces" on another are different answers over one store, so visibility lives in
a `setting` row with `scope = 'book'` (such rows travel per-row, see "What
travels to other devices"), not in a column of this table.

#### `bookmark`

A named bookmark: the place a reader wanted to RETURN to. Added by migration
v31.

**How it differs from a reading position.** `readingPosition` answers one
question — where the book was closed — and is physically one row per "book +
device" pair (the unique key of migration v16). "Where did I want to return to"
is a different question, and one book has many answers to it: two or three
ribbons in a book are ordinary, and the medieval rotating bookmark told the
stopping place from the return place long before the printing press. One row per
book would mean the second ribbon pushes out the first.

**How it differs from an annotation.** An annotation is about text (the quote,
the colour, one's own thought, the triage); a bookmark is about a place and
nothing else. They share exactly two things, and both are shared deliberately:
the address format (`position`, the same W3C selectors) and the ordering-key
format (`sortIndex`). Because of the first, a bookmark survives a change of
edition and of file; because of the second, one book's bookmarks and excerpts
fall into a single order — "in book order" — with no conversion at all.

| Column | Type | Required | Meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | INTEGER | PK, autoincrement | Internal identifier; never leaves this database. |
| `uuid` | TEXT | NOT NULL, UNIQUE | Stable external identifier — what synchronization addresses. |
| `bookId` | INTEGER | NOT NULL, FK → `book.id` ON DELETE CASCADE | The book the bookmark sits in. |
| `fileSHA256` | TEXT | NULL | Fingerprint of the file the place was found in, or NULL if the file is unknown. A reference by CONTENT, not to a `bookFile` row; a garbage-collection root (see that section). |
| `label` | TEXT | NULL | The bookmark's name as given by a human. NULL means there is none; it is never written as an empty string. |
| `position` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The place in the book. **The same format as `annotation.position`** — see the section of that name. |
| `sortIndex` | TEXT | NOT NULL | The "in book order" key. **The same format and the same formula as `annotation.sortIndex`** — see the section of that name. |
| `createdAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | When the bookmark was placed, ISO 8601. |
| `modifiedAt` | TEXT | NOT NULL | Last modification date, ISO 8601. |
| `deletedAt` | TEXT | NULL | Soft-deletion date, ISO 8601, or NULL — the bookmark stands. Soft deletion here is functional: reads filter on `deletedAt IS NULL`. |

Index: `bookmark_on_book_sort` on (`bookId`, `sortIndex`) — the only read of
bookmarks ("this book's bookmarks in book order") goes through it, with no sort.

**A bookmark does not outlive its book — unlike an annotation.** The foreign key
cascades and `bookId` is mandatory. An annotation outlives the book on purpose
(migration v18): a quote is valuable in itself, and its snapshot keeps the
signature of the book it came from. A bookmark outside its book addresses
nothing — it IS a place in it — so there is no book snapshot here at all: there
is nothing to sign. The promise is verifiable by opening the file: delete a
`book` row and look at `bookmark` — none of its bookmarks remain. The
application itself never deletes a book physically (deletion is soft), so the
cascade only fires for whoever deletes the row directly.

**There is no uniqueness on the "book + place" pair, by design.** Two bookmarks
in one place are legitimate — one place, two reasons to come back — and after
merging two editions of one book (see "The merge tombstone") they occur
literally: each edition had its own ribbon on the same paragraph. Display order
is read as `ORDER BY sortIndex, uuid`, where the second key resolves ties
deterministically (`id` will not do — it is local).

**A known limitation: Beresta places a bookmark only where an order can be
derived.** The key is computed from a fragment pointer (an EPUB CFI) inside
`position`; places without such a pointer — a bare quote, a PDF page, an unknown
selector from a third-party tool — the application refuses to bookmark rather
than substituting a placeholder key ("00000#…" is a legitimate place in a book,
the start of the first section, and passing it off as "unparsed" would put the
bookmark at the beginning of the book without a single sign of trouble). The
format is not narrowed by this: the column accepts any string per the
"`annotation.sortIndex`" section, and a third-party writer may put its own key
there — Beresta will read such a row as it stands.

**The "place and key" pair changes only together, but this is not enforced by
the database.** Our code cannot create a row whose key was derived from a
different place (the value is derived by one formula on every write), whereas a
third-party writer's row with a mismatched pair is read as it stands — and that
is a deliberate decision: `ORDER BY` in the database will follow the STORED
value anyway, and substituting another value at read time would show an order
the file does not contain.

#### `annotationSearch` (virtual, FTS5)

The full-text search index over annotations. Added by migration v29. Built
exactly like `bookSearch` — see "Relationship with full-text search" for
details.

| Column | Type | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `searchText` | no declared type | A copy of `annotation.searchText`, indexed by FTS5. The only content column of the virtual table; the virtual table's `rowid` equals `annotation.id`. FTS5 columns have no type at all — see the same caveat under `bookSearch`. |

Alongside it SQLite creates four shadow tables of its own —
`annotationSearch_data`, `annotationSearch_idx`, `annotationSearch_docsize`,
`annotationSearch_config`. They are FTS5 internals: never read or write them
directly, and skip them when enumerating the database's tables.

### Value formats

Some decisions cannot be inferred from column types (SQLite stores dates,
JSON, and coordinates the same way — as text), so they are recorded here.

#### What the database itself enforces

This document's rule: **anything called a guarantee here must be verifiable by
opening the file.** Below is the complete list of constraints that ANY writer
gets, not just the Beresta application. Anything absent from this list is not
enforced by the database and must not be relied on as a guarantee.

Besides the ordinary `NOT NULL`, `UNIQUE`, primary keys, and foreign keys
(listed per table above), the schema contains:

| What | Where | Mechanism |
|---|---|---|
| the shape of `changeLog.origin` — one of three templates | `changeLog` | `CHECK`, migration v7 |
| closed value dictionaries — sixteen columns, see the table below | `book`, `bookFile`, `annotation`, `collection`, `bookCollection`, `setting`, `reading`, `readerDecision` | triggers, migrations v19, v24, v25, v26, v27, v28, and v30 |
| a non-empty source identifier and its pair | `book`, `annotation`, `reading` | triggers, migration v21 |

**Closed value dictionaries.** Sixteen columns accept only the listed values;
anything else is rejected by the database engine itself:

| Column | Accepted values |
|---|---|
| `book.readStatus` | `unread`, `inbox`, `reading`, `paused`, `stopped`, `read` — the dictionary was widened by migrations **v24** (`paused`, `stopped`) and **v41** (`inbox`); the original v19 set: `unread`, `reading`, `read` |
| `book.kind` | `book`, `article` — introduced by migration **v36** together with the column; `supplement` added by **v44** |
| `bookFile.format` | `epub`, `pdf`, `fb2`, `mobi`, `azw3`, `cbz`, `djvu`, `txt`, `html`, `unknown` — the `html` value was added by migration **v36** |
| `annotation.type` | `highlight`, `underline`, `note`, `text`, `area`, `ink` |
| `collection.defaultSort` | `manual`, `title` |
| `setting.scope` | `app`, `book` |
| `reading.status` | `reading`, `paused`, `finished`, `abandoned` — the dictionary was widened by migration **v24** (before it: `reading`, `finished`, `abandoned`) |
| `readerDecision.kind` | `shelfSwap`, `abandon`, `probe`, `discard` — introduced by migration **v25** together with the table itself |
| `reading.medium` | `paper`, `file`, `audio`, `unknown` — introduced by migration **v26** together with the column |
| `reading.startedAtPrecision` | `year`, `month`, `day` — migration **v26** |
| `reading.finishedAtPrecision` | `year`, `month`, `day` — migration **v26** |
| `reading.purpose` | `newKnowledge`, `deeperDive`, `otherView`, `recommended`, `trending`, `motivation`, `fun`, `familiarAuthor`, `sleep`, `spiritual` — migration **v27**; the column is OPTIONAL and NULL passes (see caveat 1 below) |
| `reading.depth` | `skimmed`, `scanned`, `read`, `studied` — migration **v27**; the column is OPTIONAL and NULL passes |
| `annotation.intent` | `implement`, `remember` — migration **v28**; the column is OPTIONAL and NULL passes |
| `annotation.processed` | `raw`, `sorted`, `used` — migration **v28**; the column is `NOT NULL` with the default `raw` |
| `collection.kind` | `books`, `articles` — migration **v30**, the `articles` value added by **v37**; the column is `NOT NULL` with the default `books` |
| `bookCollection.reason` | `reread`, `gratitude`, `milestone` — migration **v30**; the column is OPTIONAL and NULL passes (see caveat 1 below) |

The refusal arrives as an ordinary constraint violation (`SQLITE_CONSTRAINT`,
code 19) with a message like
`book.readStatus: value outside the schema dictionary`. The check is
implemented with `BEFORE INSERT`/`BEFORE UPDATE OF` triggers rather than
`CHECK` for a purely technical reason: in SQLite a `CHECK` can only be
declared when the table is created, so adding one to an existing table would
mean rebuilding five tables. Their force is identical — neither
`INSERT OR IGNORE` nor `INSERT OR REPLACE` can bypass a trigger — and they are
just as easy to inspect as a `CHECK`:
`SELECT sql FROM sqlite_master WHERE type = 'trigger'`.

Four caveats, without which this list would promise more than it delivers:

1. **A dictionary on an OPTIONAL column does not reject emptiness.** Twelve of
   the sixteen columns are declared `NOT NULL`, and a NULL in them is rejected
   by the engine itself, not by the dictionary. Four columns are optional —
   `reading.purpose` and `reading.depth` (migration v27), `annotation.intent`
   (migration v28), and `bookCollection.reason` (migration v30) — and NULL is a
   legitimate value for them meaning "the human
   did not say": the trigger is
   built on `NOT IN (…)`, and `NOT IN` against NULL yields NULL, so it does not
   fire on emptiness at all. What follows for a third-party writer is exactly
   what is written: a closed dictionary on such a column constrains NON-EMPTY
   values and does not demand that the column be filled. Emptiness can be
   written, and a stated value can be erased back to NULL — neither is refused.
2. **Rows that were already in the database before migration v19 were never
   checked.** The trigger applies to writes going forward. This is
   deliberate: a migration must be able to open ANY file, and a retroactive
   check would break opening a database that some other program had already
   written a foreign value into — punishing the reader for someone else's
   write. Beresta's reading code therefore still does not trust these columns
   and answers with an error rather than a crash. The three dictionaries
   introduced by migration v26 (`reading.medium` and both precisions) carry no
   such caveat and cannot: v26 creates the columns itself with a non-empty
   default, so a foreign value has never been in them — not a single row is
   older than its dictionary. The same is true of the two dictionaries of
   migration v27, for a different reason: v27 creates the columns itself, and
   every existing row gets a NULL in them, which the dictionary does not check.
   v28 has both reasons at once: `annotation.intent` gets a NULL, while
   `annotation.processed` gets the non-empty default `raw`, which is itself a
   member of the dictionary (otherwise the migration would leave a database
   whose own rows could not be rewritten). So does v30:
   `bookCollection.reason` gets a NULL, while `collection.kind` gets the
   default `books`, itself a member of its dictionary.
3. **A trigger can be dropped.** `DROP TRIGGER` is available to any writer
   exactly as `UPDATE` is. The constraint guards against mistakes, not malice.
4. **A dictionary is frozen by the schema version that declares it.**
   Widening a set (a new file format, a new shelf sort criterion) is a NEW
   migration that replaces the trigger, not a silent widening on the fly. So a
   database migrated today and one migrated later accept exactly the same
   values.

The flip side of the fourth caveat, worth knowing in advance: some of these
sets are meant to grow. For `collection.defaultSort` the spec foresees four
more criteria (`author`, `added`, `published`, `rating`); for
`bookFile.format`, new formats as support for them lands; `collection.kind`
has a single value today. Everything else is rejected for now; when it
arrives, it arrives with a new migration.

The other side of the same rule is visible on a neighbouring column:
`readerDecision.reason` is NOT declared a dictionary and is not enforced at
all, even though every kind of decision has its own closed list of reasons.
The reason is precisely that those lists belong to mechanics that do not exist
yet and will change along with them: a closed dictionary would mean a new
migration for every label edited in the interface, and until that migration, a
database refusing the application's own writes. What is enforced is in the list
above; the list of reasons is a rule of the writing mechanic, not a guarantee
of the file.

**What widening a dictionary looks like, on a live example.** Migration v24
added two values to `book.readStatus` and one to `reading.status`. Internally
it **drops the four v19 triggers and creates them again** with the new list:
a trigger body cannot be altered any other way, and leaving the old one in
place would mean a database that refuses the application's own writes. Two
consequences matter to a third-party reader. First, **the migration name
determines the set of values**: a file migrated up to v23 accepts three
statuses, one migrated up to v24 accepts five, and there is no way to tell
which without looking at the trigger. Second, **previously accepted values
stay legal**: widening never takes away what was already allowed, otherwise a
migrated database would reject its own older rows.

Migrations v26, v27, v28, and v30 added nine dictionaries that are entirely
NEW — together with the columns they sit on — and still begin with
`DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS`. That
is not belt-and-braces: in a file written by a third-party tool a trigger name
may be taken by anything at all, and `CREATE TRIGGER` on a taken name is an
error — that is, a database we would refuse to open because of someone else's
write. The rule is general: a migration must be able to open any file and may
not assume what is inside it.

#### Dates

Every date column is a string in strict ISO 8601 with a `T` separator and an
explicit time zone, e.g.:

```
2026-08-05T12:00:00Z
```

This is not what SQLite or a naive `Date` serialization writes by default
(GRDB without an explicit strategy would write `2026-08-05 12:00:00.000` —
no `T`, no zone, which is not ISO 8601). The format is set by the
`ISO8601DatabaseCoding` protocol (`ISO8601DateFormatter` with
`.withInternetDateTime`) and is mandatory for every database record with
date fields. Third-party code reading the database directly must parse these
strings as ISO 8601, not as SQLite's own date format.

#### `annotation.position`

A JSON object. There are exactly two shapes depending on document type,
distinguished by the top-level `type` field.

**`bookmark.position` uses the same format** (migration v31): a bookmark
addresses its place with the same W3C selectors, and everything below applies to
both columns without a single caveat. That is a decision, not a coincidence: a
rule written down in two places drifts apart, and a bookmark tied to a page
instead of to text would survive exactly one file.

**EPUB** — `type` is `"epub"`, `selectors` is a non-empty **array** of
selectors following the [W3C Web Annotation Data
Model](https://www.w3.org/TR/annotation-model/): a precise pointer into the
document fragment (EPUB CFI) together with a text-quote fallback anchor that
survives the book file being replaced by another edition/version:

```json
{
  "type": "epub",
  "selectors": [
    {
      "type": "FragmentSelector",
      "conformsTo": "http://www.idpf.org/epub/linking/cfi/epub-cfi.html",
      "value": "epubcfi(/6/8!/4/20,/1:222,/1:311)"
    },
    { "type": "TextQuoteSelector", "exact": "цитата", "prefix": "до ", "suffix": " после" }
  ]
}
```

`FragmentSelector` fields: `type` (always `"FragmentSelector"`), `conformsTo`
(always `"http://www.idpf.org/epub/linking/cfi/epub-cfi.html"` — the EPUB CFI
scheme identifier, mandatory in the W3C model so the reading side knows the
syntax of `value`), `value` (the CFI string itself).

The CFI string is written in the form the reading engine understands: the
spine step, an exclamation mark, then the path inside the section document.
The path after `!` is counted from the CHILDREN of `<html>`, so its first step
for an ordinary highlight is `4` (that is `<body>`). Calibre stores the anchor
with an extra leading step `/2` (the `<html>` element itself); the importer
drops it, and anchors already stored were converted by migration
`v13_annotation_cfi`. A leading step of `2` would mean `<head>` and never
resolves.

`TextQuoteSelector` fields: `type` (always `"TextQuoteSelector"`), `exact`
(required — the quoted text itself), `prefix`/`suffix` (context before/after
the quote; **omitted from the JSON when empty** — an empty string clarifies
nothing and only clutters the format, so third-party code must treat a
missing key the same as an empty string).

A selector with an unrecognized `type` (a third-party tool adding its own
kind of anchor — for example, a future Zotero import bringing a page-markup
selector) is not dropped: Beresta stores its JSON object verbatim — the same
key order, the same nesting (to any reasonable depth, see below), and the
same string escaping it arrived with (a slice of the original text, not a
rebuild) — and writes it back to the same spot the next time the annotation
is saved. Today's reader can't display such a highlight, but it doesn't
corrupt or lose the third-party tool's data either.

**PDF** — `type` is `"pdf"`: a zero-based page number and a list of
highlight rectangles in page coordinates (`[x0, y0, x1, y1]`; PDF readability
lands in subproject 2, but the format is fixed already):

```json
{ "type": "pdf", "pageIndex": 41, "rects": [[10.5, 20.25, 100.0, 40.0]] }
```

**Ink** — `type` is `"ink"`: a zero-based page number and a list of strokes on
it. A stroke is an object of pen width and points in drawing order; the
coordinates are the same as for `rects` — PDF page coordinates.

```json
{
  "type": "ink",
  "pageIndex": 41,
  "paths": [
    {
      "width": 2,
      "points": [[10.5, 20.25], [11, 21, 0.62], [12, 22, 0.8, 1.05, 2.31]]
    }
  ]
}
```

**A point is an array of numbers, and its LENGTH says what is known about it:**

- two numbers `[x, y]` — position only: mouse, trackpad, finger;
- three `[x, y, pressure]` — the stylus reported pressure (0…1) but no tilt;
- five `[x, y, pressure, altitude, azimuth]` — the stylus reported tilt as
  well: the angle to the page plane and the direction, both in radians.

A length of **four is refused**, not guessed: tilt is TWO numbers, and one
without the other does not define a tilt (an angle to the plane without a
direction cannot tell a pen leaning towards the reader from one leaning away).
Guessing which of the two angles arrived would mean repairing someone else's
data — which this format does nowhere. Any other length (one, six) is refused
the same way.

Why pressure and tilt live INSIDE the point rather than beside the stroke:
they change along the stroke, and that difference is what makes a living line
instead of a wire. Why width belongs to the stroke rather than to the whole
position: different strokes of one drawing are legitimately of different
widths (you switched pens and kept drawing in the same mark), and one width
per position would force a second annotation for a second pen.

**Ink is stored as JSON, not as a blob, and the cost is stated.** A blob
(PencilKit's `PKDrawing`) would be more compact, but it is a closed Apple
format inside a format declared open: a third-party reader would get opaque
bytes it can neither draw nor parse. `PKDrawing` stays an editing cache; the
truth is these points. A long stroke in JSON is several times bulkier than a
blob; the format sets no length limit, and the database checks no boundary
(to SQLite this is just TEXT). Should the cost ever prove unbearable, the way
out is to ADD a column with a blob hash next to it — which the 1.0
compatibility rule permits even after the freeze.

**The database does not check value ranges.** Beresta writes pressure in 0…1
and angles in radians; a third-party writer may put something else there and
no trigger will notice — inside JSON the database tells no values apart. This
is a rule for writing, not a guarantee about the file.

**The format does not guarantee that `annotation.type` agrees with the kind of
position either.** A highlight with `type = 'ink'` and an `epub`-shaped
position is a perfectly legal row as far as the database is concerned:
checking that would take parsing JSON inside a trigger, and there is no such
trigger. Reading code must look at `position.type` rather than infer the
position's shape from the annotation type.

**Ink is for PDF only.** It will not come to EPUB: reflow breaks the anchoring
of raster strokes, which is why EPUB gets textual kinds of marks instead
(`docs/specs/20260805-beresta-design.md` §8).

`annotation.position` usually matches one of these three shapes — third-party
code must read `type` and handle all three cases explicitly rather than guess
the structure from which keys are present. But when the top-level `type`
itself is unrecognized (neither `"epub"`, `"pdf"`, nor `"ink"`), the position
is **not rejected**: Beresta stores the incoming JSON verbatim (same key order, same
nesting, same string escaping) and hands it back unchanged the next time
it's written. Tolerating the open format matters more than refusing to read
an annotation over one unfamiliar field — a third-party tool can add its own
kind of anchor without risking silent data loss on the first read in Beresta.

**Limits of the tolerance** (review round 1 — see `packages/beresta-core/
Sources/BerestaCore/Models/Annotation.swift`, the `AnyCodableJSON` type):

- Tolerance covers the **kind** of anchor, not its **syntax**: an
  unrecognized selector or position `type` is kept as-is, but the JSON inside
  it must still be syntactically valid — a number with a leading zero
  (`007`) is rejected, just as the JSON specification itself rejects it, and
  so is a repeated key within one object. Beresta doesn't try to fix or guess
  around syntactically invalid JSON inside an unrecognized node — reading the
  position fails with a clear error instead of silently carrying an invalid
  document forward.
- Nesting depth of an unrecognized node is capped (64 levels of objects/
  arrays) — comfortably more than any realistic selector nesting (at most
  two or three levels in the owner's live library), but finite: parsing
  unbounded depth crashes the process rather than raising an error.
- Verbatim round-tripping is **only guaranteed through the public**
  `AnnotationPosition.encodedJSON()` **method** — the same one `Annotation`
  uses to serialize `position` before writing it to the database. Encoding a
  position that holds an unrecognized node directly through `Codable`/
  `JSONEncoder` (bypassing `encodedJSON()`) throws instead of writing a
  document with foreign content spliced in: the original text is substituted
  back in only AFTER the `Codable` part of encoding has finished, and without
  that step there's nowhere to get the original text from.

#### `annotation.text` — how the quote is stored

A quote is stored not as a verbatim slice of the document but **normalized to a
single form**. A third-party writer needs this rule, otherwise its records will
look foreign next to ours.

The rule: **the soft hyphen (U+00AD) is removed**, **any run of whitespace is
collapsed into a single ordinary space**, **there is no whitespace at the
edges**, **the Unicode form is NFC**.

Each clause answers its own question:

- *Soft hyphen* — a typesetting mark, not part of the word: in another edition
  of the same book it sits elsewhere, or is absent entirely.
- *Collapsing whitespace* — a highlight that crosses a paragraph boundary
  carries a line break and markup indentation. The reader did not select those,
  and in a note they would tear the quote apart mid-sentence.
- *Edges* — a typesetting mark at the selection boundary, not part of the quote.
- *NFC* — "й" can be written as one code point or two; those are different
  strings under byte comparison even though they are identical on screen.

**The same string lives in the record twice**: in the `text` column and inside
the position, in `TextQuoteSelector.exact` (see above). The rule applies to both
copies, and they must agree: were they to diverge, a reader would not know which
of the two editions of the quote is the real one.

**The context (`prefix`/`suffix`) passes the rule once — before the length
trim, not after.** Beresta trims context to 32 characters after collapsing, and
a slice exactly the length of the limit may legitimately begin or end with a
space: it ended up there because of the trim, it was not the edge of the string.
Stripping it yields context one character shorter than advertised.

**The storage rule is not the search rule.** Beresta locates a place in the book
by a quote with whitespace removed ENTIRELY (measured: removing all whitespace
finds 278 anchors out of 287, collapsing finds 41 out of 287, because joining
section text places adjacent paragraphs flush against each other). But the quote
is stored and displayed with whitespace collapsed: without it, a note would
receive words fused together. Two rules, two roles — and everything about them
is shared except whitespace.

Records written before the `v35_stored_quote_rule` migration were normalized by
it once. Anchors are unharmed: they are resolved by the search rule, for which a
raw and a normalized quote are the same string.

#### `annotation.chapterPath`

A JSON array of strings: table-of-contents headings from the TOP level down,
the last one being the place where the excerpt sits.

```json
["Part I. The Machinery", "Chapter 7. Attention, memory, and sleep", "7.1.1 Night"]
```

**An array rather than one string** — because one string forces you to pick a
level, and the level picked is the top one: an excerpt from "7.1.1" ends up
captioned with the name of chapter 7, or of part I altogether. This is not a
hypothesis: it is exactly what Beresta's import did before v32 — it took
Calibre's `toc_family_titles.first` and put it into `pageLabel`. In the
owner's library a path is present on 287 records out of 291, deeper than one
level on 171, and four levels deep on 39.

**Why not a separator-joined string** ("Part I > Chapter 7"): the first
heading that contains the separator — and every book has one — splits into two
levels, and there is nothing left to restore the original division from.

**Beresta's writing rules:** headings are trimmed of surrounding whitespace;
no level is an empty heading (dropping one would promote every level below it
and caption the excerpt with the wrong place); an empty path is not written at
all — `NULL` instead of `[]`, so that absence has only one spelling.

**What the database does NOT guarantee.** Everything above is a rule about OUR
writing, not a constraint of the file: the column is plain TEXT, and a
third-party writer may put anything there, including an empty heading, an
empty array, or something that is not an array at all. Beresta reads such a
string with a loud refusal (the path does not parse), but that does not take
the excerpt with it: quote, own thought, and color read as usual. The only
thing verifiable here by opening the database is that our own rows obey the
rule.

**Nobody checks the path against the book's table of contents.** The path is a
snapshot taken when the row was written; replace the book file with another
edition whose contents differ and the path stays as it was, exactly as the
quote does. It is refreshed by whoever writes the excerpt again (the Calibre
import, on every run), not by reading.

**Search does not cover the path.** `annotation.searchText` (migration v29)
joins the quote, the own thought, and the topic; the path is not part of it,
and a query by chapter name will find no excerpts. The reason is not laziness:
changing the formula means reindexing the whole database rather than editing
code, and it should be done when search by chapter is actually asked for — the
1.0 compatibility rule allows exactly that (see "Relationship with full-text
search").

#### `annotation.sortIndex`

A string like `00003#.000002.000004.000068.000001#0000000222`: three fields
separated by `#` — the document's section number (5 digits), the path to the
node inside that section, and the offset within that node (10 digits). The
path is the sequence of steps down the document tree (for EPUB, the numeric
steps of the CFI), each step written as `.` followed by 6 digits.

**`bookmark.sortIndex` uses the same format and the same formula** (migration
v31): one book's bookmarks and excerpts fall into a single "in book order"
without any conversion, because one and the same rule computes the key for both.

The leading zeros are not decorative — they are what makes lexicographic
string comparison (`ORDER BY sortIndex` in SQL, plain string comparison in
any language) match numeric comparison, without the reading code having to
parse the value. Zotero uses the same trick for sorting annotations.

The path is required; the offset alone is not enough. An offset is a position
inside ONE text node, not inside the section, and it is zero for every
highlight that starts at the beginning of its paragraph (212 of 287 in the
reference library) — such highlights are indistinguishable by offset even
though they sit in different places in the book.

The separators are chosen so that a path that is a prefix of another sorts
before its extension: `.` (0x2E) is below any digit, and `#` (0x23) is below
`.`. An empty path (an annotation that has none) puts the entry at the start
of its section.

**Each field's width is a hard limit, not a hint.** Leading zeros only work
while ALL keys are the same length: strings of different lengths compare
digits of different significance, and a single over-wide value breaks the
order of the whole book. The fields therefore have upper bounds, and those
bounds are part of the format:

| Field | Width | Allowed values |
|---|---|---|
| section number | 5 digits | 0…99,999 |
| path step | 6 digits | 0…999,999 |
| offset | 10 digits | 0…9,999,999,999 |

No field admits negative values. A value outside the bound means no key
exists for that position: writing a truncated or wrapped number instead is
not allowed — it silently files the highlight in the wrong place (and for
values that are multiples of 2³², at the very start of the book). Our
implementation refuses to build the key and says so; a third-party writer is
expected to do the same rather than squeeze the number into the width.

The bounds are generous: an EPUB with 100,000 sections, a node with a million
children, or a paragraph of ten billion characters has bigger problems than
highlight ordering.

**Where both values come from.** The section number is the zero-based index of
the `<itemref>` in the package document's `<spine>`; the path is the CFI steps
AFTER `!`, that is, counted from the children of `<html>`. Both are derived by
a single method — `Annotation.makeSortIndex(cfi:)` — from the very CFI string
stored in `annotation.position`. This is a requirement, not an implementation
detail: as long as the key and the anchor are computed in two places they
drift apart, and "book order" stops matching the book. Highlights created by
the reader and highlights brought in by import must land in one order; in the
owner's database they diverged at the very first path step (2 against 4), and
everything imported sorted before everything created in every section.

**The `/6` invariant.** The spine step of a full anchor is always `/6/N`:
`<spine>` is the third element child of `<package>` (the EPUB 3.3 content model
fixes the order `metadata` → `manifest` → `spine`, and everything optional
comes after), and `<spine>` contains nothing but `itemref`, so the k-th
`itemref` sits at step `(k + 1) * 2`. Verified across the owner's whole
library: 1433 books out of 1433, no exceptions. Separately: the engine does
not hard-code `/6` — it takes the index of `<spine>` from the OPF itself. So a
malformed OPF breaks our import but not the engine, and they diverge silently.

#### `annotation.anchorFileSHA256` and anchor refinement

The anchor of an annotation imported from Calibre is assembled out of the parts
Calibre itself stores: the local start CFI, the local end CFI, and a section
index. Two things are missing from those parts, and both can only be taken from
the BOOK FILE itself:

1. **Calibre counts the offset over the element's combined text**, while a CFI
   addresses one separate text chunk between element children. Where a paragraph
   is split by a nested `<em>` or `<a>`, the offset overshoots the first chunk
   and the anchor cannot be resolved at all. Laying the offset out across the
   chunks requires their lengths — that is, reading the section document.
2. **The section index goes stale** when the book file is replaced with another
   edition: the spine is rebuilt and the index leads into a different chapter.
   The right section can be found again by the stored quote — again by reading
   the file.

A schema migration can do neither: the migrator has no access to the blob store
and must not have one (it is built statically and runs against in-memory
databases too, where no store exists). So migration v13 fixes the anchor's
format, while a separate background pass in the application "refines" it into
place after launch — and `anchorFileSHA256` is that pass's entire memory.

What the value means: **the hash of the book file the anchor has already been
checked against.** Not a reference to content, but a record of work done. That
single column yields the three properties the pass must have:

- **resumability** — a pass interrupted midway continues at the next launch from
  the row it did not reach;
- **run-once** — refined anchors are not redone: the hash equals `fileSHA256`,
  so the row is never picked up again;
- **redo on file replacement** — replace the book with another edition,
  `fileSHA256` changes, the hashes diverge, and the anchor is checked anew.

Only two places write the value: the refinement pass itself and the Calibre
import (which assembles the anchor with the file already open and has no reason
to leave work behind). Migration v14 adds the column and leaves it empty: empty
means "the anchor has never been checked against the book file", which is true
for every row stored before it.

Third-party code may read the column as "this anchor was verified against that
file", but is not required to maintain it: it affects neither the meaning of
`position` nor that of `sortIndex`.

On the owner's live data (287 annotations, 8 books) refinement rewrites 61
anchors out of 287, after which the engine resolves and text-matches 281 anchors
instead of 224.

#### `book.sortTitle`

The title sort key is not "the title in lower case" but a purpose-built string
for which **plain byte comparison** (`ORDER BY book.sortTitle`, i.e. `BINARY` in
SQLite) yields the order a reader expects from a list of books. No custom
collation is registered anywhere: it would be available to this application
only, while the format must be readable by any tool. The same technique, for the
same reason, is used by `annotation.sortIndex` and `bookCollection.sortKey`.

The source value is Calibre's `sort` field (where the source has already moved
leading articles: "The Hobbit" → "Hobbit, The"), falling back to the title
itself. The key is built from it in three steps
(`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Database/BookSortKey.swift`):

1. **Lower case and «ё» → «е»** — the very same `SearchNormalizer.normalize`
   that normalizes text for full-text search (see "Relationship with full-text
   search"). Without the fold, «ё» (U+0451) sits AFTER «я» (U+044F) in Unicode,
   so a title starting with «Ё» is exiled past the whole Russian alphabet.
2. **Dropping leading quotes, brackets, and spaces** (`" ' « » „ “ ” ‘ ’ ( [
   {`) — they sort below any letter, so a quoted title would jump ahead of the
   entire alphabet. Only the start of the string is trimmed. A title consisting
   solely of such marks keeps them (otherwise several such books would become
   indistinguishable); a title of nothing but spaces yields an empty key.
3. **Left-padding numbers with zeros to 20 digits** — otherwise "Volume 10"
   precedes "Volume 2". Every run of ASCII digits is its own fixed-width field;
   leading zeros are insignificant ("007" and "7" produce the same key). The
   width of 20 is exactly the width of the maximum unsigned 64-bit integer, so
   any number a third-party consumer can parse at all fits the field intact (in
   the reference library the longest number is 13 digits, an ISBN-13).

**A number longer than 20 digits** is encoded as `:` + its length (encoded by
the same rule) + the digits themselves. The colon (0x3A) sits immediately after
digit "9" (0x39) and before any letter, so such a number outranks any
regular-width number yet stays inside the "numeric" stretch of the order; two
oversized numbers compare by length first, then by digits. This is a correctness
requirement, not a flourish: padding "to a width" without such a branch produces
the wrong order for longer numbers (the string "100" is lexicographically less
than the string "99").

The fill rule is part of the format, not an application detail: any tool that
WRITES to this database must store a key built by these rules in `sortTitle`,
otherwise its books will land in the wrong place in the list. The key is
idempotent — running it through the rule a second time changes nothing.

Existing databases have the column recomputed by migration `v17_sort_title_key`.
On the owner's library (1555 books) it changes the key of 183 books; the
relative order changes for 2513 pairs of books out of 1,208,235.

#### `bookCollection.sortKey` and `collection.defaultSort`

Task 13 (`docs/specs/20260806-reading-lists-design.md`). The owner wanted
"reading lists" — a separate entity was decided against: a list is just an
ordinary collection with `collection.defaultSort = 'manual'`. The flag lives
in a setting, not in the structure.

**Key format** — a fractional index, following
[`rocicorp/fractional-indexing`](https://github.com/rocicorp/fractional-indexing)
(CC0, David Greenspan's algorithm); the implementation is
`FractionalIndex.swift`. Alphabet
`0123456789ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz` (base62,
characters in ascending ASCII code order — this is exactly why byte-wise
string comparison matches numeric comparison of the fractions). A key is a
head (`A`…`Z` or `a`…`z`, encoding the integer part's length: `Z`/`a` is
shortest, `A`/`z` is longest) plus an integer part plus an optional
fractional part. Invariant: a key's fractional part never ends in the
alphabet's first character (`"a1"` and `"a10"` would otherwise encode the
same fraction as different strings). Examples: the first key of an empty
collection is `"a0"`; appending further — `"a1"`, `"a2"`, … `"az"`, `"b00"`,
`"b01"`; between `"a0"` and `"a1"` — `"a0V"`.

**Why a string, not an integer.** An integer order would require
renumbering the tail of the list on every mid-list insert — on sync that is
an avalanche of edits that then have to be merged across devices; this is
exactly why Zotero's order column (`collectionItems.orderIndex`) has sat
unused since 2007 — the maintainers name synchronization as the reason
outright. A fractional key changes one field of one row:
`BookRepository.moveBook(_:in:after:)` computes a new key between two
neighbors and rewrites the `sortKey` of EXACTLY one `bookCollection` row.

**Read contract for `defaultSort = 'manual'`** (part of the open schema —
any third-party reader must know it; for `defaultSort = 'title'` the order is
plain `ORDER BY book.sortTitle`, `sortKey` not involved at all — see
"`collection.defaultSort`" below):

```sql
SELECT book.* FROM book
JOIN bookCollection ON bookCollection.bookId = book.id
WHERE bookCollection.collectionId = ? AND bookCollection.deletedAt IS NULL
ORDER BY bookCollection.sortKey, book.uuid;
```

String comparison is byte-wise (SQLite's default, `BINARY`; not `NOCASE` —
the key's alphabet has both upper- and lower-case letters, and a
case-insensitive comparison would break the order). The second sort key,
`book.uuid`, is mandatory: it breaks the tie when TWO DEVICES INDEPENDENTLY
ASSIGN THE SAME `sortKey` TO DIFFERENT BOOKS (for example, both append a
book to the end of a short list without knowing about each other — not a
rare event under sync). Without the `book.uuid` tiebreak, order under
colliding keys would be non-deterministic: the database gives no guarantee
of stable row order for equal `ORDER BY` values, so two runs (or two
devices) could see different results on the same data. `book.id` doesn't
work as a tiebreak — it's local to a given database.

**This task's scope is a short queue (five to ten books), not a long list.**
Jitter on key generation is deliberately not implemented — it addresses the
problem of SEVERAL DEVICES inserting at the same point of a LARGE list at
the same time, disproportionate complexity for this scale.

**The "two devices produced the same `sortKey`" conflict on READ** is always
resolved by the rule above (the `book.uuid` tiebreak) — deterministically,
no matter how many consecutive rows share a `sortKey`. **On WRITE** (circle
of revisions 1, task 13 review) — `BookRepository.moveBook` repairs a PAIRWISE
collision itself: if both neighbors of the target position carry the same
`sortKey` (typically both independently appended a book to the end of a
short list on different devices), the RIGHT neighbor's key is regenerated
first — using its own next neighbor as the new upper bound, one extra field
write — and only then is the moved book's key computed. This is exactly what
the spec (§8, "repair on collision") asks of the writing side: without
repair, that insertion point would be unreachable FOREVER (the collision is
deterministic and reproduces on every attempt), and there is no key
regeneration affordance in the UI. A meaningful error
(`BookRepositoryError.duplicateSortKeyNeighbors`) remains only for the rare
CASCADING case — three or more consecutive rows sharing the same `sortKey`
(several independent sync merges in a row with no read in between): a single
repair of the right neighbor doesn't free up room, and unwinding the whole
chain automatically is out of scope ("don't scope-creep"); `FractionalIndex.
between` is never called in that case (its contract forbids equal
neighbors) — the code fails with an explicit error instead of crashing.

**Key length has no cap — and that's fine.** Repeatedly inserting at the
same point (e.g. a book dragged back to the same corner of a short queue
over and over) lengthens `sortKey` by roughly one character per six
insertions at that point — linearly, with no ceiling. Rebalancing (rewriting
every key in a collection from scratch), which the spec allows as a manual
action for pathologically long keys, is not implemented in this task — at a
"five to ten books" scale it's disproportionate complexity. The one hard
requirement is that the implementation must not CRASH from this growth. The
Swift port (`FractionalIndex.midpoint`) is iterative, not recursive:
computation depth doesn't grow with key length, so even thousands of
consecutive insertions at one point (unreachable at this task's scale) don't
exhaust the stack.

**`collection.defaultSort`** defaults to `'title'`, not `'manual'` as the
spec's first draft literally proposed: a collection becomes a reading list
by the owner EXPLICITLY flipping this field (`BookRepository.
setDefaultSort(_:for:)`), not silently for every new shelf. `'manual'` turns
on manual order; the spec's other values (`author`/`added`/`published`/
`rating`) are not in the schema yet — only `manual`/`title`, the minimum
task 13 needs. `BookRepository.books(inCollection:)` only reads by the rule
above for `.manual`; for `.title` (the default) it reads by `book.sortTitle`,
the same order as the plain library list.

This column's vocabulary is CLOSED: `manual` and `title`, in exactly that
spelling and case. The database does not enforce it (see "Closed value sets"
— the schema has exactly one CHECK, and it is not this one), so anything can
be written here — but a third value does not mean "some order or other".
Reading such a collection FAILS instead of falling back to the default:
falling back to `title` would show a reading list alphabetically, silently
replacing the owner's manual order, and any drag inside it would then write a
`sortKey` nobody can see. A third-party writer that needs its own sort
criterion must keep it on its own side, not in this column.

**The `sortKey`/`addedAt` placeholder trap — circle of revisions 1 (review,
important finding 5).** Both columns are declared `NOT NULL` with a default
of `''` (migration v11 cannot give every row its own unique value on `ALTER
TABLE ADD COLUMN`, unlike `uuid` in v6 — a shared placeholder default is the
only path available here, the same technique already used for `changeLog.
origin`/`setting.deviceId`). A plain insert of a book-collection link via raw
SQL WITHOUT going through `BerestaCore` — e.g. `INSERT INTO bookCollection
(bookId, collectionId) VALUES (?, ?)`, exactly what the project itself did
before task 13, and something any third-party writer of this open schema is
entitled to do — legally leaves a row with `sortKey = ''`/`addedAt = ''`. An
empty string is neither a valid fractional key (`FractionalIndex` treats it
as corrupt) nor a valid ISO 8601 date. `BerestaCore` defends against this on
its own side (`BookRepository.normalizedSortKey` treats an empty `sortKey`
as absent when computing neighbors; operations that only need "does this
link already exist" use `EXISTS` and never decode `addedAt`) — but
third-party code reading or writing this schema directly must either always
supply `sortKey`/`addedAt` explicitly on insert, or assume up front that an
empty string in these columns means "not yet assigned," not a real
fractional key or a real date.

**The same placeholder in `collection.createdAt`/`collection.modifiedAt`.**
Both columns are declared `NOT NULL DEFAULT ''` for the same reason and by the
same migration v11, and a plain insert of a shelf via raw SQL (`INSERT INTO
collection (name, sortOrder) VALUES (?, ?)`) leaves an empty string in them
just the same. The difference is the price: for our code an empty `sortKey`
means "not yet assigned", whereas an empty date does not parse at all, and any
read that decodes a WHOLE collection row answers such a row with a date
parsing error. In `BerestaCore` that read is
`BookRepository.collections(of:)` — the only remaining path of this kind;
neighboring operations (`setDefaultSort`, `renameCollection`) deliberately
patch their own column with a targeted `UPDATE` instead of decoding the whole
row, and never touch the placeholder.

The practical conclusion for third-party code is the same but stricter: when
creating a shelf directly, fill `createdAt`/`modifiedAt` with real ISO 8601
dates. An empty string here is not "not yet assigned" — it is a row some
readers will not be able to read at all.

**A foreign key value is refused — not a crash, and not a silent repair
(task 17).** The empty placeholder isn't the only thing that can sit in a
column of an open schema: a third-party writer is entitled to leave
anything there — a truncated key, a key from a DIFFERENT ordering
implementation (LexoRank, say), plain garbage. Before task 17 the answer to
such a value was the process dying on a signal (the `precondition` inside
`FractionalIndex` is alive in release builds too), and not only while
dragging: the "append to end" key is computed from the largest existing key
in the collection, so a foreign row entered the arithmetic during an
ordinary "add a book to a shelf" and during import as well. The key's shape
is now checked explicitly (`FractionalIndex.isValidKey`) and the operation
REFUSES with a meaningful error (`BookRepository.SortKeyError.
corruptSortKey`, carrying the collection and the offending value). Exactly
what the arithmetic relies on is checked: a head from `A`…`Z`/`a`…`z`; an
integer-part length, as declared by the head, no greater than the key
itself; every digit drawn from the base62 alphabet; a fractional part that
doesn't end in the alphabet's first character; and the key not being the
smallest integer with no fractional part.

There is no automatic renumbering here, and none is planned: silently
rewriting someone else's data on read is worse than an honest refusal.
Repairing corrupt keys is a separate, explicit action by the owner, not a
side effect of opening a shelf. The practical consequence for third-party
code: writing `sortKey` is allowed, but write either a key of this format
or leave the empty placeholder — an arbitrary string makes the shelf
un-reorderable until someone repairs it.

#### `annotation.color` and `annotation.colorHex`

The highlight color is stored TWICE — as a name (`color`, e.g. `"yellow"`)
and as a value (`colorHex`, e.g. `"#ffd54f"`) — and always in sync: the
value is always derived from the name by the same formula
(`AnnotationColor.hex(for:)`,
`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Models/Annotation.swift`).

**Precedence: `color`.** The name is the stored value; `colorHex` is its
derivative. If the pair has drifted apart, `color` is right and `colorHex`
should be recomputed with the formula below; the palette is published right
here, so any program can recompute it, not only ours. The rule is stated
