because the database does NOT enforce the pair — see the next paragraph.

**What the database does not guarantee.** The pair cannot drift apart by
construction on THREE independent paths that create or change a record in OUR
code (listed below), but that is a property of Beresta's code, not of the
file's shape: the schema holds no `CHECK` and no trigger keeping the pair
together. A third-party writer may store `color = 'green'` alongside
`colorHex = '#000000'`, and the database will accept it. Our own reader will
not notice such a row — `Annotation.init(from:)` re-derives the value from the
name and never reads the stored column at all — while a third-party reader
reads exactly that column and will see a different color.

The absence of a database check here is deliberate. The formula assigns an
unfamiliar name a fallback value (yellow), so a trigger demanding agreement
with the formula would FORBID a third-party tool from adding its own color
with its own true value — it would be forced to store our fallback instead of
the truth. For an open format that is worse than drift: the constraint would
work against the very people the format is opened for. Hence a read rule
here, not a write ban.

Migration v20 (`v20_annotation_color_pairing`) repairs ONE kind of drift, and
exactly once: a row where `color` is set but `colorHex` is empty gets its
value filled in by the formula. This is the hole left by migration v9 — it
only converted rows with `color LIKE '#%'`, so a row where `color` already
held a name was left without a value. A pair where `colorHex` is set and does
NOT match the formula is left alone by v20: that is a foreign write with
explicit intent, and repairing foreign data is not a migration's business.

The three paths on which drift is excluded by construction (review cycle 1:
the first version of this section made the same claim, but it only held for
one of the three paths — review reproduced the drift on the other two against
that exact version of the code):
1. **construction** — `Annotation`'s initializer has no separate parameter
   for the value; `colorHex` is derived from the name that was passed in;
2. **mutating an already-built record** — `colorHex` is declared
   `public private(set)`: assigning it directly (`annotation.colorHex =
   "…"`) is impossible from ANYWHERE outside `Annotation.swift` itself —
   a build error, not something UI or repository code could do by
   accident. The only way to change the color is through `color` (which
   stays freely mutable — the one path a future "change highlight color"
   UI needs), and `colorHex` follows it automatically via `didSet`;
3. **decoding from an external representation** (a database row through
   `FetchableRecord`, or raw JSON through `Decodable`) — `Annotation`
   does not rely on Swift's synthesized `init(from:)`: its own
   `init(from decoder:)` does NOT read `colorHex` from the source at
   all; it derives it from the decoded `color` with the same formula.
   The source cannot hand it a mismatched pair — whatever sits under the
   `colorHex` key simply goes unused.

The two forms serve DIFFERENT consumers. The appearance theme colors
highlights by name — if the database held only the finished value, the
theme could not override it. Third-party code reading this open database
format directly, unaware of our name palette, needs the value — if the
database held only the name, such code would see a word instead of a color
and would not be able to render the highlight at all.

The palette (`AnnotationColor.palette`) holds eight names:

| Name | Value | Origin | In the picker row |
|---|---|---|---|
| `yellow` | `#ffd54f` | Calibre (also the default value) | yes |
| `red` | `#e57373` | Calibre | yes |
| `green` | `#7cb342` | Calibre | yes |
| `blue` | `#4a90d9` | Calibre | yes |
| `purple` | `#ba68c8` | Calibre | yes |
| `magenta` | `#e56eee` | Zotero | yes |
| `orange` | `#f19837` | Zotero | no, behind the "more" button |
| `gray` | `#aaaaaa` | Zotero | no, behind the "more" button |

The first five names keep the values of CALIBRE'S BUILT-IN PALETTE rather
than Zotero's reference values, even though the names coincide: migration v9
already filled 287 live rows in the owner's database with those values, and
changing them after the fact would disagree with the migrated rows. The last
three names never occur in Calibre, so there is nothing to migrate — they
take Zotero's reference values (`docs/specs/20260806-theming.md`, §7.2).

**Eight in storage, six in the interface.** Storage accepts all eight names
today, so that a highlight arriving from Zotero or from another device does
not lose its color name. The picker row in the reader shows six
(`AnnotationColor.interfaceNames`); `orange` and `gray` will move behind a
"more" button that does not exist in the first wave. The list only ever grows
by ADDING pairs.

**The reverse lookup (value → name) breaks ties by display order.** All
palette values are distinct today, so the question does not arise — but the
format allows extending the palette by adding a pair, including one whose
value already exists, and then a single value would have two names. The one
that comes EARLIER in the display order (`AnnotationColor.order`) wins. The
rule is stated because "either one" is not acceptable here: without it the
answer would depend on hash-table iteration order, i.e. change from run to
run, the same highlight would be painted differently, and a migration
translating values into names would write DIFFERENT names for identical rows
on two devices.

`AnnotationColor.hex(for:)` tells apart THREE kinds of unfamiliar input
rather than treating them as one:

- `nil` (a highlight with no color at all) — there was no value, and none
  gets invented: `colorHex` is `nil` too;
- a string shaped like `#rrggbb` (a value outside the palette that is
  already a finished value, not a name — exactly what migration v9 leaves
  in the `color` column, see below) — passed through unchanged, not
  replaced with the fallback;
- any other unfamiliar NAME (a custom color chosen as an arbitrary word
  rather than its own value, or a non-color Calibre style — see below) is
  kept in `color` as-is, and `colorHex` gets the fallback default value:
  there is still nothing to guess the real color from for an unfamiliar
  name.

Final branch review: before this fix the function did not distinguish a
`#rrggbb`-shaped string or `nil` from an unfamiliar name and replaced both
with the fallback yellow — which disagreed with what migration v9 produces
for exactly these two cases (see below), meaning reading such a row through
the model, followed by any write-back (`update(db)` writes the WHOLE record,
not just the changed field — re-import correcting a quote does exactly that),
would have erased the original value. The seam is covered by
`MigrationsTests.migratedColorRowsRoundTripThroughModelUnchanged`: a row
produced by the migration is read through `Annotation` and written back
unchanged.

`CalibreImporter` passes the source's color name into `color` unchanged.
Before migration v9, the importer converted the name into a value right
before writing, using its own internal table of the same five colors —
and anything not in that table collapsed into the default yellow: the
original name was lost for good, and `color` carried a finished value
instead of a name. Calibre stores more than color in that same style
field — also decoration (a wavy underline, a strikethrough:
`style.kind == "decoration"`, `style.which` is `"wavy"`/`"strikeout"`).
There is no separate annotation type for decoration in the schema yet.
`CalibreImporter` keeps the decoration name in `color` the same way it
keeps an unfamiliar color name (it is not discarded), but counts such
highlights separately in the import report
(`CalibreImportReport.annotationsWithDecorationStyle`) so the signal is
not lost silently.

Migration v9 (`Migrations.swift`, `v9_annotation_color`) converts rows
already in the database: before it, `color` carried a finished value — the
new `colorHex` column gets that value as-is, and `color` is converted to a
name by a reverse lookup in the palette. Verified on a copy of the owner's
production database: all 287 live highlights hold palette values (217
`blue`, 41 `green`, 25 `yellow`, 2 each of `red` and `purple`) — the
migration converts every single one, with not a single row left with an
empty `colorHex`.

#### `changeLog.hlc`

A Hybrid Logical Clock stamp: physical time plus a counter, so that changes
happening within the same millisecond (or after the system clock is turned
back) still have a strict order. Format:

```
<12 hex digits of milliseconds>-<4 hex digits of counter>-<deviceId>
```

Example: `019fd1cb7600-0003-mac-a1b2c3`. The first component is Unix time in
milliseconds, written as a 12-digit hexadecimal number (zero-padded); the
second is a counter that increases on a physical-time collision (same
millisecond, or time moved backward), also zero-padded; the third is the
identifier of the device that issued the stamp (needed to tell apart stamps
issued by different devices within the same millisecond). As with
`sortIndex`, the leading zeros in the first two components turn
lexicographic string comparison into chronological order: `changeLog` sorts
by `hlc` with a plain `ORDER BY`, no parsing required.

**The width of the first two components is a hard ceiling, not merely a
minimum for printing.** The first component cannot exceed `0xFFFFFFFFFFFF`
(12 hex digits), the second cannot exceed `0xFFFF` (4 hex digits): a value
wider than that would grow the string past the declared width, and
lexicographic order for that stamp would stop matching chronological order
(strings of different lengths would compare digits of different
significance — a short but "later" string would lose to a long "earlier"
one). This is not hypothetical: the receiver of a stamp from another device
(`HybridLogicalClock.next(after:observing:physical:deviceId:)`) must check
this bound while parsing the foreign string — nothing guarantees it before
parsing, unlike the local stamp the code itself prints. A stamp that
violates the bound fails to parse and takes no part in computing the next
stamp.

Clock state (the last stamp issued per device) is stored in the
`changeLogClock` table rather than recomputed from the current time on every
run — otherwise the monotonicity guarantee would be lost across app restarts
or when the system clock is turned back.

**Counter overflow inside a batch write.** A batch write
(`ChangeLog.record(_:origin:)` with an array of changes) issues every stamp
in the batch inside one transaction. Physical time is re-read for EVERY
change in the batch, not once before the loop — thanks to that, batch stamps
don't lag behind real wall-clock time, and in practice (measured on a batch
of 20,000 changes) the counter never goes past a few dozen within any single
millisecond, nowhere near the `0xFFFF` limit. Had physical time been read
once before the loop (an earlier version of this code), a large enough batch
(importing 1,555 books at several fields per book — tens of thousands of
changes) would have made overflow a routine scenario rather than a rare edge
case.

If the counter overflows anyway (chiefly via receiving a foreign stamp,
`next(after:observing:physical:deviceId:)`, where `remote` can contend for
the counter against an already near-exhausted local one), the clock THROWS
(`HybridLogicalClock.OverflowError`) rather than carrying the overflow into
the time field. An earlier version carried it: added 1 to the millisecond
without checking the upper bound (`maxEncodableMilliseconds`, 12 hex digits)
— but `remote`, coming from another device, can legitimately sit RIGHT AT
that bound (`parseComponents` accepts such a value as valid, not oversized).
Carrying in that case added one to an already-maximal millisecond and grew
the field to 13 hex digits — the very format violation that the parse-time
check (above) closed for PARSING foreign stamps, while the carry reopened it
for COMPUTING our own. Throwing rather than carrying is the same choice made
by the reference HLC implementation this stamp format is built on
(`Timestamp.OverflowError` in James Long's `crdt-example-app`): the clock
does not fake physical time just to produce a stamp at any cost — it leaves
the decision of what to do next to the caller, which for `ChangeLog.record`
means rolling back the whole batch transaction, the same way any other
failure inside it is handled. Field width therefore never exceeds what's
declared, unconditionally rather than only "for a batch of any size": the
clock either issues a stamp strictly within the format's bounds, or it
issues none at all.

#### `changeLog.origin`

Who made the edit. One of three shapes:

- `user` — an edit made through the app's UI (the default value);
- `import:<kind>:<instance>` — an edit made by an import, e.g.
  `import:calibre:library-1` (`kind` is the kind of source, `instance` is
  the specific instance, e.g. a particular Calibre library). Kinds today:
  `calibre` — an import from a Calibre library; `snapshot` — the initial
  snapshot of a library created before the journal existed (`instance` is the
  identifier of the device that took it). The list is open: an unfamiliar kind
  is not a data error and should be preserved and passed on as is;
- `module:<id>` — an edit made by a module (e.g. a language model), e.g.
  `module:annotator`.

The string is built by joining parts with `:` (`ChangeOrigin.encoded` in
code). The database checks the value's shape with a `CHECK` constraint (one
of the three templates above) — this catches typos and foreign values coming
from raw SQL writes that bypass `ChangeLog.record`, but it does not look
inside `kind`/`instance`/`id`: SQL cannot reference application code.

**The rule for parsing the string back** (for third-party code reading the
database directly, not through `BerestaCore`) depends on the tag before the
first `:`:

1. Split the string on the first `:` — this yields `tag` and, if the tag
   isn't `user`, a remainder.
2. If `tag == "import"` — split the remainder ONE MORE TIME on its first
   `:`: the first part is `kind`, the second is the entire tail AS-IS, with
   no further splitting — that is `instance` (it may contain `:`).
3. If `tag == "module"` — the remainder is NOT split again: the whole thing
   is `id` (it may contain `:`). Applying step 2's rule here is a mistake:
   `"module:llm:gpt-4"` would parse as `kind = "llm"` (which doesn't exist
   for `module`) instead of `id = "llm:gpt-4"`.

So: at most two splits, and the second one (when it happens at all) applies
only to `import`; in every case the last field is a greedy remainder, not a
token between delimiters. The rule is unambiguous ONLY because `kind` cannot
contain `:` — that guarantee comes from the closed `ImportKind` enum on the
Swift side, not from anything derivable from the string itself. Without it,
`("kind": "a:b", "instance": "c")` and `("kind": "a", "instance": "b:c")`
would both produce the same string `import:a:b:c` — the encoding would be
ambiguous. `instance` and `id`, unlike `kind`, are open-ended strings: a
colon inside either breaks nothing precisely because both are taken as a
greedy remainder, not a token.

The import source kind (`kind`) is a closed list — currently `calibre` is
the only value; a new import source is added as a new case of the
`ImportKind` enum in code (a code change, not a data change — consistent
with the 0.x schema status). The module identifier (`id`) and the source
instance (`instance`), by contrast, are open, unconstrained strings.

Third-party code that encounters a value whose tag (the part before the
first `:`) is not one of the three listed — `user`/`import`/`module` —
should treat it as a data error, the same as `readStatus` below: the set of
tags is fixed by this schema for version 0.x. A specific `kind` value inside
`import:kind:…`, or `id` inside `module:id`, that the reading code doesn't
recognize is a different matter, not a data error: both sets are open-ended
and expected to grow (new import sources, new modules), so a record with an
unfamiliar but well-formed `kind`/`id` should be kept and passed through
as-is, not discarded.

#### `changeLog.rowUUID` for many-to-many links

A link row (`bookTag`, `bookAuthor`, `bookCollection`, `annotationTag`) has
no `uuid` of its own: its primary key is a pair of LOCAL auto-increments
(`bookId`+`tagId` and so on), and an auto-increment means different rows on
different devices for the same number. For such rows `changeLog.rowUUID` is
not a stored value but a COMPUTED one: `LinkRowUUID.make(left, right)` in
code
(`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Database/LinkRowUUID.swift`),
whose result is the same on any device for the same pair of link ends,
regardless of the order they were passed in. A random `uuid` of its own
wouldn't work here: two devices that independently attach the same tag to
the same book would each mint a different random identifier for what is the
same link — sync would see two different facts instead of one, and merging
edits cannot resolve that (both rows are "real"; there is nothing to
merge).

The result's format is a length prefix before each (sorted) end of the
pair, with no delimiter between the parts:

```
<byte length of left in UTF-8>:<left><byte length of right in UTF-8>:<right>
```

where `left`/`right` is the pair of original `left` and `right` sorted in
ascending order (the order of `make`'s arguments doesn't matter — the
result is the same either way). For example, for
`book.uuid = "22222222-2222-2222-2222-222222222222"` and
`tag.uuid = "11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111"`:

```
36:11111111-1111-1111-1111-11111111111136:22222222-2222-2222-2222-222222222222
```

Joining by length, not by a delimiter, is deliberate. Task 5 of the
`20260806-1b-format-fixes` plan (`changeLog.origin` above) found that
joining strings with `:` is not
injective if `:` can appear INSIDE a joined part (`("a:b", "c")` and
`("a", "b:c")` produce the same string) — that case was closed with a
closed `ImportKind` enum on the Swift side, but `left`/`right` here are
arbitrary `String` values, not a closed list, so that fix doesn't transfer.
A run of decimal digits before `:` cannot be confused with a part's content
(the digit run itself never contains `:`), and right after the declared
length exactly that many bytes are consumed next, whatever they contain,
including `:` and the empty string — the set of inputs is recoverable from
the result unambiguously for ANY content of the parts, not just today's
special case of "both ends are UUIDs without colons".

The format is guaranteed never to collide with a plain `rowUUID`: a string
shaped like `"36:<uuid>36:<uuid>"` fails `UUID(uuidString:)` and doesn't
match a plain row's 36-character `uuid` in length either — the conflict
resolver cannot mistake a link edit for a book or tag edit.

**Wired in on 20260824** (task 1 of the `20260824-синхронизация` plan). The
`bookTag`, `bookAuthor` and `bookCollection` links write to the journal using
this address; `annotationTag` has no write path at all yet — tags on annotations
do not exist.

**The service field `bookUUID`** is the journal's second field absent from the
table schema. A book file, a bookmark, a reading and a reader decision know
their book only through the `bookId` column, which is a local autoincrement: it
never travels, so such a row would arrive on another device an orphan with
nowhere to go. The journal sends the book's address under the name `bookUUID` —
the same name the book snapshot already carries on an annotation
(`annotation.bookUUID` is a real schema column). One rule for every row: the
field `bookUUID` means "the book this row belongs to". It is sent only with a
full row write: a targeted edit does not need the parent — the row is already
in place.

**Moving contents during an edition merge is NOT transmitted** — and will not
be. A file, a link or an annotation has no way to say "I now belong to another
book" in the journal. Instead, the device that receives the merge tombstone
(`mergedIntoUUID`) performs the move LOCALLY — with the same code and the same
outcome (`RecordMerge.moveContents`). This is the same technique the project
already uses for annotation orphaning, layer clearing and the derived reading
status: a local consequence of a synced fact is produced on the spot rather
than shipped over the wire. If the surviving book has not arrived yet, the move
waits for the next exchange; the state "merged, contents still in place" is
legitimate and does not lie: the book is already hidden.

**Link membership is the `present` field**: `"1"` — the link exists, `"0"` —
it was removed. A separate field is required because removal is otherwise
inexpressible: the row is gone from the table, so there is nothing left to
speak of its disappearance — a tag removed on one device would come back from
another at the first exchange. A link's own fields (`position` for an author,
`sortKey`/`reason`/`reasonNote` for a shelf) travel as ordinary fields under
the same address.

`bookCollection` additionally has its own tombstone (`deletedAt`), which
travels as an ordinary column. The two ways of removal mean two different
facts: `present = "0"` — the book was taken off the shelf; `deletedAt` — the
book was deleted entirely and the shelf entry followed it.

#### `book.uuid`, `annotation.uuid`, and reference `uuid`s for imported records

On first insert of a new book or highlight coming from Calibre, and on
first insert of a new author/tag/collection (whether from import or
created by hand), `uuid` is not a random `UUID().uuidString` but a
COMPUTED one: `DeterministicUUID.make(kind:instance:key:)` in code
(`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Database/DeterministicUUID.swift`),
where `kind` is the source's kind for `book`/`annotation` (always
`"calibre"` today) or the reference's kind for `author`/`tag`/`collection`
(`"author"`/`"tag"`/`"collection"`) — open-ended in both cases; `instance`
is the `sourceLibraryUUID`/`libraryUUID` of the specific Calibre library
for `book`/`annotation`, or the empty string for references (they aren't
tied to one source — reused across every source in a given Beresta database
by name, see `book_on_source`/`name`'s uniqueness below); `key` is the
record's identifier IN THAT source (the book's `uuid`, or the highlight's
`annot_data.uuid`/`annot_id` from Calibre) for `book`/`annotation`, or the
`name` for references. The result is identical on any device that
independently imports the same record from the same library: without
this, two devices importing the same Calibre library would give the same
book (or the same author/tag/collection) different `uuid`s, edit merging
(by row field, not by a unique key) wouldn't resolve the resulting
duplicate, and the library would end up with two copies of every entity.

The hash input is joined the same length-prefixed way as
`changeLog.rowUUID` above (shared helper `LengthPrefixedJoin` in code,
`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Database/LengthPrefixedJoin.swift`),
for the same reason: naively joining `"\(kind):\(instance):\(key)"` is not
injective if `:` can appear inside `instance`/`key` (both are raw strings
from someone else's Calibre database, with no format guarantee) — and here
that wouldn't just confuse a journal row, it would collapse TWO DIFFERENT
BOOKS/ANNOTATIONS from the source into the same imported `uuid`. Unlike
`LinkRowUUID.make`, the parts here are NOT sorted — `kind`/`instance`/`key`
play different roles (source kind / source instance / record key), not two
interchangeable ends of one link.

##### The formula in full

This is a **version 5 UUID per RFC 4122** (SHA-1 over a namespace and a
name) — a standard function, not an invention of ours: it ships ready-made in
almost every language (`uuid.uuid5` in Python, `uuid.NewSHA1` in Go, the
`uuid/v5` package in JavaScript). The formula is published here in full, and
deliberately so: without it a third-party importer of THE SAME Calibre
library could not arrive at the same identifiers, would diverge from us on
every book, and would run into the unique index — meaning the promise of
portability would go unmet technically, not just rhetorically.

1. **Namespace** — fixed forever: `6f8a1e2c-4d3b-5a7e-9c1f-0b2d4e6a8c0f`.
   Its 16 RAW BYTES go into the hash, in the usual RFC 4122 order (most
   significant byte first) — not the text of its written form.
2. **Name** — a `LengthPrefixedJoin` of three parts IN THIS ORDER: `kind`,
   `instance`, `key`. Each part is preceded by its length IN UTF-8 BYTES and
   a `:`, with nothing separating the parts:
   `<len kind>:<kind><len instance>:<instance><len key>:<key>`.
   The length counts BYTES, not characters — for non-Latin names these are
   different numbers.
3. **Hash** — `SHA-1(namespace bytes ‖ name bytes in UTF-8)`, 20 bytes.
4. Take the **first 16 bytes** of the digest.
5. Set the version and variant bits:
   `byte[6] = (byte[6] & 0x0F) | 0x50`, `byte[8] = (byte[8] & 0x3F) | 0x80`.
6. Render canonically (`8-4-4-4-12`) in **UPPERCASE** — that is exactly how
   it is stored in the database. The case is part of the format, not an
   incidental detail: SQLite compares text bytewise, and the same string in
   lowercase will not match ours in a unique index.

**Worked examples.** An implementation that produces these identifiers from
this input is compatible with ours:

| `kind` | `instance` | `key` | Join (the name) | Result |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `calibre` | `11111111-1111-1111-1111-111111111111` | `22222222-2222-2222-2222-222222222222` | `7:calibre36:11111111-1111-1111-1111-11111111111136:22222222-2222-2222-2222-222222222222` | `6A15A908-3576-53B8-971C-0616DC29B519` |
| `tag` | *(empty)* | `фантастика` | `3:tag0:20:фантастика` | `4ECA9B51-12BC-5163-888D-064C92F2F2F1` |

The second example is chosen on purpose: `фантастика` is 10 characters and
20 BYTES, and the length in the join is 20. An implementation that counted
characters would get a different identifier and diverge from us on the very
first non-Latin tag. The same line also shows that an empty part is written
as `0:` rather than skipped.

The examples are pinned by the test
`DeterministicUUIDTests.publishedWorkedExamples`, so the document and the code
cannot drift apart silently. The values themselves were computed by an
independent implementation (`uuid.uuid5` in Python), not read back off our
own code.

**A third consumer of the same formula is the book file's row address**
(`bookFile.uuid`, migration v33): `kind` = `bookFile`, `instance` =
`book.uuid`, `key` = the same length-prefixed join of format and fingerprint.
The formula, its worked example, and the caveat about merges live in the
`bookFile` section above. There is no second identifier scheme in this format,
and none should be introduced.

**An already-existing row is never reissued a new `uuid` on the fly — but
`annotation` differs from the rest in how re-import idempotency works:**

The key used to find a row on re-import is SEPARATE from `uuid` for every
entity without exception: for `book` it is the pair
`sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceBookUUID` (see `book_on_source` above), for
`annotation` it is the pair `sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceAnnotationUUID` (see
`annotation_on_source`), for references it is the unique `name`. Changing the
`uuid`-generation scheme therefore doesn't break re-import idempotency by
itself: the new scheme applies on the fly only to rows FIRST seen after it,
and an existing row's `uuid` belongs to the row, not to the source.

Before migration v12, `annotation` was the one exception: its re-import
idempotency rested on `uuid` itself, so a row already imported under the old
scheme had to be MOVED onto the new deterministic identifier on the fly, at
every import. That asymmetry was expensive:

- a database that had been migrated but never re-imported was not ready for
  sync — its highlights still held Calibre's raw identifier, while a second
  device importing the same library with current code would produce the
  deterministic one, so the very same highlights would arrive as duplicates;
- as soon as a row that had arrived over sync appeared next to the old one,
  the move broke FOREVER: `UPDATE annotation SET uuid` hit a uniqueness
  violation on `annotation.uuid` (the target value was already taken by the
  arrived row), the error went into the import report one highlight at a time
  on every run, the duplicate stayed in the database, and the move stopped
  working for every other row as well. Verified by an executable run against
  a copy of the owner's production database on 20260808.

Migration v12 (`Migrations.swift`, `v12_annotation_source_key`) gives the
annotation its own source key and fills it from the previous `uuid` value
(before v12, only Calibre's raw `annot_data.uuid`/`annot_id` ever ended up
there), then reissues `uuid` itself using the deterministic formula — the same
technique, for the same reason, as v8 applied to `book` and the references.
Rows are selected by the SHAPE of the previous value: Calibre's raw key is 22
base64 characters, whereas an annotation created by Beresta carries a canonical
`UUID` string, so `UUID(uuidString:) == nil` tells them apart reliably and the
migration stays correct even after the reader starts creating highlights in
already-imported books. If the target `uuid` value is already taken by a row
that arrived over sync, the source key goes to that row and the old one is left
untouched: reconciling duplicates is sync's job, whereas a migration must above
all not make the database impossible to open.

**But "doesn't break idempotency" is not the same as "converges across
devices."** Review round 1 of task 9 found: `book`/`author`/`tag`/
`collection` rows inserted BEFORE the switch to the deterministic scheme
would have kept their old random `uuid` FOREVER — nothing touches them on
the fly during import (see above), and re-importing an already-existing
row never changes its `uuid`. Two devices that independently imported the
same library BEFORE and AFTER the code update would diverge: the
intersection of the owner's production database's `book.uuid` set (all of
its books were imported BEFORE task 9) with the `uuid` set from a fresh
import of the same library was 0 out of 1555 books. At sync time this
isn't duplicates — the unique index `book_on_source` on
(`sourceLibraryUUID`, `sourceBookUUID`) rejects the second device's insert
with a different `uuid` for the same source book.

So migration v8 (`Migrations.swift`, `v8_deterministic_import_identifiers`)
moves ALREADY-EXISTING rows onto the deterministic scheme once, when the
database is opened: for `book`, only rows with a known source
(`sourceLibraryUUID`/`sourceBookUUID` both non-NULL — the formula isn't
computable without a source), for `author`/`tag`/`collection`, every row
(by name, with no source instance — the same formula a new row would get).
Each row gets its own unique new value (`DeterministicUUID` is injective by
construction), so the migration is a plain per-row `UPDATE`, no table
rebuild needed. Conditions are favorable for it right now: as of task 9,
the change log (`changeLog`) isn't populated by any consumer yet — moving
an identifier leaves no dangling reference to the old value in any table.

#### A reference's deterministic identifier and renaming

**Accepted limitation (task 17).** The `uuid` of an author, tag, or
collection is derived from its name ONLY at the moment the row is created,
and is NEVER recomputed afterwards. Renaming a reference changes `name`
(and `modifiedAt`, on `collection`, which has that column) and leaves
`uuid` as it was. Any third-party tool writing to this database has to know
the rule: recomputing `uuid` from the new name "so the formula still holds"
means breaking the links.

Why. Name-derived determinism solves convergence: two devices that
independently meet a shelf named "Summer reading" must arrive at the same
identifier, or sync sees two different shelves. But it has a flip side —
renaming:

- **recompute** and EVERY rename breaks: device A renames the shelf, device
  B doesn't know about the rename yet, and one and the same shelf splits
  into two identifiers. A wide seam, opened by an entirely ordinary action;
- **don't recompute** (what is accepted here) and a NARROW seam remains:
  device A renames "Summer reading" to "Autumn", device B independently
  creates a NEW shelf called "Summer reading" — both rows carry the same
  `uuid`, and merging will silently fuse two different shelves into one.

The narrow seam is the chosen one. What decides it isn't arithmetic but
what each failure costs: visible duplicates the owner fixes by hand in a
minute, whereas a silent fusion of two shelves goes unnoticed. Renaming a
shelf is also an everyday action, while two devices independently choosing
the same name within the same window is rare.

The same limitation dictates a rule for the CODE: the initializers of
`Author`, `Tag`, and `Collection` have no default value for `uuid` (task 17
removed `= UUID().uuidString`). The default would have silently handed a
random identifier to every new caller — a future "create shelf" screen, for
one — and such a shelf would never converge with the identically named one
on another device. Now every call has to name the source of the identifier
explicitly.

#### `setting.scope`, `setting.scopeId`, and `setting.deviceId`

**`setting.uuid` is deterministic too**, using the same tool
(`DeterministicUUID.make(kind:instance:key:)`) as `book.uuid`/
`annotation.uuid`/reference `uuid`s above: `kind` is always `"setting"`,
`instance` is `scope.rawValue` (`"app"` or `"book"`), `key` is `scopeId`/
`key`/`deviceId` joined via `LengthPrefixedJoin` (not a plain separator —
the same non-injectivity reason as for `book.uuid` above: `scopeId` is an
arbitrary address string, not only `book.uuid`, and a separator character
inside it isn't ruled out). Without determinism, two devices would each
create their own row for the same setting address, and merge resolution
(per row field, not per unique key) would never reconcile them.

**`deviceId` cannot be NULL.** SQLite treats every NULL in a unique index as
DISTINCT from every other NULL, so `UNIQUE(scope, scopeId, key, deviceId)`
with NULL standing for "shared setting" would not stop an unlimited number
of shared rows from being created for the same key — the same defense
already applied to `author`/`tag`/`collection.uuid` in v6 (see its comment
in `Migrations.swift`). The placeholder is an empty string: it is NOT NULL
and participates in the unique index as an ordinary value, so a second
shared setting on the same address is rejected by the database itself, not
by relying on repository code (see
`SettingRepositoryTests.sharedSettingUniquenessEnforcedByDatabase`).

**Reading** (`SettingRepository.value(forKey:scope:scopeId:)`) looks at this
device's own setting first (this device's `deviceId`), then the shared one
(`deviceId == ''`) — an own setting whose value was cleared (`value ==
NULL`) does not mask the shared one; the read falls through to it exactly
as if there were no own setting for this device at all.

**Clearing a setting** (`value(forKey:...) = nil` via
`SettingRepository.set`) does not delete the row and does not leave a
"ghost" row with the old value next to it: it's the same row (the address
determines `uuid`), its value is `NULL`. The value does not resurrect on
the next read.

#### `book.readStatus`

One of six values: `unread` (never opened), `inbox` (arrived from a transfer,
not yet looked at), `reading` (being read right now), `paused` (started and
set aside), `stopped` (started and given up), `read` (finished). The default
for a new book is `unread`. This is a closed dictionary
and the database enforces it (see "What the database itself enforces"): writing
a foreign value is rejected. Reading code must nevertheless still treat an
unknown value as a data error rather than as an additional status — the check
does not cover rows that were in the database before migration v19, and it can
be removed with `DROP TRIGGER`.

**There were three values; migration v24 made it five, and migration v41 made
it six.** Before it, a book set
aside, a book honestly started and given up, and a book never opened at all all
stored THE SAME value `unread`: the schema remembered reading and forgot
attempts. A file migrated up to v23 contains no `paused` or `stopped` values
and does not accept them.

**The status is derived from the reading history (`reading`), as of migration
v15.** One formula, five rules in order, and the order is the whole point: a
book may have an open pass, a finished one and an abandoned one all at once,
and yet it has a single status.

1. There is an open pass (`reading.status = 'reading'`) — the book is being read
   right now (`reading`). An open pass outweighs any other: a re-read is
   `reading`, not `read`, because the status answers "what is my relationship
   with this book" TODAY. The schema has no separate value for re-reading (such
   as AniList's `REPEATING`): the fact of a re-read is visible in the history
   itself.
2. Otherwise, if there is a paused pass (`reading.status = 'paused'`), the book
   is `paused`. A paused pass outweighs a finished one by the same "today"
   rule: you finished it once, started again and set it aside — today it is set
   aside, not read. `book.finishedAt` is NULL here: the date belongs to the
   finished pass, not to the current relationship, and next to `paused` it
   would say "set aside, finished on such a date" — two answers to one
   question. The date is not lost: it stays on its own pass in
   `reading.finishedAt`.
3. Otherwise, if at least one pass is finished, the book is `read`, and
   `book.finishedAt` equals the LATEST known finish date among its readings (not
   the date of the highest-numbered pass: a pass may have no date at all, and
   taking it from the last one would trade a known date for an unknown one). If
   no pass carries a date, `finishedAt` is NULL.
4. Otherwise, if there is an abandoned pass (`reading.status = 'abandoned'`),
   the book is `stopped`, and `book.finishedAt` is NULL: "how far did I get" is
   answered by the reading position, not by a finish date.
5. Otherwise — `unread`. This rule now has a single way in: the book has no
   live readings at all.

**`inbox` is the only value the formula does not produce (migration v41).**
"Inbox" is an intention to look at the record, not a reading experience; it
cannot be derived from the reading history. Its rule is rule zero, BEFORE the
five rules of the formula: a stored `inbox` is legal only when the formula
yields rule 5 (no live readings) and is then preserved; any live pass wins —
for good, because a cleared history brings the book back to `unread`, not
`inbox`: "inbox" is written by the source of the record (the article
transfer), and there is nothing to write it a second time. A file migrated up
to v40 contains no `inbox` values and does not accept them. The staleness
guard knows rule zero: an inbox book with no history is not a discrepancy; an
inbox book with a live pass is.

**What v24 did to data already in the file.** A book that stored `unread` —
that is, exactly what the old formula would have given it — with a live
abandoned pass and no open, paused or finished one became `stopped`. A row with
any OTHER stored value was left alone even where the new formula disagrees with
it: that value was not written by our formula, and repairing someone else's
data is not a migration's business. On the owner's database the backfill
changed the status of none of the 1555 books: it had no abandoned passes.

**Why the column stays.** The status is read on every cell of the library grid
(1555 books for the owner) — where a per-row subquery against `reading` would
cost more than storing a ready value.

**What keeps the status and the history from drifting apart.** The
`readStatus`/`finishedAt` pair has no write path of its own: a value passed by
the caller into the book repository is IGNORED on save and replaced by the value
derived from the history — the same technique that already protects
`book.searchText`. Any edit to the history recomputes the status IN THE SAME
transaction, so the status cannot fall behind the history even if something
fails midway. The schema is open and a third-party tool may write the column
directly — for that case there is a watchdog
(`ReadingRepository.booksWithStaleStatus`) that compares the stored value with
THE SAME formula across the whole database.

Only Beresta sets `reading`, `paused` and `stopped`: Calibre has a "read" flag
and nothing else, it knows no intermediate states and can neither confirm nor
deny them. In history terms this is the same rule as "the import only governs
its own reading" — see "`reading`". A source flag does, however, close an
UNFINISHED pass — open or paused alike: to a reader that is one and the same
fact "I finished it", recorded elsewhere.

#### The golden shelf: reason, provenance, and the seat limit

Migration v30 added four columns for a single mechanic — the "golden shelf"
(Igor Mann, *Chitay!*, chapter 38): a small shelf of personally important
books where every book has ITS OWN reason for being there. The schema stores
only the shape of that mechanic; the shelf screen, showing it to others, and
picking candidates are the application's business.

**The reason lives on the link (`bookCollection.reason`), not on the book.**
There are two shelves by design — "I reread" (for myself) and "I recommend"
(for others) — and one book legitimately stands on both for DIFFERENT reasons.
A column on `book` would hold one answer to two questions: the second shelf
would overwrite the first shelf's reason, and both would show the last write.
The same follows for how long a reason lives: exactly as long as the PAIR does.
Take the book off the shelf and the reason is gone (`removeCollection` deletes
the link row physically); delete the book itself and the link is tombstoned, so
the reason comes back with the book (`addCollection` revives the same row
without rewriting `reason`/`reasonNote`).

**Provenance lives on the book (`book.provenance`), not on the link.** "Signed
by the author" is a property of the COPY: it holds while the book stands on two
shelves and while it stands on none. On the link there would be as many
autographs as there are shelves.

**The reason may be NULL, and that is not an oversight.** Requiring a reason is
a rule of the shelf screen, not of the file: shelves exist long before the
mechanic does, and some links will stay reasonless forever. A third-party
writer may add a link with no reason and may erase a reason back to NULL — the
database will not object (see caveat 1 under "What the database itself
enforces").

**Own words sit beside the dictionary, not instead of it.** `reasonNote`
answers "which occasion exactly" where `reason` answers "of what kind". It is
shaped exactly like the `readerDecision.reason`/`note` pair, and for the same
reason: three dictionary values do not hold "my teacher's book".

**There is NO seat limit in the schema, and that is a decision.** Mann's shelf
holds 28 books — a property of his shelf, not of the format. The limit is kept
as a `setting` row: `scope = 'app'`, `scopeId = ''`, key
`shelf.<collection.uuid>.limit`, the value an integer written as text; with no
row present the default is 28. It is addressed by the shelf's `uuid`, not by
its name: names get renamed (see "A reference's deterministic identifier and
renaming"), `uuid`s do not. The row is shared (`deviceId = ''`), so it travels
to other devices.

**Eviction is a decision, not a side effect.** When a twenty-ninth book takes
the seat of the twenty-eighth, the departure is recorded in `readerDecision`
with kind `shelfSwap` (introduced by migration v25 for exactly this case). The
schema knows no list of reasons for that kind — as with every other kind, the
list belongs to the mechanic, not to the file.

**The collection's kind (`collection.kind`) is a column with a single value.**
Today every shelf is a shelf of books, and the default `books` is true of every
existing row. The column exists so that the question "can a shelf hold
something other than books?" has a place to be answered in the format: after
the 1.0 freeze a column can still be added, but third-party readers already
written cannot be re-taught.

**A known limitation worth knowing in advance.** A book's set of shelves is
reset wholesale by the Calibre import: `CalibreImporter` collects the list from
the source's custom column and hands it to `setCollections`, which takes the
book off every shelf missing from that list. A book unchanged in Calibre since
the last import is not touched at all (its `sourceModifiedAt` is compared), so
this does not happen on every run — but a shelf created in the application
rather than in Calibre disappears at the first import that finds the book
changed, and the reason disappears with the link. The behaviour predates the
golden shelf, yet the reason raised its price: what used to be lost was
membership, and what is lost now is the owner's words. The shelf screen has to
deal with this first.

### What travels to other devices

Not everything stored in the database means anything outside this machine. The
decision is made when a table is created, not when synchronization is written —
which is why it lives in an explicit closed list, `SyncedDatasets.all`
(`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Database/SyncedDatasets.swift`).

**Travels** (`synced`): `book`, `bookFile`, `author`, `tag`, `collection`,
`bookAuthor`, `bookTag`, `bookCollection`, `annotation`, `annotationTag`,
`annotationLayer`, `bookmark`, `reading`, `readerDecision`, `setting` (row by
row, see
below).

**Stays here** (`localOnly`): `changeLog` and `changeLogClock` (the journal
itself and merge state), `readingPosition`, `device`, `bookSearch` and its four
shadow tables (`bookSearch_config`, `bookSearch_data`, `bookSearch_docsize`,
`bookSearch_idx`), `annotationSearch` and its four (`annotationSearch_config`,
`annotationSearch_data`, `annotationSearch_docsize`, `annotationSearch_idx`),
`grdb_migrations`.

Both full-text indexes stay here for the same reason: they are derived. Not one
of their rows holds anything absent from `book` and `annotation`, and on another
device they rebuild themselves from the same books and excerpts.

**Why the reading position does not travel through the journal.** A journal row
weighs about 390 bytes with the live index set; two fields per page turn is 780
bytes, an evening of reading about 230 KB, a year about 50 MB on EVERY device
and on the server — for data whose useful lifetime is a single row. The
position will still have to move between devices, but through its own mechanism
with clock-based resolution, not as a stream of changes in the shared journal.

**`setting` travels row by row.** A shared setting (`deviceId = ''`) travels;
a setting of THIS device (`deviceId <> ''` — font size on this screen, theme
chosen here) does not: travelling, it would overwrite the same setting on
someone else's screen. The schema already has the mechanism for this (the
`setting.deviceId` column), so the registry stores a predicate
(`deviceId = ''`) for such a table rather than a whole-table flag.

**A limitation now lifted: `bookFile` has an address of its own.** Before
migration v33 there was nothing to put in `changeLog.rowUUID` for a book file —
the row had no `uuid` — and the registry recorded that state
(`rowIdentity = .unaddressable`). v33 added the column and addressed every row
that already existed; the registry now says `rowIdentity = .uuid`.

It was added BEFORE its first consumer — against the project's usual rule —
for one reason: **here, delay got more expensive, not cheaper.** The column
could be added after 1.0, but there would be nowhere to take the values for
rows that already exist: two devices, each with its own library, would assign
one and the same file two different values. Today those values are derived
from what the row already holds (see "`uuid` — the row's address and how to
compute it").

No travelling dataset is left unaddressable.

**Reference columns hold a local number, not an identifier.**
`annotation.bookId`, `annotation.readingId`, `annotation.layerId`,
`reading.bookId`, and both columns of every link table are autoincrements valid
only on this machine. Such a
column cannot go into the journal as is: on another device the same number
points at a different row — the journal entry must carry the target's `uuid`.
Every such column is listed in the registry, and a test checks the list against
the live schema's foreign keys in both directions: a new reference column
breaks the build instead of silently travelling as a number.

**How the registry is protected from forgetfulness.** By the same technique as
the garbage-collection roots (see below): the test
`SyncedDatasetsTests.everyTableIsClassified` reads the actually migrated schema
(`sqlite_master`) and fails in both directions — a table exists in the schema
with no decision recorded, or the registry holds an entry for a table that no
longer exists. Two neighbouring tests check row addressing and the reference
column list against the live schema.

**The journal guard — and what it cannot do.** The invariant "the origin of a
change is known only if EVERY change goes through the journal" is checked
neither by the compiler nor by the database. It is checked by `JournalGuard`, a
transaction observer in the test target: it sees changes to policed tables
(including those made by raw SQL bypassing the models, and cascading deletes
through foreign keys) and requires that `changeLog` contain, in THE SAME
transaction, an entry with the same dataset + row pair. The limits, stated
honestly: **the guard only sees our own process** — GRDB explicitly documents
that changes from external connections and other processes are not delivered to
observers. The database is an open format, and any third-party program may
write into the file bypassing everything; the guard is about the discipline of
our code, not the integrity of the file. The second limit: a deleted row can no
longer be read after the commit, so for deletions the guard compares COUNTS
(how many rows disappeared against how many journal entries for that dataset
were not spent on inserts and updates), not identifiers.

**State of affairs as of 20260824.** All nine write paths go through the
journal: books (creation, edit, date repair, deletion, edition merge), book
files, reference tables (author, tag, shelf), links, annotations, bookmarks,
readings, reader decisions, annotation layers and shared settings. The Calibre
import names itself with the origin `import:calibre:<library>` instead of
posing as a human edit: conflict resolution rests on `origin`, and fifteen
hundred books recorded as manual edits would override the real ones on another
device.

**One rule for every path** (`ChangeLog.recordRow`): values are read FROM THE
DATABASE after the write (otherwise there would be two date formats); what
never travels is the row number (`id`), its address (`uuid` — present in every
journal entry), foreign-key columns holding local numbers, and derived values;
a row of a synced table may itself be unsynced — for `setting` that is decided
by the registry predicate.

**Derived columns do not travel** — and that is not about saving bytes. Were
`annotation.colorHex` (the colour's value) sent alongside `color` (the colour's
name), merge resolution could take the name from one device and the value from
another. The pair holds precisely because the derived value is not stored in
the journal independently: the receiver recomputes it with the same formula.
Declared derived today: `annotation.colorHex`, `annotation.searchText`,
`book.searchText`, `book.readStatus`, `book.finishedAt`.

**What is still missing:** moving a book file or a link during an edition merge
is not expressible in the journal — a known debt (`docs/backlog.md`), to be
closed together with synchronisation. Reading position is deliberately not
journalled (see above) and will travel through its own channel.

**The initial snapshot.** A library created BEFORE the journal started being
written (and the first user's library is exactly that — the import came before
the second device) would travel empty: the journal only knows edits. Hence a
one-off sweep — a snapshot that journals every existing row the way a write path
would. It is idempotent per row: anything the journal already knows is skipped,
so it can be run at every enabling of synchronisation. Measured on the live
library (20260824): 9085 rows, 66,206 journal entries, 2.8 seconds, database
file grown to 33 MB.

**Cost measurement** (20260824, the owner's live library): importing 1555 books
without files produces 40,100 journal entries and a 22 MB database file in 6.3
seconds. The registry and the guard are still needed: they make journal bypass
visible instead of relying on the memory of whoever writes the next path.

### What soft deletion means

Soft deletion answers the question "is this row in the library" and no other.
It says nothing about where the row's content went — that is a different fact
and a different column (`book.mergedIntoUUID`, see "The merge tombstone").

The `deletedAt` field exists on `annotation`, `book`, `bookFile`,
`collection`, `bookCollection` (since task 13), `reading` (since migration
v15), `author` (since v23), `readerDecision` (since v25), `annotationLayer`
(since v28), and `bookmark` (since v31). Soft deletion is functionally
implemented for `annotation`, `reading`, `readerDecision`, `annotationLayer`,
`bookmark`, and — as of task 18 — for `book`, `bookFile`, and `bookCollection`.
Softly deleting a book does NOT touch its bookmarks, deliberately: bookmarks are
visible only inside their own book, so while the book is hidden they are hidden
too, and when it comes back its ribbons come back with it. The same reasoning
leaves a deleted book's excerpts alone (their snapshot is refreshed instead),
and the opposite reasoning frees the shelves: a shelf is a shared list, and a
removed book must leave it. For a layer of marks soft deletion has
a consequence the others lack: dropping a layer RETURNS its marks to the main
one (`annotation.layerId` is nulled in the same transaction). The foreign key
does not fire here — it is about physical deletion — so the code does the
nulling itself; see `annotationLayer`. While `deletedAt` is empty (`NULL`), the row is active. When
the user deletes an annotation, the row is **not physically removed**:
`deletedAt` is set (and `modifiedAt` is updated), and read queries
(`AnnotationRepository.fetch`, `count`) filter such rows out with
`deletedAt IS NULL`. A reading behaves the same way: history reads filter on
`deletedAt IS NULL`, and deletion itself (clearing a book's history, or a read
flag cleared in the source) sets the date without removing the row.

`book`, `bookFile`, and `collection` got their `deletedAt` column ahead of
time in migration v6 — for the same reason `annotation` got one back in v3,
and the same reason `author`, `collection`, and `tag` got a `uuid` in that
same v6: cross-device sync (subproject 3) cannot address a row without a
stable identifier, and cannot deliver the fact of a deletion without soft
deletion. Deferring these columns until sync itself lands would mean either
running the same migration later against tables that will only have grown in
the meantime (the exact risk the table rebuild solves for `uuid`), or losing
whatever deletions happened before the column existed. `collection` still has
no soft-delete method of its own — there is nothing to delete shelves with
yet; `book` and `bookFile` got theirs in task 18 (see below).

**Deleting a book (task 18).** `BookRepository.deleteBook(uuid:)` does all of
it in ONE transaction: it flags the book itself, flags its files, flags its
links to shelves, and PHYSICALLY erases its reading positions (a flagged
position would keep holding the bytes of a deleted book's file, and a
position is local — its tombstone has nowhere to travel). The annotations of
the book are deliberately NOT flagged: they lose `bookId` (the foreign key is
`ON DELETE SET NULL`, and the delete path nulls it explicitly), keep the book
snapshot on their own row (`bookUUID`, `bookTitle`, `bookAuthors`,
`bookCoverSHA256`, refreshed at the moment of deletion), and go on living as
orphans — see "An annotation outlives its book". A softly deleted book is not
resurrected by the next import either: the importer skips it and counts it in
`CalibreImportReport.booksSkippedAsDeleted`.

**`bookCollection.deletedAt` — the same head start, with one difference.**
The column was added by migration v11 for the same reason (ahead of time, for
future sync). The ORDER-READING queries (`BookRepository.books(inCollection:)`,
`moveBook`, `collections(of:)`) filter on `deletedAt IS NULL` — that's part of
the open-schema read contract (see "`bookCollection.sortKey` and
`collection.defaultSort`"), not a separately decided soft-deletion behavior.
As of task 18 its only writer is `deleteBook`; `removeCollection` still deletes
the link physically. `addCollection`/`setCollections` REVIVE a flagged link
with a fresh order key rather than inserting a second one — otherwise adding a
book back to a shelf would be silent inaction, because the uniqueness
constraint already holds the flagged row (finding B13).

The reason is cross-device synchronization (subproject 3, not yet
implemented). A physical `DELETE` leaves nothing in the database that could
be shipped to other devices as "this annotation should be deleted there too"
— the fact of deletion would simply vanish along with the row. A non-null
`deletedAt` is exactly such a fact: it survives until sync time, gets
propagated to other devices, and only then can the row be truly erased by a
garbage collector (out of scope for subproject 1a).

This differs from the cascading `ON DELETE CASCADE` on foreign keys — but not
everything hanging off a book is wired the same way, and the difference
matters. If the book itself is physically deleted
(`DELETE FROM book WHERE id = …`), its files (`bookFile`), tag and collection
links, reading positions, and readings (`reading`) are genuinely removed: those
cascade. **Its annotations are not.** Since migration v18 `annotation.bookId`
is `ON DELETE SET NULL`, so highlights stay in the database and lose only the
link. Cascading is a referential-integrity mechanism, not a sync mechanism,
and it never applies to a user deleting a single annotation. Why annotations
were taken out from under the cascade while everything else stays is the next
section.

### An annotation outlives its book

Since migration v18, deleting a book does not take its annotations with it.
`annotation.bookId` lost its NOT NULL constraint and its foreign-key action
changed from `ON DELETE CASCADE` to `ON DELETE SET NULL`; four snapshot
columns appeared on the annotation row: `bookUUID`, `bookTitle`,
`bookAuthors`, `bookCoverSHA256`.

**Why.** A book is someone else's file that can be downloaded again; the
highlights are a reader's own work accumulated over years. Losing the second
along with the first is not acceptable. This is not only about someone hitting
"delete" by accident: soft deletion of books (once it lands) has a sequel — a
garbage collector that will eventually erase flagged rows physically — and the
cascade would then fire across the whole library at once.

**Why this is enforced by the file's shape, not by the application's
behavior.** The schema is declared an open format, and this document actively
invites third-party writers. `PRAGMA foreign_keys` is **off** by default in
SQLite: another program (or `sqlite3` from a terminal) will delete a book with
a plain `DELETE FROM book`, and nothing inside Beresta will hear about it. A
promise that rests only on Beresta's own delete method would be a promise for
Beresta alone. A foreign-key action holds no matter who is holding the pen.

**An orphan** is an annotation with an empty `bookId`. It is a legitimate
state, not data corruption. Read it like this:

- `bookUUID` is the **address**: the `book.uuid` of the book the annotation
  belonged to. An orphan is reunited with its book through it if the book comes
  back under the same identifier (Beresta does this when inserting a book).
  `bookId` cannot play that role: a local autoincrement is only valid inside
  this database file, and it never travels in the sync journal;
- `bookTitle`, `bookAuthors`, `bookCoverSHA256` are the **caption** by which a
  human recognizes what the quote referred to. This is a **snapshot as of the
  annotation's last write, not a mirror of the book**: while the book is alive,
  display its live fields (`book.title` and the rest); the snapshot matters
  exactly when the book is gone. Beresta refreshes the snapshot on every write
  to the annotation;
- `readingId` becomes empty on an orphan: the reading pass (`reading`) itself
  went away with the book, by cascade. A reading is a fact about a book; a
  highlight is a fact in its own right.

**A naive reader stays correct.** Third-party code that reads annotations via
`JOIN book` needs no change at all: orphans simply fall out of the join —
no crash, no corruption. A reader that wants to see them too needs only a
`LEFT JOIN` and the snapshot columns; no second table and no dictionary.

**`bookUUID` permits NULL even though its meaning says it shouldn't.** That is
not sloppiness. A migration must succeed on any input file, including a
database written by a third-party program with foreign keys disabled: there
`annotation.bookId` may point at nothing, and there is nowhere to get an
address for such a row. Declaring the column `NOT NULL` would mean such a
database simply fails to open. The strictness lives in code instead: every
Beresta write path fills the address in. A third-party writer that doesn't will
produce an annotation that outlives its book but cannot find its way back —
no data is lost, a link is.

**What travels to other devices when a book is deleted.** The orphaning itself
does not: it is a local consequence of the book's deletion, and on another
device the same foreign key will produce it once the deletion arrives there.
The snapshot, by contrast, does travel with the annotation, deliberately: an
annotation of a deleted book may arrive on a device that never saw that book,
and there would be nowhere for it to derive a caption from. `bookId` still
never travels — it is a local reference.

### The merge tombstone

A `mergedIntoUUID` column exists on **book** (migration v22) and on **author**
(v23). A non-empty value means the same thing in both: **this record is not
here; all of its content now lives in the row with that `uuid`**. This is what
merging two records of one and the same thing looks like — two editions of one
book (a different file, a different import run), or two spellings of one name.

The rule below is shared by both tables; wherever book and author differ, this
is stated explicitly rather than implied.

**How this differs from `deletedAt`.** Soft deletion says "this book is not
here" and says nothing about where its annotations, files and history are now.
The difference is not cosmetic: it decides where to put a record that arrives
for the deleted book tomorrow. A deleted book expects nothing; a merged one
expects everything addressed to it to travel to the survivor.

**Both columns are set together, in a single statement.** A reader that knows
only `deletedAt` behaves correctly — it hides the row; a reader that knows
`mergedIntoUUID` also follows the link. Downward compatibility rests on
exactly that pairing, which is why writing the two columns separately is
forbidden: there would be an instant in which the row is marked merged but not
deleted, and any 1.0 reader would show both books at that moment — handing the
owner back the very duplicate they had just removed.

**What a reader must do.**

1. **Hide a row with a non-empty `mergedIntoUUID`** regardless of `deletedAt`.
   The second condition is not redundant: the database does NOT enforce the
   pairing (why — below), so a third-party writer may leave a row merged but
   not deleted, and reads have to cope with it.
2. **Follow the link TO THE END, not one step.** A chain (`A→B→C`) is a legal
   state; see below.
3. **Remember where it has been.** A cycle (`A→B`, `B→A`) is legal too, and a
   naive walk over it never terminates. Beresta does this in
   `RecordMerge.resolveSurvivor`.
4. **Not treat a dangling link as a failure.** The surviving book may arrive
   by sync later than the losing one; until it does, `mergedIntoUUID` is an
   address that is not in the database yet. There is deliberately no foreign
   key to `book.uuid` here: it would abort applying an incoming change, that
   is, wreck merging exactly where merging matters most.

**The losing row keeps its source key.** This is the main reason the rule
belongs to version 1.0. The `sourceLibraryUUID`+`sourceBookUUID` pair stays
put — otherwise re-import would not recognize its own record and would create
a third book. But the data of that source record now lives on a DIFFERENT row,
and an importer that knows only 1.0 will read the pair as "here is my book"
and restore the duplicate after every merge. The tombstone does not disturb
the partial unique index `book_on_source`: the pair is still unique.

**What the database checks and what it does not.**

The triggers `book_merged_into_on_insert`/`_on_update` (v22) and
`author_merged_into_on_insert`/`_on_update` (v23) reject two values:

- **the empty string** — the same discipline as the source key (v21): `NULL`
  means "never merged", the empty string means nothing;
- **a row pointing at itself** — an unresolvable loop for anyone walking the
  tombstones.

The following is deliberately NOT checked:

- **pairing with `deletedAt`.** `RAISE(ABORT)` rejects any write, including
  one arriving by sync, while conflict resolution works FIELD by field: a
  device that restored the book to the library and a device that merged it
  would produce a row the trigger would never accept, on any attempt. The
  schema already declined a unique index for the same reason (see "What
  travels to other devices"). The pairing is upheld by the write path and by
  the reading rule above;
- **the absence of chains and cycles.** There is nothing to forbid them with:
  two devices merging independently (`A→B` here, `B→C` there) produce a chain
  after exchanging changes, and `A→B` plus `B→A` produce a cycle, while
  neither of the two writes violates anything on its own. So chains and cycles
  must be survived by the reader, and collapsed by whoever merges: on merging,
  Beresta repoints both the losing row and every tombstone that pointed at it
  to the final survivor.

**What moves to the surviving book and what stays.** What the owner
accumulated moves: `bookFile`, `annotation`, `bookmark`, `reading`,
`readerDecision`, `readingPosition`, `bookTag`, `bookCollection`. Bookmarks
(v31) move on the same grounds as excerpts, and it costs exactly nothing: a
place in a book is given by selectors that do not depend on the book's row at
all — so neither `position` nor `sortIndex` is recomputed by the move. Reader
decisions move on the
same grounds as the reading history: a merge declares two rows to be one book,
and the owner's judgement about it — why they gave up, why it lost its place on
the Golden Shelf — belongs to the book, not to the row. Leaving them on the
tombstone would hide them forever: a tombstone appears in no read of the
library. A moved annotation gets its book snapshot
refreshed (`bookUUID`, `bookTitle`, `bookAuthors`, `bookCoverSHA256`) —
`bookUUID` is its ADDRESS, and without that write the annotation would stay
with the tombstone.

`bookAuthor` stays on the tombstone. Authorship is not the owner's work but a
description of the edition that came from the source together with the record;
the survivor is chosen by a human, usually precisely because its card is
better, and merging author lists would drag the loser's junk into it. The
links go away by cascade when the garbage collector erases the row itself.

Collisions are resolved as follows: a link that would become a duplicate is
dropped from the loser; a shared shelf keeps the earlier place (`sortKey`) and
the live state; for a shared file (the same `sha256` in the same format) the
loser's row is deleted physically — the bytes stay under the survivor's row,
and a tombstone here would claim "the owner removed this file", which never
happened.

**What is still a limitation and what has been lifted.** Merging was the first
consumer of a `bookFile` address, and it also showed that the once-intended
formula `bookId + format + sha256` will not do: a file's `bookId` changes when
books are merged. Migration v33 closed that twice over — `book.uuid` takes the
local number's place, and the value is stored rather than recomputed, so
**moving a file does not change its address** (see "`uuid` — the row's address
and how to compute it"). What remains a limitation is different: the FACT of
the move cannot be expressed in the sync journal — a journal row would have to
carry the book's new address, and `bookFile` has no column for it, only the
local `bookId`. On another device the file will therefore stay attached to the
tombstone — but no longer nameless.

#### An article as a library record

Migration v36 added two columns to a book — `kind` and `sourceURL` — and one value
to the format dictionary (`html`). That is the whole of what an article is: it has
no table of its own.

**Why a record kind rather than a second table.** An article and a book share
EVERYTHING a library exists for: title, authors, tags, collections, annotations,
reading position, status, search, and sync between devices. A separate table would
mean a second copy of each of those links — `articleTag`, `articleCollection`,
`articleAnnotation` — and a second copy of every rule built on them. The
difference between an article and a book is exactly one thing: where the record
came from and how to show it. One difference earns a column, not half a schema.

The precedent is our own and direct: `collection.kind` (v30) was introduced with
the same lever and the same argument — "should a shelf of articles appear
tomorrow, its kind is declared by a new migration widening the dictionary".

**Why the address is a column, not only a field inside the file.** An article is
assembled into an EPUB, and the address of the original goes into `dc:source` — a
legitimate Dublin Core field that travels with the file. That is not enough: a
field inside an archive cannot be searched, shown in a list, or opened without
unpacking the whole file first. A column answers all of that in one query.

The address is deliberately not reduced to a hash. The temptation is there — an
address is long — but it is the only thing by which an article is recognized
across devices and across imports, and the only thing a person may want to see and
click. A hash does not open.

**Why `html` is the format of an archival file, not a readable one.** An article
is read as the assembled EPUB: the same engine, the same path, the same
annotations. The web page snapshot stays beside it as the original — the same
relationship a PDF has beside an EPUB. Hence `html` is in the format dictionary
and is not among the formats the reader opens.

**What the schema does NOT decide.** Whether a shelf of articles exists
(`collection.kind` still has the single value `books`) and whether an article gets
a status of its own. Both questions are open and belong to the owner, not to a
migration.


#### The author: what differs here

**The name stays on the tombstone, and the tombstone is itself the record of
the alias.** `author.name` is `NOT NULL UNIQUE` — there is nothing to erase it
with, and nothing to free the name for another row either. So the row "Igor
Borisovich Mann" pointing at "Igor Mann" says exactly what a separate table of
name variants would have said, and that is why the schema has no such table: it
would be a second record of the same fact. A book's tombstone keeps its source
key for the very same reason.

**What an importer must do — and why filtering out deleted rows is NOT
enough.** Looking an author up by name finds the TOMBSTONE: the name is unique
and still there. Discarding it as deleted is not an option — the code would go
on to insert a new row with the same name, and the insert would fail on the
unique index, so the first import after a merge would abort the whole book. The
tombstone must be **resolved**: follow `mergedIntoUUID` to the end, remembering
where you have been.

The same follows for a book's author list: what must be compared are the author
ROWS the names resolved to, not the names themselves. After a merge two
different names legitimately lead to one row, and a book whose source hands
over both spellings at once (routine for Calibre) produces two inserts of one
and the same link — which the `bookAuthor` primary key will not accept.

**A dangling link weighs more for an author than for a book.** The surviving
row may arrive by sync later than the losing one. For a book the link is, until
then, merely an address that does not exist. For an author a new row under the
same name cannot be created (`UNIQUE`), so the import attaches the book to the
TOMBSTONE ITSELF, and it will move only once the survivor arrives and a human
merges the spellings again. This is a named limitation, not an oversight.

**What moves.** `bookAuthor` — the very table that, when BOOKS are merged,
stays on the tombstone instead. The difference is not accidental: when editions
are merged, authorship is a description of the edition that came from the
source and there is no reason to drag it into the survivor's card; when
spellings are merged, the link to the book is the only observable consequence
of two names being one person. Leaving it behind would amount to doing nothing.

There is exactly one collision here: a book carries BOTH spellings. The loser's
link is then dropped and the remaining one keeps the **smaller** `position` —
author order is visible to a human on the book card, and an author who stood
first under the merged spelling must stay first.

**The search text of the affected books is rebuilt by the same operation.**
`book.searchText` is derived from the title, the set of authors and the series,
and merging spellings changes exactly the set of authors while leaving the book
row alone. This is verifiable by opening the file: after a merge, the
`searchText` of every moved book carries the canonical spelling and none of the
words of the spelling that went away. `book.modifiedAt` is NOT moved: not a
single field of the book changed, and declaring a hundred books modified would
send sync a hundred edits where there is not a single one; the search text is a
derived pointer, and on another device it is rebuilt from the same links. The
`bookSearch` full-text index is updated meanwhile by the v5 triggers, exactly as
it is on any other write to a book.

**What an author does not have.** `modifiedAt` — a reference table has none at
all, and the tombstone does not move one. Sync conflict resolution for an
author row rests on the row's own fields, not on a timestamp.

**A known limitation.** Moving a `bookAuthor` link is not written to the
journal — like everything else `BookRepository` does, having no journal at all
(the debt is named below, in "What travels to other devices"). There is
something to address that row with (the computed link identifier); there is
simply nobody writing it yet. Merging spellings also does not refresh the book
signature in an annotation's snapshot (`annotation.bookAuthors`): that is a
denormalized copy refreshed by the annotation write paths and by book deletion,
and a stale spelling lives in it until the nearest of those.

### Relationship with full-text search

`bookSearch` is a virtual FTS5 table (SQLite, `unicode61` tokenizer with
`remove_diacritics=2`), declared as an "external content" table
(`content='book'`, `content_rowid='id'`): it does not keep an independent
copy of the rows itself, but indexes the values of the `book.searchText`
column, with the FTS row's `rowid` equal to `book.id`. Keeping it in sync
with `book` is not the calling code's job: migration v5 creates three
triggers (`__bookSearch_ai/_ad/_au` on `book`'s `INSERT`/`DELETE`/`UPDATE`)
that update the index automatically on every book change. If the index ever
drifts from the data (e.g. after manually editing the database bypassing the
triggers), it can be rebuilt entirely from `book` with the standard FTS5
command (`INSERT INTO bookSearch(bookSearch) VALUES('rebuild')`) — no
separate storage is needed for recovery.

Important for third-party code: `searchText` is stored **normalized**, not
as-is. Normalization (`SearchNormalizer.normalize`) means lowercasing and
folding the letter "ё"/"Ё" into "е" (example: "Ёж" → "еж"). The reason is
that the `unicode61` tokenizer lowercases Cyrillic on its own, but does
**not** fold "ё" into "е" even with `remove_diacritics=2` (verified on
SQLite 3.51 — this is a quirk of that particular tokenizer version, not a
standard guarantee). Searching `bookSearch` with the user's raw query instead
of the normalized one means words containing "ё" won't be found, even though
they are present in the index (as "е"). Therefore any third-party code
searching `bookSearch` directly (bypassing `SearchRepository`) must run the
query through the same normalization function (`lowercased()` plus
"ё"/"Ё" → "е") first — otherwise its search results will differ from
Beresta's own results on text containing that letter.

The same normalization is the first step of the title sort key
(`book.sortTitle`, see the section of that name): the "ё" folding rule is
shared between search and sorting so that search never finds a book that
sorting has filed in the wrong place.

**The second index — `annotationSearch` (migration v29) — is built the same
way.** It indexes `annotation.searchText`: the quote (`text`), the reader's own
thought (`comment`) and the topic (`topic`) as one normalized string; the FTS
row's `rowid` equals `annotation.id`; synchronization is three triggers
(`__annotationSearch_ai/_ad/_au`); rebuilding uses the same command (`INSERT
INTO annotationSearch(annotationSearch) VALUES('rebuild')`). Before v29 the text
of annotations was not searchable at all: `bookSearch` indexes spines — title,
authors, series — so finding your own excerpt by a word inside it was
impossible.

**Why one joined column is indexed instead of three raw fields.** Precisely so
that the "ё" folding rule stays a SINGLE rule. Indexing `text`, `comment` and
`topic` directly would mean repeating the normalization in SQL — inside the
trigger body — and the database would end up with two independent rules for one
letter: one in code for books, another in the schema for annotations. They would
drift apart silently, and the user-visible result would be that one and the same
query finds a book but not the excerpt taken from it. The price of the single
rule is a derived column, and the table above says so plainly.

**What this means for third-party writers.** `annotation.searchText` is built by
a formula: `text`, `comment` and `topic` (empty parts dropped) joined with a
space, then run through the same normalization used for books. Write an
annotation bypassing Beresta without touching that column and you get a row that
sits in the database but is not found by search: the index is built from the
