column, not from the quote. There are two fixes — fill the column yourself using
the formula above, or let Beresta rewrite the row (every write path rebuilds the
search text and ignores whatever value came in). The `rebuild` command does NOT
help here: it rebuilds the index from the column, not the column from the quote.

**There is no morphology, and that is a limitation, not an omission.** The
tokenizer splits text into words and knows nothing about Russian inflection;
prefix matching comes from the star that `SearchRepository` appends to every
query word. Hence the observable behaviour: "библиотек" finds "библиотеку",
while "библиотеку" does not find "библиотека" — the latter is not a prefix. If
morphology is ever needed it will arrive as a SECOND index over stems, not by
swapping this one's tokenizer: a swapped tokenizer would make already-built
indexes incompatible.

### Blob store garbage-collection roots

Book files and covers are not stored in the database — only their SHA-256
lives here (see `bookFile.sha256`, `book.coverSHA256` above), while the bytes
sit in a content-addressed directory outside subproject 1a (`BlobStore`,
`packages/beresta-core/Sources/BerestaCore/Storage/BlobStore.swift`). Over
time more consumers will address content the same way: theme packages, font
files, Zotero attachments. A garbage collector for that store can only keep a
blob alive if something in the database still points at its hash — anything
it doesn't know to check for, it is free to delete.

`BlobStore.GCRoots.all` is the explicit, closed list of `(table, column)`
pairs the collector must treat as roots. As of this writing:

| Table | Column |
|---|---|
| `bookFile` | `sha256` |
| `book` | `coverSHA256` |
| `annotation` | `fileSHA256` |
| `annotation` | `anchorFileSHA256` |
| `readingPosition` | `fileSHA256` |
| `annotation` | `bookCoverSHA256` |
| `reading` | `fileSHA256` |
| `bookmark` | `fileSHA256` |

`annotation.fileSHA256` belongs on this list for a concrete reason: an
annotation can be pinned to a specific book file version (useful when a book
has several formats with different pagination/structure), and that version
is not necessarily the `bookFile` row a collector would otherwise consider
current. Leaving it off the list — as an early draft of this very task did —
would let the collector delete a blob an annotation still points to.

`annotation.anchorFileSHA256` (migration v14) is the one root that is not a
reference to content but a memory of work done (see
"`annotation.anchorFileSHA256` and anchor refinement"). It is listed as a root
deliberately: while the mark differs from `annotation.fileSHA256` — the book was
replaced with another edition and refinement against the new file has not run
yet — the refresher must compare the old against the new, and a collection that
took the old file away first would silently rob it of that chance. The pin is
temporary: the refresher rewrites the mark to the new hash, and the next
collection takes the old file.

`readingPosition.fileSHA256` (migration v16) — a reading position belongs to a
specific book file, and while a position exists in it, collecting that file
away means robbing the reader of the place they stopped at. Unlike
`anchorFileSHA256`, this pin is **not** temporary: the position row lives as
long as the book does — which means deleting a book file must also delete that
file's positions, or the collector will never reclaim the bytes.

`annotation.bookCoverSHA256` (migration v18) — the cover inside an annotation's
book snapshot. While the book is alive the same blob is referenced by
`book.coverSHA256` and the entry looks redundant. It matters exactly when the
book is gone: nothing but the orphan points at that cover any more, and the
first collection would take the annotation's face away for good — the very
thing migration v18 exists to prevent. This pin is **not** temporary either: it
lives as long as the annotation does.

`reading.fileSHA256` (migration v26) — the file a pass was read from. This is
not a bookkeeping mark but part of the reading record: the answer to "which
edition was I reading back then" loses its meaning together with the file,
because a hash with no content is comparable to nothing. The pin is **not**
temporary, like `readingPosition.fileSHA256`: a reading row lives as long as
the book does and outlives a file replacement — history is not rewritten after
the fact. So deleting a book file must reckon with this reference too, or the
collector will never reclaim the bytes.

`bookmark.fileSHA256` (migration v31) — the file the bookmark's place was found
in. An empty value is legitimate here (the file is unknown, or gone), and such a
row is no root at all: the list holds a COLUMN, not each of its rows. The pin is
**not temporary**, like `readingPosition.fileSHA256`: it lives as long as the
bookmark does. The bookmark is held by W3C selectors and
survives a file replacement, but while the file is there it IS the edition the
place was found in, and a sweep that took it away would remove any chance to
check the bookmark against the text it was placed against.

**Not every table added after this list joins it — and that is a decision in
both directions.** Neither `readerDecision` (v25), nor `annotationLayer` (v28),
nor `annotationSearch` with its shadow tables (v29) stores a reference to
content: a reader's decision has no file and no image, a layer of marks is a
name and nothing else, and a full-text index holds only words taken from the
very same rows of this database. `bookmark` (v31), on the other hand, does join
it — and would have joined it even if the migration's author had not thought of
it. None of this has to be checked by eye — a
hash column is recognizable by its name (`...SHA256`, see below), and the test
catches a forgotten registration on its own.

**The list is a convention and guarantees nothing by itself** — nothing
stops a future migration from adding a new hash-reference column without
updating it. What makes forgetting to update it costly rather than silent is
a naming convention plus a test that enforces it against the live schema,
not against this document: any column whose name ends in `sha256`
(case-insensitively — `sha256`, `coverSHA256`, `fileSHA256` all qualify,
`BlobStore.GCRoots.matchesNamingConvention`) is, by convention, a reference
into the blob store. `BlobStoreTests.everyHashLikeSchemaColumnIsRegisteredAsGCRoot`
reads the actual migrated schema (`sqlite_master` + `PRAGMA table_info` via
GRDB's `db.columns(in:)`) and fails `make test` in both directions: a
matching column missing from `GCRoots.all` (a forgotten consumer — the
scenario this section opened with, a theme's font column added without
registration), or an entry in `GCRoots.all` whose column no longer exists (a
stale root). A future theme-package or font table is expected to follow the
same `...SHA256` naming convention for its content-hash column precisely so
this test catches it if the author forgets to register it.


---

### Export format version

**This is the only section of the document that speaks about something other
than the database.** Everything else here is verified by opening the file;
this cannot be, because the file itself does not exist yet. That is stated
plainly instead of passing a promise off as a property of the format.

**Why it exists already.** The Beresta archive (a human-readable folder
layout, a copy of the database, and a manifest) is meant to be a backup and an
export at once, and scripts will be written on top of it within the first
month. A silent change of markup is not a hypothesis but a ready precedent
from the Calibre world: "you seem to have changed the HTML markup of the
annotations saved in the calibre database… I run a script to extract my
Annotations" (MobileRead forum, post 3971136, 20200401). There is no way to
declare a version retroactively: archives already scattered across other
people's disks would carry no mark to tell them apart. So the promise of a
version lands in 1.0 ahead of the archive itself.

**The manifest** — a `manifest.json` file at the root of the archive:

```json
{
  "format": "beresta-archive",
  "formatVersion": 1,
  "schemaVersion": "v33_book_file_identity",
  "schemaMigrations": ["v1_books", "v2_files_tags_collections", "…"],
  "application": "Beresta",
  "applicationVersion": "1.0",
  "generatedAt": "2026-08-11T12:00:00Z"
}
```

The current export format version is `formatVersion` = 1.

**What is promised.**

- `format` and `formatVersion` are the manifest's header, and it is promised
  unchanged across ALL future versions. Without it a reader could not even
  learn what exactly it fails to understand.
- `formatVersion` is an integer and grows ONLY when a reader of the previous
  version cannot read the archive correctly. Adding files and fields that do
  not break the earlier reader does not move it.
- `schemaVersion` and `schemaMigrations` are what lies in `grdb_migrations` of
  the database shipped inside the archive, not what the build that produced
  the archive happens to know how to apply. **This is the only statement in
  this section that can be verified by opening a database:** open the copy
  from the archive and compare
  `SELECT identifier FROM grdb_migrations ORDER BY rowid` with the list in the
  manifest — they must match line for line.
- `generatedAt` is the same ISO 8601 string as every date column in the
  database.

**What is NOT promised.** The archive's layout: folder names, book file names,
the format of the annotations exported alongside the database. All of that
will be described by the version of this document in which the archive
appears; today there is nothing to describe, and inventing a layout in advance
would mean passing off as a contract something that has never once been
assembled. A reader that meets a `formatVersion` higher than the one it knows
must stop and say so rather than parse the archive by guesswork.
